## Executive Summary

The Guam State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) serves as the island's strategy to conserve its native biodiversity and sustain healthy ecosystems for present and future generations. The primary goal of the SWAP is to promote the recovery and sustainable use of Guam's priority native species by addressing conservation needs across terrestrial, marine, and freshwater environments. As required for continued funding through the State Wildlife Grant Program (SWGP), the revised 2025 Guam SWAP outlines priority actions for species of greatest conservation need (SGCN) and their habitats.
The revision process reflects a collaborative effort led by the Department of Agriculture (DOAG) Division of Aquatic and Wildlife Resources (DAWR), hereon referred to as the Department, and the University of Guam Center for Island Sustainability and Sea Grant (UOG CIS and SG). Local agencies, researchers, traditional knowledge holders, and conservationists were consulted through meetings and outreach events. Gaining public input and working with partners in the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands (CNMI) were also key practices throughout this process. These efforts shaped the identification of SGCN, threats, and conservation actions and priorities. As a result, the plan reflects community values, ecological and economical importance, and cultural relevance.
The 2025 revision builds upon the 2015 SWAP through incorporating community input and scientific data. While most information and structure were retained from the previous plan, key updates include:
• Revised criteria for species inclusion into the SWAP • Expanded SGCN list to include culturally significant species • Updated species information and conservation actions • Integration of landscape-scale conservation across the Mariana Islands
The revised Guam SWAP identifies a diverse range of SGCN across terrestrial, marine, and freshwater ecosystems. Species selection was based on conservation status, rarity, threats, ecological function, cultural or economic significance, and local and regional conservation priorities. Each species has a species account (Chapter 3) which reflects scientific data and expert opinion. These accounts describe the species' historic and current status, habitats, threats, and recommended conservation actions.
Guam's SWAP identified 128 species including:
• 60 terrestrial species (mammals-2, birds-14, reptiles-5, arthropods-1, snails-3, insects-2, and plants-33), • 12 freshwater species (fish-4, eel-1, crustaceans-3, snails-2, and plants-2), and • 56 marine species (marine mammals-14, fishes-14, skates & rays-3, echinoderms-6, corals-3, arthropods-1, gastropods-1, clams-4, reptiles-2, marine plants-8).
Species of Greatest Conservation Need are found across Guam's varied habitats ranging from pristine limestones forests to wetlands, freshwater streams, savannas, and coral reefs. The health of these ecosystems is increasingly vulnerable to threats such as:
• Invasive species, particularly the brown tree snake (BTS), invasive plants, and ungulates • Sedimentation and pollution affecting freshwater and marine systems • Habitat loss and fragmentation caused by development and improper land use • Lack of legal regulation and enforcement • Climate change effects, including ocean acidification and increased frequency and severity of weather events
These concerns emphasize the need to link species recovery efforts to the protection and restoration of key ecosystem types. The 2025 SWAP outlines conservation strategies in major categories:
• Conservation planning -Integrate species and habitat recovery into land-use planning • Habitat management and restoration -Control invasive species, restore degraded lands, and support the protection of natural resources • Monitoring and research -Fill data gaps and track conservation outcomes • Education and outreach -Promote stewardship and increase public awareness • Collaborative partnerships -Strengthen coordination among agencies, community groups, and regional partners across the Mariana Islands
The strength of Guam's SWAP is the Department's ability to secure funding from the U.S. Congress through the State Wildlife Grant Program (SWGP) to carry out its plans. Acceptance of SWGP funding was contingent on the development of the initial SWAP (formerly the Guam Comprehensive Wildlife Conservation Strategy), and completion of the revised 2025 SWAP will qualify the Department for continued funding under this program. The success of the plan will rely on continued collaboration and adaptive management. Implementation will be led by DAWR with aligned efforts from other local and federal agencies, academic institutions, and regional conservation networks. The Department will prioritize applying the SWAP's framework for monitoring the progress of conservation actions, evaluating effectiveness of these goals, and adjusting approaches as new information and challenges arise. Protecting Guam's unique biodiversity is a responsibility of all who call the island home. The 2025 Guam SWAP offers a roadmap for coordinated, science-based, and community-driven conservation. It invites all residents, agencies, and stakeholders to join in safeguarding Guam's natural heritage for the benefit of future generations and the resilience of the island's ecosystems.

## Road Map to the Eight Required Elements

This section is provided to assist the Regional Review Team (RRT) in evaluating this document for the purpose of determining if and/or how well Guam's SWAP meets the eight required elements. Table 
1
 where each element was addressed in the 2025 Guam SWAP.
Element 1. Information on the distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as the State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, is indicative of the diversity and health of the State's wildlife.
Element 2 . Descriptions of locations and relative condition of key habitats and community types are essential to conservation of species identified.
Element 3. Descriptions of the problems that may adversely affect species identified in (1) or their habitats, and priority research and survey efforts needed to identify factors that may assist in restoration and improve conservation of these species and habitats.
Element 4. Descriptions of conservation actions determined to be necessary to conserve the identified species and habitats and priorities for implementing such actions.
Element 5. Proposed strategies for monitoring species identified in (1) and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of the conservation actions proposed in (4), and for adapting these conservation actions to respond appropriately to new information or changing conditions.
Element 6. Descriptions of procedures to review the Guam State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) at intervals not to exceed 10 years.
Element 7. Plans for coordinating, to the extent feasible, the development, implementation, review, and revision of the Guam State Wildlife Action Plan with federal, state, and local agencies and Indian tribes that manage significant land and water areas within the state or administer programs that significantly affect the conservation of identified species and habitats.
Element 8. Provisions to ensure public participation in the development, revisions, and implementation of projects and programs. Congress has affirmed that broad public participation is an essential element of this process.

: Locations of the 8 Required Elements

## Required Element

Geographic and Climatic Setting Guam, a US territory located at 13°28' N, 144°45' E, is the southernmost island in the Mariana Archipelago and the largest island in Micronesia, with a land area of 560 km2. The northern portion of the island is a limestone plateau, rising nearly 200 meters above sea level in some places, and holds the northern Guam lens aquifer 
(Lander 1997)
. The southern half of the island is oldweathered volcanic material with a cap of limestone most prominent on the Mt. Lamlam-Alifan ridge 
(Fosberg 1960
) and holds the island's highest point at Mt. Lamlam with an elevation of 406 meters. This region is predominantly covered by grasslands and ravine forests, with wetlands also comprising an important habitat type 
(Fosberg 1960)
.
Guam has a tropical climate with average daily temperatures ranging from 24-30°C (75-86°F).
The average annual rainfall is 84-116 inches (2133-2946 mm) (USGS 2015). The climate is marked by two distinct seasons: a wet season from June to November and a dry season from January to April. December and May serve as transitional months. The wet season is characterized by high humidity and weak southerly to southeasterly winds, while the dry season brings lower humidity and northeasterly trade winds 
(Engbring and Ramsey 1984)
. Humidity levels range from 65-90%. Typhoons can occur throughout the year but are more frequent during the wet season 
(NOAA 1982)
.
The El Nino Southern Oscillation (ENSO) is the primary driver of climate in the Pacific with events causing significant fluctuations in rainfall, wind patterns, air and sea surface temperature, sea levels, and cyclone activity, with impacts across the Central and Western Pacific Islands 
(Fletcher et al., 2012;
Keener et al., 2012;
Romine et al. 2013;
Crausbay et al., 2014;
Frazier et al., 2017;
Brainard et al., 2018;
Herring et al., 2018)
.

## Climate Change and Its Impacts

Changes in Guam's seasonal climate patterns, particularly those driven by natural variability such as ENSO, already pose threats to the island's physical, ecological, cultural, and economic systems. These include impacts on coral reefs, aquatic and terrestrial wildlife, and the habitats and ecosystem services provided. To address these challenges effectively, the Department of Agriculture must be equipped with actionable scientific research to understand the potential impacts of both natural variability and anthropogenic climate change. This knowledge is vital for developing and implementing effective adaptive management strategies that safeguard key assets such as land, water, fish and wildlife, plants, and the broader ecosystems upon which island communities depend.
In addition to the natural climate, Guam is increasingly affected by anthropogenic climate change. Across the Pacific, there is clear evidence of rising air and sea surface temperatures, sea-level rise, increased frequency and duration of droughts, saltwater intrusion into freshwater lenses, shifting habitat and species distributions, and altered ocean chemistry. Additional indicators include ocean acidification, shifting rainfall patterns, and altered wind and wave regimes. These changes present further challenges for small island ecosystems, including:
• Increased coastal flooding and shoreline erosion 
(Yeo et al., 2023)
 • Increased intensity of tropical storms 
(Yeo et al., 2022)
 • Loss and migration of forest ecosystems 
(Grecni et al., 2020)
 • Elevated extinction risk for native species 
(Grecni et al., 2020)
 • More frequent coral bleaching events 
(Cipolla, 2021)
 • Intensified threats to marine ecosystems 
(Grecni et al., 2020)
 These changes contribute to more extreme weather events, overall affecting ecosystems and species distributions. The implications are far-reaching, threatening freshwater availability, food security, wildlife habitats, and human health and infrastructure. Without proactive adaptation, these impacts are expected to intensify in the coming decades.

## Rich Species Diversity

Under natural conditions, Guam hosts a rich diversity of terrestrial and aquatic species. Over 100 species of birds have been documented on the island including migrant, wetland, seabird, grassland, and forest birds 
(Reichel and Glass 1991;
Engbring and Fritts 1988)
. Three native mammals are also known to Guam, the Marianas fruit bat (Pteropus mariannus mariannus), little Marianas fruit bat (Pteropus tokudae) and the Pacific sheath-tailed bat (Emballonura semicaudata rotensis), although the Marianas fruit bat is the only extant species. There are six native reptiles, five skink species, and one gecko species that are still found in the wild. Several native tree snail species still exist in low numbers in Guam with four species snails listed as endangered under the Endangered Species Act (ESA; 1973) (Samoana fragilis, Partula radiolata, Partula langfordi, and Partula gibba). There are more than 320 native plant species with seventeen being ESA-listed species. In addition, Guam holds more than 5,000 known marine species 
(Paulay 2003a
). Guam's SWAP identified 128 species including 60 terrestrial species (mammals-2, birds-14, reptiles-5, arthropods-1, snails-3, insects-2, and plants-33), 12 freshwater species (fish-4, eel-1, crustaceans-3, snails-2, plants-2), and 56 marine species (marine mammals-14, fishes-14, skates & rays-3, echinoderms-6, corals-3, arthropods-1, gastropods-1, clams-4, reptiles-2, marine plants-8).

## Threats to Biodiversity and Ecosystems

Over the last 50 years Guam has experienced tremendous domestic growth and suffered significant environmental degradation island-wide. Guam's native flora and fauna have been impacted by numerous insidious threats, such as poor land management practices, overexploitation, and unsustainable economic development. High demand for imported goods increases the number of invasive species entering the island, emphasizing the need for effective biosecurity measures. Anthropogenic threats are exacerbated by the increased frequency of natural disasters and other effects of climate change. Many of the terrestrial and aquatic species that may be of abundance now face environmental and man-made threats that could easily reduce or decimate populations. The cognizance of the many threats assailing Guam's native flora and fauna makes all species listed in the Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) legitimately a high priority for conservation actions. The various resource agencies of the Government of Guam and their partners continue to address these issues, knowing that economic prosperity and preservation of the Chamoru culture are dependent on the successful recovery and sustainable use of the island's natural resources.
into this revision. These improvements aim to provide a more consistent and effective framework for assessing species status and inclusion in the SGCN to guide conservation strategies.
Climate change is now addressed more explicitly in this plan. Anticipated impacts to Guam's wildlife, ecosystems, and natural resources require focused, adaptive management. This revision includes a commitment to collaborate with partner agencies to investigate and monitor the effects of climate change on biological resources.
Species accounts have also been updated. New information led to revisions in the objectives, actions, and conservation statuses for several species, including the forest birds, Mariana eightspot butterfly, and marine fish (see Chapter 3: Species Accounts). In addition, native plants used for traditional medicine are now included. Their inclusion supports the research and protection of these valuable species to reflect the importance of safeguarding Guam's cultural practices. Several species were added to this 2025 iteration, such as six sea cucumber species and three manta ray species. Candidate species under consideration, especially marine species with minimal information, will be evaluated for future inclusion. Taxonomic updates have been made for a few bird species. No SGCN were removed in this revision, but ongoing assessments will help refine the list by identifying priority species based on current population trends. The 2025 SWAP also includes landscape-scale conservation across the Mariana Islands through collaboration between the Guam and CNMI teams. In these efforts, conservation managers identified priority species in the region to establish common goals to support species' habitats, population, and movement through the islands.
Finally, new strategies to improve stakeholder engagement and public involvement are outlined in Chapter 8. Although participation in this SWAP iteration was much higher than that of previous versions, the Department is committed to building meaningful partnerships with technical experts, community members, students, and agency stakeholders to ensure the SWAP remains a relevant and effective conservation tool. Terrestrial Habitats
Prior to their decline, many of Guam's terrestrial native species were found more readily throughout the island and in a variety of habitats 
(Jenkins 1983)
. There are eight general terrestrial habitat types found in Guam as described by 
Fosberg (1960)
, 
Stone (1970)
, and 
Engbring and Ramsey (1984)
. Guam's terrestrial habitats have faced degradation since the introduction of domestic pigs (Sus scrofa) 
(Intoh 1986)
 and Philippine deer (Cervus mariannus) 
(Conry 1988)
 in the late 1600s and 1770s respectively, as these non-native animals disrupt forest regeneration by trampling and uprooting native plants, consuming seedlings, and facilitating the spread of invasive species. The introduction of the brown tree snake has resulted in the loss of many of Guam's native species of birds and lizards and the Mariana fruit bat 
(Savidge, 1987;
Rodda et al., 1997;
Wiles 1987a)
, drastically reducing the number of key pollinators and key dispersers. This decline has also contributed to reduced regeneration and diversity of native vegetation. These biological pressures are exacerbated by the ongoing loss and fragmentation of habitat due to urban development, military expansion, and infrastructure projects, which further isolate remnant native habitat patches. Climate change adds another layer of stress through increased temperatures.
Limestone forests, scrub (secondary forest), mixed woodland, ravine forests, strand, and beach strand are important habitats for all of Guam's native avian, invertebrate, reptilian, and mammalian species. Grassland, coconut grove, and open field provide habitat to native species, though to a lesser degree. The following describe the key habitat types, location, and current status important to the management and conservation of Guam's species of greatest conservation need (Element 1). A summary of the status of these habitats and their conservation actions is provided in Table 
3
.
Limestone forest. Limestone forests typically grow on uplifted coral reef limestone formations.
The terrain is rugged with sharp karst outcrops, sinkholes, and caves. Unique plant communities have adapted to thrive in these conditions. Limestone forests have a multi-layered canopy. The canopy is composed of large native tree species such as Ficus sp., Intsia bijuga, Artocarpus mariannensis and Elaeocarpus joga, with a moderately dense canopy of 10-30 m high. There are no or only a few openings in the canopy, and understory vegetation varies from open to dense. There are several distinct limestone forest types including Artocarpus-Ficus, Mammea, Cordia, Merrilliodendron-Ficus, and Pandanus 
(Fosberg 1960
, Donnegan et al. 2002)
.
Limestone forests are found on the northern limestone plateau and on large limestone outcroppings in southern Guam. 
Donnegan et al. (2002)
 combined scrub forest and limestone forest into a single category, reporting that together they made up 34% of Guam's available habitat. As a result, the actual extent of true limestone forest remains unknown. Vegetation mapping in 2005 indicated that only 13% of forested areas were composed of limestone forests 
(Brown)
. Most recent vegetation mapping reported that limestone forests made up only 10% of forested areas 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
. Increased development and quarrying for limestone aggregate have caused much of this decline. Habitat loss from these activities continues to reduce limestone forest cover. The structure of the remaining limestone forests has been altered by Philippine deer (Cervus mariannus) and feral pigs (Sus scrofra), which browse on seeds and seedlings, suppressing regeneration 
(Wiles et al. 1999)
. These ungulates also harm the integrity of the forest in many other ways (e.g. stripping bark from adult trees, causing erosion by wallowing, consuming bulbs of native orchids, etc.). Invasive plant species such as Vitex parviflora and Mikania micrantha alter forest structure by outcompeting native trees and understory plants, forming dense thickets that block light and prevent native species regeneration. Over time, this shifts forest composition toward monocultures of invasive plants, reducing biodiversity and degrading overall ecosystem function. Large-scale alterations of the limestone forests, whether from human activities like pesticide use and bulldozing, or storms such as Typhoon Mawar, are exacerbated by the presence of feral ungulates and invasive plants. Typhoons, especially in areas already degraded by invasives, accelerate canopy loss and erosion.
The combined effects of these threats continue to drive the decline and poor health of Guam's limestone forests. Without intervention and restoration, this habitat type will be altered so severely that it will not be a suitable habitat for the reintroduction of SGCN. This habitat is vital for almost all of Guam's native forest birds, snails, insects, lizards, and the fruit bat. These species developed ecological relationships over thousands of years with native trees and understory plants, relying on them for food, shelter, and nesting sites. These species often depend on specific native plants for fruit, nectar, leaves, or habitat structure, and in turn, some native plants rely on birds and fruit bats for seed dispersal and pollination. This co-evolution means that the loss of limestone forests disrupts these long-standing ecological relationships, putting many native species at greater risk.
Scrub forest or Secondary growth forest. Scrub forest is a degraded, yet diverse, brush-type forest, generally with an open canopy under 10 m high and a dense understory 
(Jenkins 1983, Engbring and
Ramsey 1984)
. While some native species typical of mature limestone forests persist, scrub forests often have a higher abundance of invasive plant species and lack the structural complexity and canopy closure of intact limestone forests. In northern Guam, this habitat is dominated by Vitex parviflora, an introduced species from the Philippines. Other introduced species that may be found include Leucaena leucocephala, and Triphasia. However, within this forested area native plants can be found as understory cover including Pipturus argenteus, Macaranga thompsonii, Ochrosia oppositifolia, Pandanus tectorius, Cynometra ramiflora, and other species.
Examples of scrub forest are the Tarague Plateau and Northwest Field region in northern Guam. 
Donnegan et al. (2002)
 combined scrub forest and limestone forest into a single category, reporting that together they made up 34% of Guam's available habitat. 
Brown (2005)
 later reported that this habitat made up 22.62% of the land cover in Guam. 
Amidon et al.
 reported that scrub forest made up 8% of the land cover on Guam 
(2017)
. Examples of these forest types tend to be rare and mixtures of primary and secondary species. Historically, these areas (Tarague and Northwest Field) were cleared for military purposes and repeated destruction by typhoons have played a major role in altering these habitats because of the presence of invasive species. Invasive tree species are often not adapted to typhoons and will fall over during typhoons but regrow in an uncontrolled manner after.
The structure of scrub forests is being altered by many of the same factors affecting limestone forests (feral deer and pigs, invasive plant species, and typhoons). In the absence of deer, pigs, and invasive plants, scrub forest regeneration into primary limestone forest habitat might one day be possible, although this would be limited by the lack of seed dispersers. Intervention is needed to ensure the continued existence of this habitat type. Although this habitat type contains many invasive plant species, it still supports numerous native limestone forest plants that provide resources for Guam's native forest birds, snails, insects, lizards, and fruit bats. With targeted forest restoration, including the removal of invasive plants and replanting of native species, these areas can be restored over time to functioning limestone forest.
Developed vegetation. Areas of developed vegetation comprise about 11% of the available habitat in Guam and include agriculture fields (ranches and cattle pasture) and other open areas including the unpaved areas around runways, and parks 
(Engbring and Ramsey 1984)
. These habitats contain a variety of grass species including Paspalum conjugatum, Panicum maximum, Chrysopogon aciculatus, and Sorghum. Scrub forests surround this habitat type in most places within the central portion of Northern Guam 
(Fosberg 1960)
. While open field habitats are the result of human disturbance, those that are mowed and maintained can provide important habitat for migratory birds including plovers and sandpipers. Unfortunately, these fields also provide ideal habitat for invasive weeds to take hold.
Coconut grove. Coconut (Cocos nucifera) groves consist of stands of abandoned coconut plantations 
(Engbring and Ramsey 1984)
. A coconut grove consists of 15-25 m high coconut trees with an understory comprising a variety of native and non-native shrubs and young coconut palms.
Coconut groves make up about 1% of the available habitat in Guam 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
 and are more common along sandy coastal areas such as Tarague, Tanguissan, Ritidian, Urunao and Pajon Basins. Small patches of C. nucifera are found throughout the northern plateau and most cover less than 5 ha of area.
Coconut palms (Cocos nucifera) are culturally significant and ecologically valuable in Guam, providing traditional food, medicine, materials, and habitat for wildlife. As a coastal and lowland species, coconuts also help stabilize shorelines and support nutrient cycling in open forest habitats.
Recognizing their ecological and cultural importance, coconut palms have been included as a SGCN. However, oconut palms are susceptible to termites and coconut rhinoceros beetles (Oryctes rhinoceros, CRB). Although this species was once the second most abundant tree on island (following the endemic cycad), many coconut and ornamental palms in Guam have been severely damaged or killed by CRB. There is no official estimate of the proportion of palms killed but dieoffs are occurring at an alarming rate (A. 
Moore, pers. comm.)
. Coconut groves and mixed palm stands, where coconuts still persist, are also experiencing ecosystem degradation from extensive rooting and soil disturbance by feral pigs, which further limits regeneration of coconut seedlings and associated vegetation. These altered habitats, while degraded, are still used by native species such as the Guam kingfisher, Guam rail, and Micronesian rufous fantail, which historically depended on coconut stands for foraging and shelter. Protecting and restoring these habitats, including controlling feral pigs and CRB, is critical for maintaining the ecological and cultural role of coconut palms on Guam.
Broken forest or Mixed woodland. Mixed woodland is a habitat similar to the limestone and ravine forest but dissected by many small, open or scrubby fields 
(Engbring and Ramsey 1984)
. This habitat type contains both native and naturalized vegetation having much lower, more open canopy unlike mature limestone forest containing Neisosperma, Artocarpus, Pandanus, and Cocos 
(Jenkins 1983)
. Like open fields, broken forest is a result of human disturbance occurring in northern and central Guam 
(Jenkins 1983, Enbring and
Ramsey 1984)
. Broken forest takes up an estimated 37% of land area on Guam 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
. This number consists of percentages of bamboo thicket, leucaena thicket, acacia forest, vitex forest, casuarina forest, mixed grass and herbaceous areas, and mixed introduced forest.
Ravine forest. Ravine forest is a patchy forest type found in narrow, steep-sided ravines. Its structure is highly variable but generally consists of a tangled mix of low-stature trees, shrubs, and dense brush, with an irregular canopy only a few meters above the ground. Vegetation tends to be denser around the edges of ravines, transitioning to a slightly more open, taller forest in the deeper ravine bottoms where a tree layer and shrub/herb layer may be distinguishable. Due to the limited extent of these habitats, their overall species diversity is generally lower than that of limestone forests. Ravine forests serve as important refuges for native plants and wildlife in otherwise degraded landscapes. This habitat type tends to follow topographic contours such as river valleys and depressions is only found in southern Guam. Ravine forests are highly degraded and contain many non-native species including betel-nut palm (Areca cathecu) and palma brava (Heterospate elata). Native species that can be found in these forests are Ficus prolixa, Glochidion mariannensis, Hibiscus tiliaceus, Pandanus tectorius, Pandanus dubius, and Premna serratifolia.
Historically, ravine forests were more abundant but have been reduced in quality and quantity by damage from deer, pigs, fire, and introduced plant species. Historically, ravine forests were more abundant but have been reduced in both quality and extent due to damage from deer, pigs, fire, and invasive plant species. Deer and pigs browse on seedlings and young plants, preventing regeneration and disturbing the soil, which accelerates erosion on steep ravine slopes. Fires, often human-caused, open the forest structure, allowing invasive grasses and shrubs to establish and dominate. Invasive plant species further alter forest composition by outcompeting native plants, choking understory growth, and preventing natural recovery. These combined pressures have led to the degradation and fragmentation of Guam's ravine forests. This habitat type is estimated to take 6% of land cover in Guam 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
. There are few native ravine forests left and efforts to restore these areas are underway.
Efforts by the Department of Agriculture's Forestry and Soil Resources Division (FSRD) to improve much of the degraded habitat of southern Guam began in 1997. FSRD started outplantings of Acacia sp. in the badlands, areas where much of the organic topsoil had eroded. Acacia are fast-growing, nitrogen-fixing trees that stabilize soils and improve soil fertility, creating conditions suitable for later native species establishment. In August 2005, FSRD expanded restoration efforts to include out-plantings of several native species to reclaim these areas.
Restoration of these degraded lands directly benefits SGCN by increasing available habitat, supporting food resources, and restoring ecological processes essential for native birds, bats, snails, and other species. Over time, these efforts aim to transition restored areas from early successional stands into functioning native forests.
Savanna or Grassland. Guam's savanna ecosystems are composed of several distinct plant communities that occupy specific habitats based on 
Fosberg (1960)
. These include the Miscanthus community, dominated by tall grasses in less degraded areas; the Dimeria community, found on drier, open slopes; the Phragmites (reed) community, which grows in wetter low-lying areas; and the weed community, which establishes after disturbances like fire or clearing. A key feature of the savanna is the erosion scar community, found on exposed, degraded slopes. These scars are first colonized by Dicranopteris lineraris, a fern that spreads across bare soil using horizontal rhizomes. Shrubs such as Myrtella bennigseniana, Wikstroemia indica, Scaevola taccada, Geniostoma rupestre, and Melastoma malabathricum, and intermix with sedges and grasses such as Dimeria chloridiformis. These plant communities can get colonized by fire-susceptible Casuarina equisitifolia and fire-resistant Pandanus tectorius that are typically stunted and in poor condition 
(Stone 1970)
. Although initially sparse, these communities slowly fill in as more plants establish, helping to stabilize slopes and control erosion across Guam's savanna landscapes.
Savanna is a habitat type primarily found on hillsides with volcanic soils in southern Guam 
(Fosberg 1960)
. These savanna habitats are frequently entirely burned by uncontrolled (and often deliberately started) grass fires. As a result, marginal trees are killed, allowing swordgrass and other associated savanna species to expand their total area 
(Stone 1970)
. While Stone observed colonization of native species, nowadays invasive grasses and non-native weeds and trees fill the gaps. This condition is further exacerbated by off-road vehicles that erode the bare soil especially during the rainy season. Frequent fires in Guam's savanna and forest edges burn away vegetation, leaving the soil exposed and vulnerable to erosion. Without plant cover to protect and stabilize the soil, heavy rains quickly wash the topsoil away, creating bare, degraded areas known as badlands.
Over time, these badlands expand as erosion deepens and widens scars, making natural recovery increasingly difficult. The sediment from this erosion is carried by streams and runoff into coastal waters, where it settles on coral reefs. This sedimentation smothers corals, blocks sunlight needed for photosynthesis, and disrupts reef ecosystems, contributing to the decline of Guam's already stressed coral reefs. Currently, savanna covers 16% of land area in the southern region encroaching surrounding forested areas 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
. This habitat is important as a foraging habitat for the Mariana swiftlet.
Beach strand. Guam's beach strand supports specialized plant communities adapted to harsh coastal conditions. On sandy shores, vegetation typically establishes just behind the active beach, where plants form a mix of woody and herbaceous species on beach ridges and sandy flats. On rocky shores, vegetation is limited to terraces, cliff edges, and crevices, where hardy shrubs and small trees grow directly from pockets of soil on fossilized reefs and limestone formations. The structure and composition of coastal vegetation vary depending on wind exposure, with stunted, wind-shaped plants dominating exposed northern shores, while taller, more diverse plant communities occur on more sheltered western coasts. Beach strand is a habitat consisting of open sand beaches, barren coastal outcroppings, and coastal areas of sparse vegetation generally 2-3 m or shorter in height 
(Engbring and Ramsey 1984)
. In most areas, open sand beaches are bordered with a mix of scrub and limestone forest. The dominant plant of this habitat is Pemphis acidula, a salt-tolerant species common on the pitted limestone of the windward coast. Tournefortia argentea and Casuarina equisetifolia are two other salt-tolerant tree species found along beach strands. Currently this habitat is found in most areas such as Cocos Island, Ritidian, Pati, Tarague and Pajon Basins, and islets around southern Guam. It is estimated to cover less than 2% of land cover around the island 
(Amidon et al., 2017)
. Beach strand is important for nesting seabirds, reptiles (nesting for turtles and lizards), and invertebrate species.
Strand forest. Strand forest generally grows on the inland border of beach strand habitat 
(Fosberg 1960)
. and is characterized by a mixture of trees at the top of the beach, adjacent to a forest comprising Pisonia grandis, Hernandia sonora, Cordia subcordata Barringtonia asiatica, and Casuarina equisetifolia along sandy beaches 
(Stone 1970)
. Along the rocky coasts you can find halophytic forests composed of different species, such as Casuarina equisetifolia, Mammea odorata, Terminalia catappa and Calophyllum inophyllum. Strand forest coverage is dependent on location, varying between northern and southern Guam. 

## Freshwater Habitats

Guam has considerably more wetlands and a wider variety of types than any of the other Mariana Islands. All of its rivers and nearly all wetlands occur in the southern and central parts of the island, where clay or argillaceous limestone soils retard water percolation and permit surface waters to accumulate. Many interior wetlands are located along the upper drainages of rivers and smaller tributaries. In contrast, the northern limestone plateau allows rapid water seepage, and consequently only a few marshy areas and ephemeral streams exist in the vicinity of Mt. Santa Rosa. Four basic categories of wetlands are described below, with larger sites often having more than one type and their relative condition and conservation actions summarized in Table 
7
.
Freshwater swamps. Freshwater swamps of woody vegetation are the largest category of wetland and may be found on the edges of marshes, along river courses, and in wet depressions in forests. H. tiliaceus is usually the major species involved, although the largest tract of swamp forest on the island, the Talo'fo'fo' River Valley, is dominated by Barringtonia racemosa 
(Fosberg 1960)
. Other trees that may be present are P. tectorius, Cynometra ramiflora, Heritiera littoralis, and A. catechu.
Freshwater marshes. Natural freshwater marshes are also common, with individual sites varying in size from the Agana Swamp (approximately 0.96 km2 or 237 acres of marshland) to many that are smaller than 0.5 ha (1.2 acres). Most are dominated by dense, nearly pure stands of Phragmites karka that are 2-5 m tall 
(Fosberg 1960)
. Other grasses (e.g. Panicum muticum), sedges (e.g. Eleocharis ochrostachys and Cyperus spp.) and the fern Acrostichum aureum are often present but usually less prevalent. Two other natural wetlands are important to the Mariana common moorhen (Gallinula chloropus guami) include the Atantano, and Namo wetlands 
(Takano 2003, Ritter and
Savidge 1999)
.
Reservoirs. Man-made freshwater wetlands were originally constructed as water impoundments for humans, cattle and crop irrigation, and are found widely through southern Guam. Many are no longer used but they continue to collect water and maintain aquatic ecosystems. The largest is Fena Lake (0.81 km2 or 200 acres), which still functions as an important reservoir for drinking water. Other sites are much smaller and tend to have deeper, more open water than natural marshes. These may include ponds dug for aquaculture purposes. Vegetation is variable, but Phragmites and Hibiscus are usually minor components.
A number of these sites are crucial to the preservation of the Mariana common moorhen 
(Stinson et al. 1991)
 and are very important to migratory species.
The Masso Reservoir is a 9105 m 2 (2.25 acre) man-made reservoir located in Piti. DAWR started work in 2005 to restore the reservoir to a state suitable for public fishing and also enhance it as habitat for the endangered Mariana common moorhen. The Navy constructed the reservoir just after World War II to act as a source for drinking water. Due to chronic sedimentation problems, the project was abandoned in 1951. The Navy retained ownership of the land containing the reservoir until 2000, when it was returned to the Government of Guam. Ownership of the reservoir was transferred from the Ancestral Lands Commission to the Department of Agriculture through Executive Order (E. 
O. 2006-14)
. DAWR has again received funding to rehabilitate the Masso Reservoir in Piti. Work on the site included dredging the reservoir to its original depth, installation of both a fishing platform and educational signage and out-planting of native and Acacia trees.

## Estuarine Areas

Estuarine wetlands occur in areas of tidal intrusion or brackish water and consist primarily of mangroves and the lower channels of rivers. Mangroves are dicotyledonous trees or shrubs found in the coastal area between nearshore shallow marine and terrestrial environments.
They consist of all species of plants and shrubs that have evolved physiological and morphological adaptations to live in salty, wet soil. They are defined by their ability to exist in the intertidal zone, which are facilitated by a daily tidal variation of about 75-90 cm. The largest concentrations of mangroves exist along the eastern shores of Apra Harbor, with smaller zones present in Malesso' and Inalåhan. Although they only total about 0.02 km 2 (5.56 acres), Guam's mangroves are the most extensive and diverse in the Mariana Islands. Species include Rhizophora mucronata, R. apiculata, Bruguiera gymnorrhiza, Avicennia marina, Lumnitzera littorea, Nypa fruticans, and Xylocarpus moluccensis 
Fosberg 1960
, Moore et al. 1977)
.. Other plants that are sometimes considered a mangrove, or otherwise a mangrove associate, are Heritiera littoralis, Hibiscus tiliaceus and Acrostichum aureum.
Records also have noted the presence of R. stylosa, which is extremely similar to R. mucronate, with R. stylosa just being distinguished by a slightly longer style in the flower. Some genetics studies have also considered the two species to be essentially the same. Rhizophora spp. mangroves are the most common and provide significant habit and coastal protection with large prop roots. L. littorea in particular seems to experience extremely low germination rates due to insect predation of the seeds and potentially lower pollination from the lack of birds in Guam. B. gymnnorhiza also may be suffering lack of pollination and potentially lower reproductive rates, due to the local extinction of the Micronesian Honeyeater or egigi, which pollinates this tree in Saipan. These two species, in addition to X. moluccensis are of greater concern due to these threats and their smaller populations, with a smaller range of suitable habitat given they tend to exist between the seafront Rhizophora mangroves and the terrestrial forest. Nine of the island's 46 rivers that empty into the ocean have true estuarine zones. The lower channels of these rivers, which are typically only 5-20 m wide and 1-4 m deep, have elevated salinity levels that extend 0.5-1.6 km upstream 
(Wilder 1976)
. N. fruticans is a common indicator plant of river zones with brackish water regimes. Marshes of bulrushes (Scirpus littoralis) are a third estuarine community and are found at several locations in Apra Harbor. The largest area is the artificial San Luis Ponds. This habitat type is important for many species of migratory shorebirds, native aquatic and terrestrial invertebrates, and aquatic vertebrate

## Rivers

The island has 100 rivers and streams, ranging in length from less than 1 km to more than 5 km, all located in the volcanic southern half of the island 
(Best and Davidson 1981)
. In southern Guam, a mountain ridge running along the western coast creates small, steep watersheds to the west and broader floodplains draining into longer, larger rivers to the east. Of Guam's 100 named rivers and streams, forty-six drain into the ocean. The largest of these, the Talo'fo'fo', drains an area of approximately 72.83 km 2 
(Best and Davidson 1981)
. Tables 
4
5
6
show data for some of the representative rivers of Guam. Note the greater lengths and watersheds for rivers draining to the east coast.
Forty-six of Guam's rivers drain to the ocean. Nine of these forty-six rivers have true estuarine zones. Fish fauna of this section includes few native freshwater species, but many marine species including snappers, jacks, halfbeaks, and mullets. These reaches are characterized by soft, muddy or silt-laden substrates, slow moving, relatively, deep water. The terrain in this region is flat. The dominant vegetation along this stretch of a river includes mangroves, Nypa, and Hibiscus.
Upstream of saltwater influence, most marine species drop out, and Kuhlia becomes the dominant predator. Most of the native gobies and invertebrates appear in this region. Usually, the substrate in this area of rivers is sandy, or a mixture of sand and cobble, with areas of base-rock or clay. Channel width is generally narrower than the estuarine region. The slope of the terrain is gentle and water velocity is generally slow. Dominant vegetation in this region includes bamboo and Hibiscus.
Above the first waterfall on a river, the most common habitat found is riffles and plunge pools. This is an area characterized by small runs separated by small waterfalls and pools. In the dry season, water flow may stop in this region, and the pools become separated from each other. The substrate in these habitats is usually pavement or larger rocks. Kuhlia and Stenogobius are unable to get past a waterfall and are not found upstream of the first major falls (>3 meters). Gobies and Macrobrachium shrimp are more common here, and Anguilla eels become the dominant predator. The water in this region is shallow (< 1 foot or 30.54 cm), except in the pools. This area generally has fast-flowing water, and a relatively steep elevation drop. Dominant vegetation in this region might include ferns, Hibiscus, and Pandanus. 

## Marine Habitats

Guam's marine habitats are extremely complex and can be described in a number of ways. They can be classified by the type of cover (e.g. coral, seagrass, unconsolidated sediment, macroalgae) or by reef zone (e.g. reef flat, fore reef slope). The combination of these two classifications leads to a large number of specialized habitats, so this document will primarily refer to reef zones, and describe some of the more important types of cover found in each of these zones. These marine ecosystems are ecologically and economically important and are directly influenced by landbased activities and watershed processes, further justifying the need for integrated coastal zone management across the island. The marine habitats, along with their relative condition and conservation actions, are summarized in Table 
8
.
In Guam, reef flats extend over a kilometer from the shoreline to the wave-washed reef margin.
The seaward part of the reef, which is usually slightly elevated and often exposed at low tide, is known as the outer reef flat. The inner reef flat is adjacent to the shore, usually retains water during low tide, and is often referred to as the "moat". Intertidal fringing reef flats have no significant moat development and tend to be fully exposed during low tides. These reef flats are common along the southern coasts. In addition, some reef flats, such as the Achang reef flat, have middle reef flat moat development. In these areas, the inner and outer reef flats are exposed during low tide, but the middle reef flat retains water. The reef flat zone may be composed of substrate including pavement, seagrass beds, thickets of staghorn coral (Acropora), microatolls of massive corals (Porites), sand/rubble fields, and macroalgae. Although all of these habitats are utilized by reef fish species, seagrass beds and staghorn coral thickets are perhaps the most important reef flat habitats for juvenile reef fish, including fish species of concern.
Seagrass beds cover approximately 3.7 km 2 (917 acres) of reef flats in numerous coastal bays around central and southern Guam (NOAA 2005). Guam's sea grass beds are composed of three species: Halodule uninervis, Enhalus acoroides, and Halophila minor 
(Lobban and Tsuda 2003)
. These sea grass beds range in size from small beds a few meters in diameter to continuous beds nearly one km 2 in size. Guam's sea grass beds are found along the reef flats in the southern part of the island including Cocos Lagoon and within the Achang Reef Flat Marine Preserve. These seagrass beds are used as foraging grounds by green sea turtles (Chelonia mydas). Other substantial seagrass resources are located in Inalahan, East Agana Bay, West Agana Bay, Pago Bay, Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve, and Agat areas. These sea grass beds are important nursery areas for many reef fish species including harvested species like emperors (Lethrinidae), rabbitfish (Siganidae), wrasses (Labridae), and goatfish (Mullidae). Seagrass extent in Guam has been declining, and threats include land-based sources of pollution, climate change, recreational misuse, and overuse from personal watercraft and trampling reef-users.
Lagoon zones are areas enclosed by the low tide line of the inner edge of barrier reef flat. Guam has two areas that can be considered true lagoon zones: Apra Harbor and Cocos Lagoon in Merizo. The depth in these areas ranges from less than one meter to over 30 m. Apra Harbor has sandy and muddy seafloor habitats in between scattered patch reefs and shoals. These patch reefs and shoals boast some of the highest coral cover in Guam with many areas reaching 100% coral cover. They also support diverse macroalgae and sponge communities. Cocos Lagoon is generally shallower than Apra Harbor and has a predominantly sand bottom with numerous small patch reefs scattered. This sheltered area has delicate staghorn coral communities that provide habitat for many important species, including the humphead wrasse (Cheilinus undulatus), which is listed under the Convention of International Trade of Endangered Species (CITES). The lagoon is also home to soft coral stands that provide unique habitat found in few locations in Guam's waters. These lagoons are utilized by a wide variety of reef fish species as well as both green sea turtles (Chelonia mydas) and hawksbill sea turtles (Eretmochelys imbricata).
Channels connect lagoons or reef flats to the outer reef slope. Some examples of this type of reef zone are the Manell and Mamoan Channels in Malesso' leading into Cocos Lagoon. Many smaller channels are found leading from reef flat areas to the outer reef slope. These are strongly influenced by tidal currents and host a wide variety of plankton-feeding animals. Depending on the strength of the currents, channels may have a barren floor of shifting rubble or may be thickly covered in hard and soft corals. Based on data from the region, these areas are likely key spawning aggregation sites for manyreef fish species. Manell Channel in Malesso' is an important congregating site for green sea turtles (C. mydas) and channels in Tumon and East Hagatña are known congregating sites for manta rays (Manta birostris) and other filter feeders.
The area extending from the seaward edge of the reef flat to the submarine terrace is known as the reef front or fore reef slope. The type of coral communities and the structure of the substrate in this zone are directly tied to the level and frequency of wave actionReef front zones that are protected from strong wave action are often characterized by a gentle slope covered with large stands of branching or tabular corals. As wave energy and exposure increase, the corals in these areas tend to become smaller and more compact. Areas with powerful wave action tend to be dominated by stout, lower-profile, branched corals. Channels in these areas may be strewn with boulders. Some of these areas have spur and groove formations characterized by alternating ridges and vertical sided channels.
Seaward of the reef front is the submarine terrace where the reef front flattens out. The channels become shallower with sand rather than rubble floor. In areas with constant surge, the corals are low-profile encrusting, stocky, or massive coral species. In sheltered areas, large branching and tabular corals are common. These terraces are frequented by schools of surgeonfish and parrotfish.
The outer reef slope extends down from the submarine terrace and into deep water. They are usually steep (greater than 30º) with moderate to high coral cover. Coral cover and species composition are strongly influenced by the slope's exposure to storm swells and presence of tidal currents. In general, coral cover and diversity remain high in shallow waters down to 40-60 m depth, with plate forms dominating the seaward slopes as deep as 112 m. Beyond this depth, gorgonians and soft corals dominate the habitat.
Twilight reefs, also known as lemlem taotao mattingan, are a valuable, yet understudied, marine habitat in Guam's surrounding waters. The "twilight zone" includes both the mesophotic (moderate light) and rariphotic (low light) zones. The rariphotic zone was only recently described and spans the dimly lit depths below the mesophotic zone, but above the complete darkness of the aphotic zone. The mesophotic (~40-130 m; 10-0.1% of surface irradiance) and rariphotic (~130-309 m; 0.1-0.01% of surface irradiance) zones support distinct species assemblages. Biologically, the lower boundary of the mesophotic zone aligns with the deepest occurrence of zooxanthellate corals (corals hosting dinoflagellate symbionts), while the rariphotic zone extends to the maximum depth where calcifying red algae occurs. The four locations with the deepest recorded zooxanthellate corals are all located in the Pacific Ocean, of which one is the Mariana Islands. While specimen-validated records of calcifying red algae reach depths of up to 268 meters in the Caribbean, photographic evidence suggests that these algae may occur at depths exceeding 300 meters in the Pacific.
Guam's rich history of deep-sea exploration has led to significant scientific discoveries. However, little attention is given to the twilight zone. To date, exploration of Guam's twilight reefs has been limited to baited remote underwater video station (BRUVS) studies and a few sporadic collections and surveys conducted via dredging and technical diving. While existing BRUVS footage primarily documents fish communities and lacks comprehensive spatial and bathymetric coverage, it reveals distinctive benthic communities, habitats, and previously undocumented organisms in the Mariana Islands.
Exploratory dives using an underwater drone have shown that the twilight zone harbors a diversity of habitats, many new for the Mariana Islands, that harbor a rich biodiversity in invertebrates and seaweeds. Many of the species in the twilight zone are new records or new species to science. Even those of commercial interest, like black corals, have been studied inadequately and preliminary data indicates that initial species identifications are incorrect, and habitat maps are lacking. Further research is needed to produce accurate species accounts along a depth gradient. Additionally, accurate habitat maps are required for managing valuable natural resources like precious corals. Twilight reefs are threatened by marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, outbreaks of nuisance species, and the introduction and spread of invasive species. The extensiveness of these threats emphasizes the need for research in these understudied areas, along with proper conservation carried out once further management needs are identified. • Gather and combine databases from research partners to make a comprehensive database on reef flats studies and monitoring. • Investigate impacts of vessels on channels.
• Reduce or remove marine debris in channels.
• Effectively monitor impacts of human misuse.

1999)
, were proposed to be included as conservation lands. Though this plan was never formally adopted, the proposed areas contain habitat that are vital to restoration efforts proposed in the SWAP. Ratification of this plan would be beneficial to all species listed in Guam's SGCN.
The Government of Guam owns the Anao, Bolanos, and Cotal Conservation Areas. The land area covered by the three areas is 16.5 km2 (4077 acres). Masso River Reservoir Area was also deeded to the Government of Guam in 2006 under an agreement with the Department of the Navy and Department of the Interior for conservation, protection, development, and management of fish and wildlife recreation resources. These areas vary in habitat types and provide unique opportunities for restoration of SGCN. Historic documents at the Department of Land Management identified a fifth Government of Guam Conservation land in Yigo Guam known as the Y-Piga Conservation area.
The Anao Conservation Area encompasses an area of 2.13km 2 (526 acres) and is located in northeastern Guam. This conservation area contains several habitat types to include limestone forest (75.2%), scrub forest (9.9%), coastal scrub (8.5%), Less than one percent of the area consists of bare rock/soil, developed land, developed vegetation, mixed grass/herbaceous, and mixed introduced forest. Coastal scrub vegetation can be found in areas below the cliff line along the eastern edge bordering the coast.

## Limestone forests dominate the upper plateau and windblown vegetation along the coastal cliffs.

There is a contiguous band of limestone forest that runs north to Pati Point and west to Ritidian Point (part of the GNWR Overlay). Fruit bats likely use the area to forage due to the abundance of native limestone forest species. Access to the area is via a single dirt road to a footpath that leads down the cliff to the coast. No developed recreational facilities are present, but hikers, hunters, and anglers utilize the area and an "educational trail" was established along the footpath.
Scrub forest is located along the northwestern perimeter of Anao. With ungulate control and outplanting by FSRD, partners and stakeholders, limestone forest could one day be restored within Anao, making it favorable for reintroducing native species. Eradication of the Brown tree snake is also necessary and vital to recovery in the area.
The Bolanos Conservation area, 10.33 km 2 (2,553 acres), is managed by DAWR for hunting and outdoor recreation. The area is composed of savanna complex (Miscanthus floridulus and other grassland species) (39.4%), native volcanic forest (37.1%), scrub forest (13.46%), mixed grass/herbaceous (6.2%), and mixed introduced forest (2%). Less than 1% of the area consists of bamboo thicket, bare soil, coconut forest, emergent wetland, leucaena thicket, and open water. Historically, the area was predominantly a ravine forest with very little savanna complex. However, humans and ungulates have changed the landscape through agricultural burning and browsing. Today, Bolanos is nearly an even mix of savanna complex and ravine forest. The range of ravine forest is reduced in each successive dry season.
Bolanos Conservation Area is difficult to access and is limited to hikers and hunters. The mosaic of fire-dominated vegetation such as M. floridulus makes this area and its limited access a challenge for natural resource managers. The restoration of native fauna to Bolanos would require reducing savanna complex and rehabilitation to ravine forest. However, portions of Bolanos that still harbor native vegetation should be receptive to native fauna should sufficient habitat exist to support such reintroductions. All efforts to rehabilitate Bolanos would involve ungulate and brown tree snake control.
The 2.54 km 2 (628 acres) of the Cotal Conservation Area is predominately covered by acacia forest (53%), savanna complex (27.5%) with scrub forest (16.5%), mixed introduced forest (13.8%), and mixed grass/herbaceous (13.8%). Barren and badland habitats make up approximately 2 percent of the total area. Efforts to improve the habitat have been met by repeated arson. A stream is present in the area and provides for indigenous freshwater fauna and reef protection. At present, this area provides little forest values and is not connected to the southern Refuge Overlay. This area is accessible by the public. Reforestation efforts combined with public education activities have been implemented in this area in order to restore SGCN. The Department's FSRD has begun efforts to restore native species to this area that would make this area available to release the efforts of native species of birds and other animals in the future. However, the prevalence of grassland fires in this area makes reforestation challenging.
The Y-Piga Conservation area is a relatively small parcel of 0. In addition to locally managed conservation lands, the federal government has identified conservation areas in Guam. On June 14, 1991, USFWS published its intent to establish a wildlife refuge overlay 
(Federal Register Vol. 56, No. 115, pp. 27485-27493)
. The Guam National Wildlife Refuge (GNWR) Overlay was established in December 1993 creating the 97.1 km2 (24,000-acre) GNWR Overlay. The Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) between the USFWS, the U. S. Navy (USN), and the U. S. Air Force (USAF) established the overlay units of the GNWR. The goal of this MOU was to develop cooperative agreements for the management of Guam's natural resources on federal and conservation lands. The USAF entered into a Cooperative Agreement with the USFWS with the purpose of establishing the overlay units on lands administered by the USAF in Guam, to define the management administrative roles, and responsibilities of the USAF for the GNWR. These federal agencies, among others, are valuable partners in the implementation of Guam's SWAP.
The GNWR Overlay includes a variety of habitat types within its boundaries. The habitats include limestone forest, scrub forest, savanna complex, ravine forest, strand vegetation, and Casuarina thicket. Like the conservation areas that belong to the Government of Guam, the habitat contained within the GNWR is degraded. The overabundance of feral ungulates and other factors continue to degrade these habitats. Guam DAWR will work with GWNR management, AAFB, and COMNAVMAR to improve the habitat for the benefit of Guam's SGCN.
Another critical conservation area is Islan Dånó (Cocos Island), a 33.6-ha, atoll-like island located 2.5 km southwest of Guam. The island is 1.93 km long and 0.15 km wide. The northeastern 24.8ha of the island (approximately two-thirds) is privately owned and managed by Islan Dåno' Resort. The remaining 8.8-ha area is a park managed by the Guam Department of Parks and Recreation (DPR). The habitat on the island can be grouped into beach strand, strand forest, and shoreline.
In September 2008, DAWR, DPR, USFWS, and CIR signed a Safe Harbor Agreement (SHA) under Section 10(a)(1)(A) of the Endangered Species Act that formalized the commitment of the parties to enhance the habitat on Islan Dåno' in support of the establishment of Guam rails (Gallirallus owstoni). The SHA is a voluntary agreement for the landowner to maintain, create, restore, or improve habitat for endangered or threatened species. In return, the landowner will not be subject to future land use restrictions or liabilities due to incidental take of endangered species on the property. In this particular case, the DAWR was responsible for the management actions to establish a breeding population of rails on Islan Dåno'. Islan Dåno' Resort provided shuttle services for management-related activities until the resort was closed in 2020.
Prior to release of captive-bred Guam rails, the rodents were eradicated from Islan Dåno', and predatory monitor lizards were reduced to give the newly released birds the best chance for survival. A Biosecurity Plan was developed and implemented for the prevention and early detection of incursions of brown tree snakes (BTS) and rodents onto Dåno'. Guam Forest and Soil Resources planted native trees and provided maintenance of invasive vines to enhance the forest on Dåno' for the benefit of native species.
On November 16, 2010, 16 captive-bred Guam rails were released on the government side of the island. The released birds were radioed and monitored daily for six weeks and then at least five times per week for the following months until the transmitters failed. Within 4 months, the first nest was located. In August 2012 another release of ten birds occurred. Subsequent monitoring observed a total of 29 nests, 56 eggs, and 23 chicks by the end of 2014. Initially, rails had maintained territories near the release site in the government park; however, monitoring and sightings of unbanded birds showed rails spreading to all parts of the island.
Islan Dåno' is a demonstration of Guam's conservation issues and how DAWR can collaborate with landowners to manage natural resources to benefit SGCN. Guam rails, Micronesian starlings, native skinks and geckos, and nesting seabirds, most of which are species no longer found in Guam due to the BTS, are breeding successfully on Islan Dåno'.
Not only is Islan Dåno' an important terrestrial conservation area, but it is also an important nesting area for green sea turtles (Chelonia mydas). DAWR has documented sea turtle nesting on the island starting in 1983 up to the present. Green sea turtles continue to come back to the island year after year to nest. Maintaining and monitoring the shoreline and beach strand habitat is important to ensure the development and growth of Guam's green sea turtle nesting population.

## Freshwater Conservation Areas

There are few freshwater conservation areas in Guam. Within the Sasa Bay Marine Preserve, a wetland site has been designated as a moorhen foraging site. There is also Fena Lake, the largest reservoir in Guam, which is important for nesting moorhens. Fena Lake is located in southern Guam, on Naval Ordnance Annex, which is part of the GNWR Overlay. Three rivers empty into this reservoir. Due to the relative inaccessibility of Naval Ordnance Annex, these are some of the most pristine river habitats in Guam.

## Marine Preserves

Over 10% of Guam's coastline has been designated as five marine preserves: Tumon Bay, Piti Bomb Holes, Sasa Bay, Achang Reef Flat, and Pati Point. The preserves were established in 1997 in response to decreasing reef fish stocks but were not fully enforced until 2001. Fishing activity is restricted in the preserves with limited cultural take permitted in four of the five areas.
The largest of the preserves, Pati Point Marine Preserve, contains 20 km 2, or approximately 4900 acres of reef environment. The area was originally designated as a marine preserve and managed area by the Air Force in 1973 
(Stevens 1973)
. The preserve includes narrow reef flats edged by steep fore reef slopes that are populated by a wide variety of coral species. The beaches within this preserve are important green sea turtle (C. mydas) nesting areas. Limited access to the preserve provides some level of protection; however, this makes the enforcement of regulations increasingly difficult. Under the territorial marine preserve designation, only hook and line fishing from shore is allowed for all species of fish.
The Tumon Bay Marine Preserve lies adjacent to the central tourist district in Guam. This 4.5 km2 (1117 acre) preserve features a broad reef flat (2.7 km2, 665 acres) and gently sloping fore reef slope (0.7 km2, 166 acres), and broad bank/shelf habitat (1.42 km2, 351 acres less than 100 feet deep). This preserve contains almost one km2 (253 acres) dominated by coral. On the fore reef slope, the dominant species are plate-and-pillar coral (Porites (synarea) rus), complemented by a wide variety of other species. The reef flat contains large stag horn (Acropora), lobe (Porites), and lettuce (Pavona) coral stands. These coral stands provide rich habitat for a variety of fish species including the CITES listed Humphead wrasse (C. undulatus) and many other species of reef fish.
Extensive sand patches that harbor sea cucumbers and a variety of scavengers complement this coral-dominated area. Limited traditional fishing with hook and line or talaya (cast net) from shore is allowed in this preserve for four types of fish: kichu (convict tangs, Acanthurus triostegus), manahac or sesjun (juvenile and adult rabbitfish respectively, Siganus sp.), I'e (juvenile jacks and trevallies, Caranx sp.), and ti'ao (juvenile goatfish, Mullidae species). Talaya may be used for kichu and manahak or sesyun, along the reef margin.
Piti Bomb Hole Preserve (3.6 km2 or 896 acres) stretches from Asan Point to the outlet channel from the Cabras power plant. The broad reef flat (1.4 km2, 349 acres) in Piti Bay extends from 72 m near the mouth of Tepungan Channel to 978m east of Asan Point 
(Randall and Eldredge 1976)
.
The reef flat includes unique, natural features known as the "bomb holes" that provide deep water sheltered areas The deepest of these sinkholes are 30-32 feet at mean lower low water (MLLW) 
(Tsuda and Donaldson 2004
). These sinkholes are densely populated by a variety of hard and soft coral species. They host rich soft coral communities and fish, and invertebrate assemblages are not often found within the reef margin. In addition, Piti Bay's fore reef slope has unique rubble-pile habitats utilized by a number of unusual/rare species of echinoderms and mollusks 
(Paulay 1994
). These features are not known from any other area of Guam. The western end of the preserve is transected by Tepungun Channel. Dolphins, large rays, and bumphead parrotfish (Bolbometopon muricatum) frequent this preserve. Since 1996, the main sinkhole has been occupied by the Fish Eye Marine Park, an 11 m (36 ft.) diameter underwater observatory. There is a 1.8 m (6 ft.) wide and 290 m (950 ft.) long pier that provides access to the park. This area is frequented by commercial scuba diving tours and is visited by large numbers of divers each day.
Sasa Bay Marine Preserve (3.1 km 2 or 770 acres) includes a significant subset (0.5 km 2 , 126 acres) of the largest mangrove forest in the Mariana Archipelago. This preserve extends from the dry dock island to Polaris point and ends at the public right of way bordering Marine Corps Drive (Route 1). The northern border is route 18 and the southern is the road to Polaris Point. The diverse mangrove swamp serves as nursery grounds and habitats for jacks (Carangidae), barracudas (Sphyraenidae), snappers (Lutjanidae), and groupers (Serranidae) 
(Wiles and Ritter 1993)
.
Additionally, the mangrove whipray (Urogymnus granulatus), eels, birds, and various crab species are present. The bay also serves as a foraging ground for green and hawksbill sea turtles. Coral habitat (0.02 km 2 , 4.5 acres) is limited inside of the preserve due to the heavy sediment load entering the bay from the Sasa and Aguada rivers.
The fifth preserve is the Achang Reef Flat Preserve in Malesso' (4.8 km 2 , or 1199 acres), which extends from the Ajayan Channel to Achang Bay. This lies within the Manell-Geus habitat focus area and the Achang Marine Preserve. It includes a wide variety of habitats including the island's only shallow water lagoon, mangroves, seagrass, sand, coral, and channel. The seagrass, mangrove, and estuarine areas of this preserve are important nursery area for a number of fish species. Three species of mangroves can be found at this site: Rhizophora apiculata ('red mangrove'), Avicennia marina ('grey mangrove'), and Bruguiera gymnorrhiza ('black mangrove'), with R. apiculata being the most common 
(Raymundo et al., 2018)
. Manell channel, the largest channel in the preserve, is an important habitat for green and hawksbill sea turtles as the surrounding areas include rich foraging habitat including dense sea grass beds. 

## Federal Marine Conservation Areas

The Guam National Wildlife Refuge Overlay includes the federally owned War in the Pacific National Historical Park, Guam National Wildlife Refuge, and two Naval Ecological Reserve Areas (ERA), Orote and Haputo. The marine portion of the two ecological reserve areas compliments the marine preserves. These areas were established as mitigation for construction projects in Apra Harbor for the naval base. Managed by the US 
Navy (1978)
, these areas are closed to commercial fishing, however there is little management or enforcement.
The Orote Peninsula ERA stretches from Orote Point south to Agat Bay. Diversity and species composition of the Orote Peninsula reefs are strongly influenced by physical factors such as wave exposure, currents, riverine influence, and bottom topography. A number of unique micro and macrohabitats exist in this area with very different assemblages found within each. A recent survey of the area indicated that Blue Hole, the Orote Boulder Fields, and the Orote Point Reef Slope were biologically important due to unique species and high biodiversity. The researchers identified 1252 species of marine animals within the ERA. This included 156 species of scleractinian corals, two of which (Leptoseris n. sp. and Favia rotundata) were new records for Guam. A total of 339 fish species were recorded, representing approximately 37% of the known species from Guam. Macroinvertebrates accounted for 657 species encountered during the qualitative surveys. Diversity appears to be related to habitat, with areas such as the reef flat between Neye Island and the coast, and the patch reefs in North Agat Bay, exhibiting high levels of diversity. In general, diversity declines from Orote Point southeastward and then increases again in the Agat area 
(Paulay et al., 2001)
 The Haputo ERA is located along the northwest coast of Guam, from just south of Haputo Beach to just north of Double Reef. The area is bounded by narrow, supratidal benches or unprotected rock faces; however, the area also contains two small, localized reef flats near Haputo Beach and Double Reef. Double Reef, an incipient barrier reef, is a unique feature in this area that creates highly heterogeneous habitat, including a distinct back reef community. Surveys identified, 944 species of marine animal, including 154 species of scleractinian corals. Quantitative studies indicated that coral cover was relatively high at most Haputo survey stations, ranging between 37-64%. This is higher than most surveyed locations in Guam's waters. Two hundred and seven fish species, approximately 22% of the known species from Guam, were identified at this site. Large piscivorous and herbivorous were rare. The study indicated that while corals were thriving, the fish targeted by the local anglers were less diverse and less abundant at the Haputo ERA than expected. The low abundance of target species suggests that overfishing may also be a problem in this area. 

## SGCN Inclusion Criteria

The revised criteria for inclusion in the list of Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) reflect a more comprehensive approach built upon the 2015 SWAP framework. These criteria incorporate legal, ecological, and cultural dimensions of conservation, ensuring that the SGCN list is scientifically grounded and locally relevant. The revised criteria identify species that are vulnerable to lack of management, data deficiencies, and ecological threats, and ensure that species significant to Guam's indigenous communities are not overlooked. A species may be considered as a SGCN if it meets one or more of the established criteria. This allows for a precautionary and inclusive approach that accounts for various types of conservation concerns. Together, these revised criteria provide a transparent and justifiable foundation for prioritizing conservation needs for our species of greatest conservation need. 
, 1990b)
. Major causes for the decline of bat populations in Guam include hunting (poaching) for human consumption, habitat loss, and predation by invasive species, though some of these effects have not been explicitly investigated. 
Wiles (1987b)
 suggested that snake predation of nonvolant pups while their mothers foraged was the main cause of poor recruitment. From 1980-82, the bat population was estimated to be about 850-1000 bats (USFWS, 1990b).
