## Birds

There are over 100 species of birds documented in Guam including migrant, wetland, seabird, grassland, and forest birds 
(Reichel & Glass, 1991;
Engbring & Fritts, 1988)
. The status of birds listed as SGCN is covered in the species accounts found at the end of this chapter.
There are two recovery plans for the forest birds of Guam. The Native Forest Birds of Guam and Rota of the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands Recovery Plan covered the six federally listed native forest birds, 
(Beck & Savidge, 1990)
 including the Guam rail (Hypotaenidia owstoni), Guam subspecies of Micronesian kingfisher (Todiramphus c. cinnamominus), Mariana crow (Corvus kubaryi), Guam flycatcher (Myiagra freycineti), Mariana swiftlet (Aerodramus bartschi), and the Guam bridled white-eye (Zosterops c. conspicillata). These species were grouped together for recovery purposes because they occupied similar habitats and faced similar threats. The Mariana Crow has been extirpated in Guam but still remains on Rota. The USFWS has completed separate recovery plans for the Mariana crow and Micronesian kingfisher. Unfortunately, the population of flycatchers and white-eyes in Guam went extinct in the mid-1980s and were removed from the endangered species list 
(USFWS, 2004
).
An amendment to the recovery plan was prepared for the Mariana swiftlet for the Mariana Islands in 2019 (USFWS, 2019). This species has been able to persist in spite of the brown tree snake and continues to survive in the wild, in several small colonies totaling over 900 birds 
(Cruz et al., 2008)
. The majority of birds are found in one cave (Mahlac Cave) on the Naval Ordnance Annex in southern Guam. The swiftlet population is vulnerable to snake predation, habitat destruction, and various stochastic factors affecting cave sites, including human disturbance, typhoons and flooding. Snake trapping and video surveillance at Mahlac Cave suggests that snake predation is a major factor limiting the size of the colony 
(Gorresen et al., 2024)
 In May 1995, the Micronesian Kingfisher Species Survival Plan (MK SSP) Management Group held a conference to evaluate the captive-breeding program, resulting in the formulation of an action plan designed to stabilize and increase the kingfisher population. The Micronesian Kingfisher Species Survival Plan Action Plan was endorsed by DAWR and USFWS and identifies critical objectives for recovery. One such objective is a captive population of 250 individuals. However, while breeding success in kingfishers has improved dramatically since the first publication of the SWAP, the captive population is currently at 150 birds. One limiting factor is the lack of available space for housing birds at mainland zoos, as well as the cost associated with holding birds until a release location is available.
Other limitations include mate incompatibility and aggression, rising levels of infertility, a decline in the number of fertile eggs that hatch, young adult mortality, and continual exposure to avian tuberculosis at zoos. Unless expedient recovery actions are taken to reverse the decline of the Micronesian kingfisher in captivity and to repatriate the birds to Guam, this species may yet become extinct. However, the success of the translocation effort on Palmyra Atoll offers a reason for hope and marks a critical step toward the eventual return of the Sihek to its native habitat on Guam (US. Fish and Wildlife Service, 2024).
Extirpation of the Mariana crow in Guam can be attributed to multiple factors; however, the primary factor is low reproductive success due to skewed sex ratios and predation pressure. DAWR worked throughout the 1990s and early 2000s to increase the success of wild pairs by collecting and hand-rearing chicks for release back into the wild. This method led to multiple successes, such as in 2003 when two captive-reared crows were released and later produced two clutches of fertile eggs in the wild. DAWR also developed, tested, and successfully deployed new techniques to protect nests of Mariana crows from brown tree snake predation. The use of electrified barriers on tree trunks was found to be highly effective in preventing brown tree snake intrusion into active nests 
(Aguon et al., 2002)
, and eight fertile crow eggs were protected via this method in the 1996 breeding season alone. These techniques could potentially also be used to protect the nests of other forest bird species.
Another of Guam's surviving native endemic bird species is the Mariana common moorhen (Gallinula chloropus guami). Unfortunately, this species is poorly understood and has been neglected in terms of studies of life history and dispersal patterns. They are threatened by predation by brown tree snakes and feral cats and dogs and habitat destruction by feral pigs. The wetland habitat they depend upon is also under threat from human encroachment. Guam's limited landmass for development means there is an increasing number of conflicts between the need for wetland protection and the need for commercial and urban development. To make informed conservation decisions for this species, additional studies of moorhen life history are needed, identifying ways to increase moorhen reproductive rates, increase survivorship of young birds, and minimize negative impacts from the aforementioned threats.
Migratory shorebirds are also frequently observed in Guam and are covered in the SWAP as one group. Individuals of many migratory shorebird species overwinter on island. It is much more than a minor stopover for them, but rather a crucial part of the habitat needed to complete their life cycle as they spend significantly more time here than on their breeding grounds 
(Kastner, pers. comm.)
.

## Reptiles

Guam has several skink and gecko species though their diversity is relatively limited. 

## Insects

There are two known endemic butterfly species in Guam, the Mariana eight-spot butterfly (Hypolimnas octocula marianensis) and the Mariana wandering butterfly (Vagrans egistina). The Mariana eight-spot butterfly has faced significant decline in recent decades resulting in its endangered listing in 2015 under the Endangered Species Act. The species naturally occurs in limestone forests where its two host plants (Procris pedunculata and Elatostema calcareum) are found. It is still observed in several locations in northern Guam, but population numbers have drastically reduced. The species is under pressure from several threats, such as invasive species and harsher climatic conditions, mainly affecting host plant health and abundance. The Mariana wandering butterfly was known to occur in limestone forests and has one known host plant, Maytenus thompsonii. Though its host plant is still found in Guam, the Mariana wandering butterfly is believed to be extirpated. There is not much known about this species.

## Gastropods

Guam's terrestrial gastropods are part of the unique biodiversity of the Mariana Islands. These native land snails play important ecological roles in nutrient cycling, decomposition, and maintaining forest health. They also hold cultural significance as their shells were historically in traditional ornaments and crafts as part of the island's natural heritage. However, Guam's native snail populations have experienced significant declines, largely due to habitat loss, the introduction of invasive predators, such as the rosy wolf snail (Euglandina rosea) and predatory flatworm (Platydemus manokwari), and other landscape changes. Partula gibba, partula radiolata, and samoana fragilis are three particular native snail species of concern and are included as SGCN. These snails are not only culturally significant but also serve as indicators of forest ecosystem health. Conservation of these SGCN requires targeted efforts to control invasive predators, protect remaining native forest habitat, and explore options for captive breeding and reintroduction to support recovery of these unique and highly threatened native gastropods.

## Plants

There are 320 native plant species known to occur in Guam. These plants are part of many different plant communities that are a diverse assemblage of endemic, indigenous, and introduced species. Together these plant communities are of high value to the island's ecosystem, natural resources, and people. Plants improve soil quality and reduce erosion, which helps sustain healthy coral reefs. Many native plant species were traditionally used for construction (homes, canoes, tools, and woven materials), food, and medicine. Of the 320 native species, the SWAP identified 33 terrestrial plant species/groups to be included as SGCN.
Two plant species deserve considerable and immediate attention. The first is the federally listed håyun lagu (Serianthes nelsonii). In 1994, the USFWS published a recovery plan for S. nelsonii that outlined objectives to recover the species, such as managing the Rota and Guam populations by protecting trees from feral ungulates with fences, controlling insect pests, habitat protection, and propagation and seeding of new areas (USFWS, 1994). The second species requiring immediate attention and action is the fadang (Cycas micronesica). This species was once the most abundant forest species. The accidental introduction of the Asian cycad scale in 2003 has decimated the population throughout the island. Unfortunately, there is little funding available to implement island-wide control of the pest. In 2015, this plant was listed under the Endangered Species Act, together with 13 other species.
Newly added SGCN include native medicinal plants (åmot) and other culturally significant species such as the coconut tree (niyok), nipa palm (nipa), and seeded breadfruit (dokduk).
Presently, major threats to Guam's terrestrial plants include typhoons, development, other anthropogenic threats, insect pests, fire, and browsing by feral ungulates. The introduction of species and varieties of the same genera may result in new varieties through crosspollination.
Freshwater SGCN Guam has 101 named rivers and streams, all located in the volcanic southern half of the island 
(Best & Davidson, 1981)
. These rivers are host to a wide variety of aquatic organisms, including fish, snails, shrimp, and plants. Guam's freshwater floral and faunal compositions are typical for oceanic islands. Each of the below sections describe/include all the native freshwater species and tables of those species but they are included even IF not all those native species are included as SGCN.
Freshwater Vertebrates Guam's native freshwater fish fauna consists of 11 species: two eels, one flagtail, one sleeper, and seven gobies. Gobies are the most abundant freshwater fish but all of Guam's native freshwater fishes are useful indicators of water quality and stream health. Three species of native fishes have very limited distributions and are at greatest risk from habitat loss. Three of Guam's native fish species (Stiphodon sp., Stenogobius sp., and Sicyopus sp.) are found only in the southern Mariana Islands. Stiphodon and Sicyopus favor hard substrates, such as rock and clay, which can be covered by excess sedimentation or overgrown with algae due to excessive nutrients in the water. Stenogobius is only found over sandy and soft substrates in the lower reaches of rivers. Excessive water flow due to riverbank alteration or loss of riparian vegetation can scour a riverbed, causing the loss of sandy substrates.
All native stream fishes exhibit an amphidromous life history, in which part of the organism's life cycle, usually the larval phase, is spent in the marine environment, while the remainder is spent in a freshwater environment. All of these species except the eels spawn in freshwater. The young are carried to the ocean by river currents, where they spend between two weeks and six months as larvae. They then return to freshwater, where they metamorphose into juveniles and spend the rest of their lives. The eels mature in freshwater but migrate to the sea to spawn. After spawning, the adults die. When the eggs hatch, the young migrate back to freshwater, where they spend the rest of their lives.
Because of these life histories, native fish are vulnerable to stream blockage. It is vital that rivers are left clear of dams or other blockage for returning fish to travel upstream to find suitable habitat. A study on the damming of Fena Lake in Guam found significant impacts on freshwater fauna, particularly amphidromous species both upstream and downstream 
(Conception, 1997;
Conception & Nelson, 1999)
. Dams can act as barriers to migration, leading to reduced populations upstream. Additionally, damming alters the overall ecosystem by changing flow patterns and modifying habitat conditions 
(Conception & Nelson, 1999)
.
Freshwater Invertebrates
Guam has seventeen species of native snails, belonging to two families. The thiarid (Stenomelania plicaria) and neritid snails (Neritodryas subsulcata) are the only freshwater invertebrates of concern in Guam. Stenomelania has a limited distribution in Guam, being found in a few rivers. Neritodryas is very uncommon in Guam, though it is widespread through the Indo-Pacific.
The neritids are herbivores, feeding exclusively on algae and diatoms found growing on rocks and other solid substrates. Neritids have an amphidromous life history, similar to that of fish. Eggs are deposited in freshwater, the young hatch, and are swept to the ocean. After a marine larval stage, they return to freshwater to mature. Nerites deposit their egg cases on solid substrates, including other nerites.
Thiarids are omnivorous, feeding on both plant and animal material. They are normally found on sand or other soft substrates, where they spend a good portion of the day buried.
Thiarids live their entire life cycle in freshwater. They are live-bearing, releasing crawlaway young that are miniature copies of the adults.

## Crustaceans

Guam has at least 12 species of freshwater crustaceans, including nine species of shrimp and at least three species of freshwater crab. All of these species are amphidromous, meaning they have a marine larval stage and a freshwater adult stage. Macrobrachium and atyid shrimp are the dominant crustaceans in Guam's freshwater systems.
Two families of shrimp are represented on the island: Atyidae and Palaemonidae. Atyid shrimp are small, generally less than 4 cm in length, and function as herbivores or detritivores, feeding on algae and organic matter using their brush-like chelae. The genera, Atyoida and Atyopsis, are typically found in shallow, fast-flowing waters, while Caridina prefers slower, deeper stream sections. Halocaridina, a cave-dwelling species with greatly reduced eyes, is found exclusively in caves and sinkholes along Guam's east coast. Atyid shrimp are an important food source for native fish and birds.
Palaemonid shrimp, which include the genus Macrobrachium, are larger omnivores, growing up to 15 cm. They feed using a prominent pair of chelipeds (pincers) and are widely distributed throughout Guam, inhabiting rivers, reservoirs, and caves. These shrimps are highly adaptable and often persist in intermittent headwater pools, where they are sometimes the only aquatic fauna present. Palaemonid shrimp are also harvested for food by local residents.
Guam's freshwater crabs are less well-known and represent a minor, often inconspicuous component of the island's freshwater fauna. Three species are currently known: Ptychognathus sp., Sesarma sp., and Varuna cf. littorata, all belonging to the Grapsidae family.

## Native Aquatic Plants

Guam has at least three species of native aquatic plants. The genera are cosmopolitan in distribution and two of the three species have wide distributions. One species, Potamogeton mariannensis, may be endemic to Guam. Potamageton are flowering plants and can produce both sexually and vegetatively. Ceratopteris thalictroides is a fern and reproduces via airborne spores. While none of these plants are abundant, they all have a fairly wide distribution in Guam. Potamogeton and Ceratopteris have relatively soft, submerged leaves, and are at risk from overfeeding by non-native snails and herbivorous fish.
Nipa palms (Nypa fruticans) are found in Guam only in the lower reaches of rivers that exhibit a true estuarine habitat. Nipa palms are found along only ten rivers in Guam (DAWR unpublished data). Nipa palms were traditionally used for thatch, as the leaves are long lasting compared to coconut leaves. Due to overharvesting and habitat loss, nipa has become uncommon and is now protected under Guam law.

## Marine SGCN

## Mammals

According to 
Eldredge (2003a)
, there have been 13 species of marine mammal recorded for Guam. The most common species are spinner dolphins, pilot whales, and sperm whales.
Recent surveys by NOAA researchers have increased the number of marine mammals recorded from Guam's waters to at least 20, to include, 4 dugongs, 4 species of baleen whales, and at least 16 species of toothed whales 
(Val Brown, pers.comm.)
. Species that have been reported from Guam and the Mariana Islands are listed in Appendix A.
The Marine Mammal Protection Act of 1972 protects all marine mammals found in the waters of Guam. Information about the marine mammals around Guam is limited to incidental reports. No rigorous study of populations has been undertaken to date.
Marine mammals are threatened by loss of nearshore habitat and feeding grounds due to land-based sources of pollution and overfishing. They are threatened by climate change, marine debris, getting caught as by-catch in nets and on longlines, oil spills, overzealous tour operators, in-water military training exercises, etc.

## Reptiles

Three species of sea turtles inhabit the waters around Guam: the hawksbill (Eretmochelys imbricata), the green (Chelonia mydas), and the leatherback (Dermochelys coriacea) 
(Eldredge 2003b)
. Of these, hawksbills and green turtles are known to nest on Guam's beaches and are included as SGCN.
All three species are protected under the U.S. Endangered Species Act (ESA) of 1973. The hawksbill was listed as endangered in 1973. The green turtle was initially listed as threatened in 1978 but was later uplisted to endangered in 2016. In addition to federal protections, both the hawksbill and green turtles are protected under local law (5 GCA Chapter 63 §63101-63117), with hawksbills designated as endangered and greens as threatened.
In 2015, NOAA and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) conducted a global status review of green turtles and proposed the listing of eleven Distinct Population Segments (DPSs) under the ESA 
(USFWS 2015a, 80 FR 15271)
. This proposal recommended replacing the existing global listing with individual DPS listings: eight DPSs as threatened and three as endangered. A public hearing was held in Guam on July 15, 2015, and the public comment period closed on September 25, 2015. The Central West Pacific DPS, which includes Guam, was proposed for uplisting to endangered. The final rule was published on April 6, 2016, formalizing these changes 
(USFWS 2015b, 81 FR 20057)
.
Green sea turtles are herbivorous and widely distributed throughout tropical and subtropical oceans. On Guam, they are observed year-round, with peak sightings typically in December-February and May-June (NMFS-USFWS, 1998a).
Hawksbill turtles, also circumtropical in distribution, primarily feed on sponges. Nesting is rare on Guam, with the first documented hawksbill nest reported in November 1991 (NMFS-USFWS, 1998b).
The sea turtle life cycle makes them especially vulnerable to a wide range of threats, both natural and human induced. As eggs, they are subject to predation by invasive species such as fire ants, pigs, and monitor lizards, and occasionally harvested by humans. Hatchlings face predation from ghost crabs, birds, and fish upon reaching the ocean. Juveniles and adults may be preyed upon by sharks.
Anthropogenic threats include oil spills, entanglement in fishing nets, and being caught as bycatch on longlines. Marine debris, particularly plastic bags mistaken for jellyfish, can cause fatal ingestion. Poaching also remains a concern, with mating pairs and nesting females particularly at risk. Although illegal, turtle meat continues to be consumed locally and is often found in the back kitchens at parties and fiestas. Turtle meat holds cultural significance in the Pacific Islands and historically served as a key protein source during oceanic migrations 
(Amesbury & Hunter-Anderson, 2003)
.
Nesting beaches are also impacted by coastal development, including the loss of sand above the mean high tide and increased exposure to artificial lighting. Light pollution disorients hatchlings, which rely on visual cues to find the ocean, typically the brightest area on the horizon.

## Fish

While many reef-associated species in Guam have shown declines in average size and abundance, two species are of particular concern: the humphead wrasse (Cheilinus undulatus), and the bumphead parrotfish (Bolbometapon muricatum).
The humphead wrasse or tanguisson, as it is known in Guam, is one of the largest reef fishes, reaching a maximum size of over seven feet, and a weight of over four hundred pounds 
(Myers, 1999)
. It grows slowly, taking up to ten years to reach a weight of 70 pounds 
(Kang, 2023)
. This slow growth rate, coupled with its habit of sleeping in caves in relatively shallow water, leaves the humphead vulnerable to overfishing. This species is one of the most popular and valuable in the live reef fish food trade in east Asia and is therefore subject to a great deal of collecting pressure. Foreign fishing vessels must travel further and further into the Pacific to meet demand. In 2004, the humphead wrasse was listed in CITES appendix II as a species of concern, primarily due to overfishing of the species throughout its range. Adult tanguisson are generally found on fore reef areas and are uncommon in Guam. Juveniles are seen more frequently on reef flats, especially in the Marine Preserves.
The bumphead parrotfish, known as atuhong in Guam, is another large reef fish vulnerable to overfishing. This reef fish is also slow growing, and its habit of sleeping in large groups in shallow water makes it vulnerable to overfishing, particularly nighttime spearfishing. This fish, like the tanguisson, is uncommon in heavily populated areas. However, in remote areas, or areas with low human populations, it can still be found in large numbers where there is sufficient habitat of lagoonal and fore reef areas. This species is not used in the live reef fish trade and is not subjected to the intense foreign fishing pressure that the humphead wrasse faces. The atuhong has been almost completely absent from Guam's waters for decades. Other groups of reef fish that are a concern in Guam include groupers (Serranidae) and fish collected for the aquarium trade (Chaetodontidae, Pomacanthidae, Cirrhitidae). Groupers' life history makes them vulnerable to overfishing. They are slow growing with maturity coming after several years for most species 
(Myers, 1999)
. Groupers are not migratory. Individuals usually find a cave or other structure to act as a home base and seldom venture far from it. This not only makes them easy to find but the replacement of individuals caught for consumption is a slow process. Groupers are voracious ambush predators that will readily strike at lures or bait. They form seasonal spawning aggregations, based on a lunar cycle, and individuals may migrate for miles to congregate in favored sites 
(Myers, 1999)
 Aquarium fish collecting is not currently a major part of fish taken in Guam. In the 1980s and 1990s, however, Guam was a major source for aquarium fish in Japan and the United States. Hundreds of fish were shipped from Guam each month. The most popular species were rusty angelfish (Centropyge shepardi), flame hawkfish (Neocirrhites armatus), and butterfly fish (Chaetodontidae) (DAWR). While no quantitative data has been taken, the populations of these species certainly had to be impacted by this level of collecting for multiple years. Flame hawkfish are common in areas without aquarium fish collecting 
(Myers, 1999)
. In Guam, however, this is not a common species.

## Skates and Rays

Guam's marine environments host an array of ray species, including the reef manta ray (Mobula alfredi), oceanic manta ray (Mobula birostris), sicklefin devil ray (Mobula taracapana), mangrove whipray (Urogymnus granulatus), and ocellated eagle ray (Aetobatus ocellatus). These species are found in various habitats around the island, from shallow lagoons and mangrove habitats to coral reefs and deep offshore waters. As key components of benthic food webs, rays play an important role in maintaining ecological balance and supporting local marine biodiversity.
The reef manta ray, oceanic manta ray, and sicklefin devil ray are newly identified SGCN in this SWAP iteration. Reef and oceanic manta rays are slow-reproducing, long-lived planktivores. The reef manta rays make up a resident population in Guam's waters. Individuals are commonly spotted along coral reefs, bays, atolls, and productive coastlines. Guam's reef manta rays target surgeonfish spawning aggregations to feed on fish spawn, marking the first recorded observation of this behavior 
(Hartup et al., 2013)
. They can reach wingspans of up to 11.5 feet and weigh up to 3,000 pounds. Each manta ray features a unique spot pattern on its ventral side, allowing for tracking through photogrammetry. It is listed as vulnerable to extinction on the IUCN Red List and CITES Appendix I and II. Although the species is found across Micronesia, research on local population size and trends remains limited.
Oceanic manta rays share similarities with the reef manta ray and were previously considered a single species but were split into two distinct species in 2009. Oceanic manta rays are known for large migrations around open ocean areas of high productivity and often aggregate around underwater sea mounds, banks, and continental shelves. Snorkelers, divers and fishermen often confuse one species for the other, making it harder to determine sightings without photographs. By-catch data from commercial fisheries document both species, as their habitats are located in tropical and subtropical regions. They have been documented in the CNMI and Guam, but very little is known. The oceanic manta ray is listed on the US endangered species list; the IUCN endangered and CITES Appendix I and II.
The sicklefin devil ray is listed as endangered on the IUCN red list, CITES Appendix I and II, with the reef manta ray. The sicklefin devil ray shares similarities with oceanic manta rays in its offshore habitat and life history traits. Although rarely photographed, several videos have documented nearshore encounters with this species around Guam. The use of offshore habitats by both the sicklefin devil ray and oceanic manta ray limits our understanding of these species.
All three species face several threats throughout their range, including bycatch from commercial fishing nets, harassment or disturbance from irresponsible human activity, and altered feeding patterns caused by climate change. In Guam, rays are protected under Bill No. 44-31, which makes it unlawful for anyone to possess, sell, offer for sale, take, purchase, barter, transport, export, import, trade, or distribute ray parts.

## Corals

Corals are the backbone of the reef community, but they are also some of the most sensitive creatures in this ecosystem. Pollution, development, sedimentation, and climate change all pose serious threats to Guam's corals. Many of Guam's reefs have declined in health over the past 50 years.
In September 2014, three species of corals known to Guam were added to the Endangered Species list as threatened: Acropora globiceps, Acropora retusa, and Seriatopora aculeata 
(ESA, 1973)
. Of these, A. globiceps remains relatively common on Guam's reefs.
Acropora retusa was initially believed to occur in Guam's waters based on a limited number of observations. However, NOAA's 2023 5-Year-Review concluded that these records likely represent different or undescribed species. As a result, A. retusa is no longer considered to occur in the Mariana Islands. Seriatopora aculeata was only recorded a handful of times between 1980 and 2010 and has not been observed since. NOAA concluded that available records do not support the species' current occurrence in the Mariana Islands (NOAA, 2023). While A. retusa and A. Seriatopora were SGCN in previous iterations of the SWAP, these findings have resulted in their removal from this revision.
In 2015, the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) proposed a potential 4(d) Rule for the ESA listed coral species in the Pacific Island Region, including Guam. Under the ESA, species listed as threatened are not protected from "take," which includes activities such as harming, harassing, or collecting individuals. A 4(d) Rule allows NOAA to enact specific prohibitions to support species conservation. However, a 4(d) has not been finalized for coral species and federal prohibitions will not apply until such rule is established. The absence of this regulation further threatens the health and safety of A. globiceps individuals in Guam.

## Echinoderms

Echinoderms are a diverse group of marine invertebrates that include sea stars, sea urchins, brittle stars, and sea cucumbers. Found exclusively in marine environments, they are known for their radial symmetry, calcareous skeletons, and unique water vascular systems, which aid in locomotion, feeding, and respiration. Echinoderms play important ecological roles in maintaining the structure and function of benthic marine ecosystems.
Sea cucumbers (Holothuroidea), like other echinoderms, play a vital role in maintaining healthy reef ecosystems. They help recycle nutrients by breaking down organic matter in the sand, which enhances reef sediment quality 
(Lee et al. 2017;
Keighan 2023)
. Their digestive processes and excretion of fecal matter also help buffer ocean acidification by increasing the alkalinity of surrounding waters 
(Purcell et al. 2016)
. Protecting sea cucumbers is not only critical for biodiversity, but also for the long-term resilience of coral reef systems.
Several species of sea cucumbers that have been listed as endangered, vulnerable, or data deficient in the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species because of commercial overharvesting. These are the deepwater red fish (Actinopyga echinites), surf red fish (Actinopyga varians), the brown teatfish (Holothuria fuscogilva), the black teatfish (Holothuria whitmaei), pineapple sea cucumber (Thelenota ananas), and giant sea cucumber (Thelenota anax). Currently in Guam, individuals are permitted to harvest 100 animals per person per day. This level of harvest is unsustainable given their commercial value of these species and their slow growth and recovery rates. Species specialists suggest working with policy makers and local communities to reduce take of these ecologically important species.

## Arthropods

Marine arthropods in Guam include several culturally and economically important species, among which the spiny lobster (Panulirus spp.) is of particular concern. Spiny lobsters inhabit coral reefs and rocky coastal areas, where they shelter in crevices during the day and forage at night. They are highly valued for local consumption. Overfishing and habitat degradation pose ongoing threats to their populations.

## Gastropods

Guam's marine gastropods are also of cultural and ecological importance. The Triton's Trumpet (Charonia tritonis) is a large snail, with an attractive shell highly prized by shell collectors. In areas of dense human population, these animals are quite rare 
(Smith, 2003)
.
In some areas, these snails are collected as a food item, and the shells may be used as horns by some island people, but more frequently, they are taken for decorative purposes.
Triton's trumpet feeds on echinoderms and is one of the few natural predators of the Crown of Thorns sea star.

## Bivalves

Several marine invertebrates are of concern. Four mollusks of concern are the giant clams and the gastropod, Triton's trumpet snail. Guam currently has four species of giant clam: Tridacna derasa, Tridacna maxima, Tridacna squamosa, and Hippopus hippopus. Local law in Guam currently restricts the taking of giant clams to individuals larger than 7 inches across. In addition, no more than three may be taken per day for food, no more than ten shells may be taken per day for shell collecting, and no giant clams may be taken for commercial purposes.
Giant clams of the family Tridacnidae are widespread through the Indo-Pacific. Tridacna clams are notable for their mantle, which is large and brightly colored, and for their life history. Giant clams do not feed but instead rely on symbiotic algae living in their mantle to produce food for them. Giant clams are collected for their meat, for their shells, and for the aquarium trade. Giant clams are a popular source of meat on many islands. The shells may be harvested for the shell trade or may be used to make jewelry by indigenous peoples, including the Chamorro people of Guam. In recent years, the aquarium trade has come to rely almost exclusively on farmed clams, and the take of wild clams for the aquarium trade is minimal. Due to over-harvesting, giant clams are becoming uncommon or even extinct in many parts of their native ranges. Giant clams are listed under CITES Appendix II as species of concern, thus international trade in these bivalves is restricted.
In addition, NOAA Fisheries recently proposed listing several species of giant clams under the U.S. Endangered Species Act (ESA). Under this proposed rule, Tridacna derasa and Hippopus hippopus,both present in Guam, would be listed as endangered and threatened, respectively. This would add further federal protections but could restrict future harvest, possession, and trade of these culturally significant species.

## Seagrasses

Seagrasses play a vital ecological role in Guam's nearshore waters by stabilizing sediments, supporting biodiversity, improving water quality, and sequestering carbon. Three species, Enhalus acoroides, Halodule uninervis, and Halophila nipponica, are commonly found in protected lagoons and reef flats across the island. Guam's seagrasses face a number of threats such as sedimentation, marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, outbreaks of nuisance species, habitat loss, disease, and recreational impacts. These stressors can reduce coverage and degrade habitat quality. While none of these species are currently listed as globally threatened on the IUCN Red List, they are locally vulnerable due to limited distribution and increasing coastal pressures. Monitoring and protection of seagrass beds are important for maintaining reef resilience and ecosystem function.
Seaweeds; Marine Macroalgae Seaweeds are primary producers on tropical reefs, forming the base of the food web and serving as an important resource for both marine herbivores and humans. Across the Pacific, seaweeds are named and used in diverse ways, often reflecting deep-rooted cultural traditions. In the CHamoru language, various names are used for different seaweed types based on their appearance, use, or occurrence. Ado' refers to the edible Caulerpa "sea grapes," which are sold at markets and commonly eaten as a side dish of fresh sea vegetables. Chå'guan tåsi, literally meaning "grass of the sea," is a general term for seagrasses but also includes certain seaweeds like Sargassum, which is traditionally used as bait to catch unicornfish using rod and reel. Other CHamoru names include chacha (Gracilaria tsudae), take' uchan (the terrestrial blue-green alga, Nostoc), lo'u (a general term for seaweeds), gulimån, and lumot tasi. In addition to long-standing traditional uses, newer practices involving seaweeds have emerged in response to ecological changes on Guam's reefs. For example, large blooms of the green seaweed Chaetomorpha vieillardii have led to its use as bait for rabbitfish and as a fresh ingredient in salads.
Currently, all locally sourced seaweed in Guam is wild harvested. While the large-scale farming of agar-and carrageenan-producing seaweeds is not economically viable due to competition from regional producers such as Indonesia and the Philippines, mariculture of high-value species may hold promise. Because many tourists visiting Guam come from Asian countries where seaweed is a traditional and valued part of the diet, this creates a unique opportunity to develop niche markets for seaweed-based products. Additionally, emerging industries in pharmaceuticals and animal feed are increasingly interested in nontraditional, currently unfarmed seaweed species, presenting new avenues for aquaculture development.
Guam's fringing reefs are textbook examples of algal ridges, where calcifying red algae dominate reef crests exposed to intense wave action and desiccation. These algae form a thin, living veneer that consolidates and protects the reef structure. Beyond the crest, calcifying red algae are also abundant across other reef habitats, where healthy communities are essential for coral recovery following disturbance or mortality events. They are the preferred recruitment substrate for coral larvae, and species-specific interactions, mediated by chemical settlement cues, can significantly enhance larval settlement and recruitment success 
(Deinhart et al., 2022;
Abdul Wahab et al., 2023)
.
Species of concern, such as Rhodophyta (red seaweeds), Ulvophyceae (green seaweeds), and Phaeophyceae (brown seaweeds), occur in marine and estuarine environments from shore to twilight zone. These species face many threats including marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, habitat loss, outbreaks of nuisance species, introduction and spread of invasive species, and overharvesting. Calcifying red algae, Rhodophyta: Corallinophycidae, Peyssonneliales, face similar threats.
Large-scale outbreaks of nuisance seaweeds have become an increasing concern in the Mariana Islands. Additionally, studies indicate that several non-native seaweed species have become naturalized in Guam's waters. As the main entry point for maritime traffic, Apra Harbor is likely the primary site of introduction for many non-native species. A comprehensive baseline survey of seaweed diversity and reef community composition in Apra Harbor is therefore strongly recommended to support early detection, monitoring, and prevention of further spread.
Individual Species Accounts

## Fanihi

Pteropus mariannus

## Mariana fruit bat

Photo by Scott Vogt

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Earliest records of bats in Guam did not provide counts but simply noted that bats were "not uncommon" 
(Crampton, 1921;
Wiles, 1987)
. It was not until the 1950s that an estimate of several thousand bats, mostly found near Fena Lake in Southern Guam, was recorded (D. H. Woodside 6 month visit in Guam) 
(Wiles, 1987)
. The fanihi population slowly continued to decline until intermittent, concerted population surveys were started in the 1990s and early-2000s. A significant increase to about 850-1000 bats in the population was noted in the 1980s, probably a result of the temporary migration of bats from Rota to Guam; this is speculated to be the result of typhoon disturbance at a colony on Rota 
(Janeke, 2003)
. Between 1983 and 2008, Guam's fanihi population declined dramatically to 425-500 individuals 
(Wiles et al., 1989)
. By the early-2000s, the population of bats had been reduced to a single colony with as little as 50 individuals at Pati Point on Andersen Air Force Base (AAFB) 
(Brooke, 2008;
SWCA Environmental Consultants, 2013)
. This sharp decline was attributed to several threats, especially predation by the brown tree snake (Boiga irregularis, kulepbla), which likely preys on non-flying young while their mothers forage, but also by illegal poaching, habitat degradation caused by feral ungulates, and human disturbances (U.S. 

## THREATS:

The Mariana fruit bat faces a host of threats, including native habitat loss by development and military training activities, habitat degradation by humans and non-native species, illegal hunting, and predation by the invasive brown tree snake (Boiga irregularis), and natural disturbance such as typhoons 
(Wiles, 1987a
(Wiles, , 1987b;;
AAFB, 2008;
USFWS, 2009)
. Habitat loss and declining forest health may have detrimental effects to the bat's food sources and roosting sites. Poaching of bats occurs as a result of the historical, cultural practice of consuming bats, which peaked during World War II and the years preceding, likely as a result of severe rationing imposed on the CHamoru peoples by the Japanese 
(Rogers, 1995)
. After the war, poaching events were less common, likely due to the protected location (within AAFB) and small population size.

## HABITAT(S):

Native mature limestone and ravine forest GOAL: Implement Recovery Plan actions, restore/maintain healthy, manageable populations of Mariana fruit bat, and fill ecological knowledge gaps for this species to improve future conservation management initiatives OBJECTIVES: Establish safe foraging and roosting sites around the island that inhibit poaching and have predator monitoring and control measures in place. Further enhance the knowledge of fruit bat ecology and life history to better inform resource managers.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Investigate limiting factors on the remaining population at known roost sites and foraging areas, develop and implement effective control measures, such as multi-species barrier exclusion fencing, toxicants and biocontrol. 

## Fanihi liyang

## Emballonura semicaudata rotensis

Pacific sheath-tailed bat

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The sheath-tail bat is a subspecies endemic to Guam, Rota, Tinian, Saipan, and Aguiguan. The Pacific sheath-tailed bat previously occurred in Guam and was last seen in 1972 
(Wiles et al., 1995)
. Little is known about its ecology and natural history. As of 2004, the cave dwelling mammal only existed in the island of Aguiguan, with an estimated population of 400-500 individuals 
(Esselstyn et al., 2004)
. Most recent surveys only found the species in Aguigan with a single population estimated at a minimum of 300 individuals (USFWS, 2023).
THREATS: Predation, competition, human disturbance, and pesticides may have caused this species to be extirpated in Guam.

## HABITAT(S):

Forages in forested areas, known to previously roost in caves in southern and northern Guam.

## GOAL:

Reduce snake numbers at historic roosting caves. Research biology, ecology, and other pertinent natural history information to increase knowledge of the species (and similar species). Investigate and obtain information about current roost caves in the CNMI to compare with caves in Guam scheduled for enhancement and preparation for future species reintroduction.
OBJECTIVES: To establish a small population of bats in a historic snake-free cave in northern Guam. To assist CNMI efforts to conduct surveys and restore population in Aguiguan. To determine the feasibility of implementing a captive breeding program.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators and competition: Investigate snake population at historic caves and plan for predator control measures to be implemented. Investigate Black drongo (Dicrurus macrocercus) population near historic caves and bat's foraging areas. • Knowledge gaps: Explore partnerships with government agencies (Guam and CNMI), non-profit groups, NGOs, and other stakeholders to establish projects to study the natural history of the Sheath-tail bat to encourage research opportunities and increase monitoring efforts of the current population in Mariana Islands. • Habitat degradation and loss: Prevent the destruction, and human disturbance of caves historically known to harbor sheath-tailed bats. Reduce deer and feral pig numbers at the colony and adjacent areas, develop and implement reforestation techniques in Guam's local and federal conservation areas. • Outreach and Awareness: Revitalize and expand the "Prutehi I Fanihi" campaign to address Sheath-tail bat. Establish partnerships with other agencies, groups, and stakeholders in Guam and the CNMI to develop and implement the campaign.

## Ko'ko'

## Gallirallus owstoni

Guam rail

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Guam rail, endemic to Guam, was once found island wide with an estimated 60,000 in 1960 
(Jenkins, 1979)
. By the early 1970s, it had disappeared from southern Guam but was still observed in central and northern areas 
(Beck & Savidge, 1990)
. By 1981, the population had declined to approximately 2,300 birds, concentrated on Andersen Air Force Base (AAFB) 
(Beck & Savidge, 1990)
 In response to this rapid decline, a captive breeding program was initiated in 1984 with the capture of 22 individuals 
(Newland, pers. comm.)
. Since then, over 2,000 rails have been bred in captivity from 12 rails in that founding population 
(DOAG, 2016
).
An experimental release program began on Rota in 1989, and more than 1,000 birds have been released to date. Efforts to re-introduce the rails to snake-controlled habitats on Guam began in 1998 and 2003, including releases in Area 50 on AAFB, where nesting and hatchlings were observed. However, the birds failed to establish due to the likely predation by feral cats. By 2019, the population on Rota was estimated at 200 individuals. The last release on Rota took place in 2020 but the current number is unknown as they have not been detected since 2023. The Department aims to conduct more surveys to determine population numbers and anticipate more releases later this year and 2026. As of June 2025, approximately 30 birds were held across 9 AZA-accredited zoos in the United States 
(Newland, pers.comm.)
. The Department's breeding facility currently houses 85 birds in the captive breeding program, and an additional 25-40 birds are found on Islan Dåno' where active nesting and chick sightings confirm successful reproduction.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include: habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, feral cat, feral dog, and monitor lizard predation. Populations on Rota are known to be threatened by wildfires and should be accounted for as a potential threat for re-introduction in Guam. Little fire ants could also threaten chick survival and reproductive success.

## HABITAT(S):

The Guam rail has been observed in all habitats except wetlands.

## GOAL:

To re-establish a breeding population of rails in Guam and protect the currently established insurance population on Islan Dåno'.

## OBJECTIVES:

To maintain captive breeding population to provide for releases in the wild. To establish rails in designated predator-controlled sites in Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Continue and expand brown tree snake and feral cat control efforts at potential release sites and continue supporting efforts to improve brown tree snake studies and measures. • Small or extirpated populations: Continue captive breeding efforts and participation in Guam Rail Species Survival Plan to maintain a sustainable captive ko'ko' population that will enable both future ko'ko' introductions and supplemental releases of existing wild populations as needed. Conduct further surveys to determine population numbers in Rota. Continue support of existing wild populations on Rota and Islan Dåno' through control of introduced predators and, if needed, supplemental releases of additional birds. Identify additional suitable release sites for ko'ko' elsewhere on Guam and within the CNMI for new introductions.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

This subspecies of white-throated ground dove is endemic to the Mariana Islands and Yap. Historically, this species was abundant throughout all habitats in Guam. By the 1980s, its population was limited to the northern part of the island with about 548 individuals 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
 but was considered extirpated from Guam by 1986 as a result of BTS predation. However, at least 20 confirmed sightings have occurred between 1998 and 2021, and these individuals are presumed to be from populations in CNMI 
(Wiles et al. in prep)
. Most records are from northern Guam, specifically AAFB, with sightings also in Yigo, Agana Heights, Talo'fo'fo', and Mangilao. Most recent sightings include a male repeatedly seen at the DOAG compound in Mangilao from late 2018 through 2019, a male observed flying south of AAFB's back gate in February 2021, and another male photographed in that same area later that year.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, disease, and feral cats, monitor lizard predation.

## HABITAT(S):

The ground dove was observed occupying various habitats throughout Guam but principally prefers native limestone and ravine forests.

## GOAL:

To restore a population of ground doves in northern and southern Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To reintroduce a breeding population to Guam by translocating eggs, chicks, and/or adults from the Northern Mariana Islands to Guam and releasing birds into the wild in predator-controlled sites. To explore the potential for captive breeding this species. To determine if individuals observed in Guam are migrating or established individuals.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at potential release sites. 
, 1970-1999)
. This forest bird was extirpated in Guam in 1985 
(Wiles et al., 1995)
. Although they are not currently found in Guam, the Mariana fruit-dove still occurs in neighboring islands. A local resident reported a sighting on 2012 in the Anao area. A former conservation officer observed a totot in Yigo across Mobil gas station.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, competition, disease, and feral cat, feral dog, monitor lizard predation.

## HABITAT(S):

The fruit-doves were observed in all forested habitats including native limestone and ravine forest, except savanna complex and beach strands.

## GOAL:

To restore a population of fruit-doves in northern and southern Guam.
OBJECTIVES: To reintroduce a breeding population to Guam by translocating eggs, chicks, and/or adults from the Northern Mariana Islands to Guam and releasing birds into the wild in predator-controlled sites. To explore the potential for captive breeding this species.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at potential release sites. 

## Yayaguak

## Aerodramus bartschi

## Mariana swiftlet

Photo by Kalin Ocaña

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Mariana swiftlet is endemic to the islands of Guam, Rota, Aguigan, Tinian, and Saipan. Historically, this species was widely distributed and was described as the most common bird in Guam in surveys as far back as 1945 1 . Unfortunately, the swiftlets underwent a drastic decline during the late 1960s through the 1970s 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. In early 2000, DAWR received a report of a small number (less than 20 birds) in northern Guam, Janum Point (USFWS, 2020j). Overall population numbers were greatly reduced due to various threats described below and are still in decline. Most recently, a study from 2019 to 2023 observed yayaguak populations at Mahlac, Maemong, and Fachi caves with 500-600, 140-200, and 30 birds, respectively 
(Gorrensen et al., 2024)
.
THREATS: Habitat loss and degradation (loss of foraging habitat and cave disturbance), predation by the brown tree snake in Guam, nest damage by insects, and climate change are ongoing threats. Pesticide use may be a potential threat, but research is currently limited.

## HABITAT(S):

The swiftlet nests and roosts on cave walls and ceilings and forage for insects across various forest habitats in Guam.

## GOAL:

To restore and protect swiftlet populations in known caves.

## OBJECTIVES:

To expand the distribution of this population by recolonizing a cave in northern Guam by the translocation of swiftlets from the south colony. To maintain and enhance the population in the southern colony. To continue monitoring the population of swiftlets.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators and competition: Continue snake control measures at the Mahlac, Maemong, and Fachi caves, and implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake and other predator control to potential release sites. 

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Micronesian kingfisher is endemic to Guam. Historically they occurred throughout the island but were not as abundant in ravine and coastal forests of southern Guam 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. The kingfisher was last recorded in southern Guam by mid-1960 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. In 1981, a population occurred only in northern Guam with an estimated 3,023 individuals (American Zoo & Aquarium Association). In 1984, around 20 individuals were captured captive for breeding. By 1988, this species was no longer found in the wild in Guam 
(Wiles et al., 1995)
. Currently, 5 birds are in captivity in Guam and about 130 in various U.S mainland zoos.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, competition, disease, and feral cat, feral dog, monitor lizard predation.

## HABITAT(S):

This species once occupied all habitats throughout Guam, except savanna complex and wetlands. As this species nests in cavities, forests with Pisonia grandis trees are particularly important as it is a favored tree in which sihek excavates nest cavities.
GOAL: To restore a wild population in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To continue captive breeding efforts to maintain a sufficient supply of birds for upcoming releases.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake and feral cat (predator) control at potential release sites. • Habitat degradation and loss: Reduce deer and feral pig numbers at potential release sites and adjacent areas, develop and implement reforestation measures in Guam's conservation areas and national wildlife refuge overlay lands. • Small or extirpated populations: Continue participation in Micronesian kingfisher SSP, to continue captive breeding in Guam, and determine potential sites for Micronesian kingfisher re-introduction in Guam.

## Sihek

## Todiramphus cinnamominus

## Guam kingfisher

Photo by Martin Kastner

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Mariana crow is endemic to Guam and Rota. Historically, the crow was once abundant throughout Guam. In 1981, the crow population was restricted to parts of the northern plateau with an estimated 350 birds 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
. By 1999, fewer than 7 wild crows remained strictly on AAFB property 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. The last Mariana crow died in captivity in Guam in 2013. At present, there are no wild crows in Guam and the only remaining wild population exists on Rota.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of population decline has been the brown tree snake.
Other factors may include habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, competition, disease, predation, food resources, inbreeding, and senescence 3 . Restoration of the crow could be hampered by the loss of forest habitat required by this species.

## HABITAT(S):

The Mariana crow was found in most habitats in Guam, except the savanna. They were commonly observed in mature native forest, second growth and mixwoodlands 4 .

## GOAL:

To restore the crow population to 1984 levels in Guam of about 300 birds.
To collaborate efforts with CNMI-DFW to restore crow population on Rota.

## OBJECTIVES:

Work with the CNMI Fish and Wildlife to 1) translocate eggs or chicks from Rota to start a breeding program. Conduct research into Guam and 2) conduct research into implementing artificial incubation and hand rearing during breeding season on Rota.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Continue and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at release site and adjacent areas and assess brown tree snake threshold for crow nesting efforts. • Habitat degradation and loss: To manage deer and feral pig numbers on AAFB, develop and implement reforestation measures on conservation areas in Guam. • Small or extirpated populations: Work with CNMI to translocate Mariana crow eggs and chicks from Rota to Guam and continue to implement aviculture enhancement techniques to increase the size of the Guam' crow population and to supplement the Rota population.

## Ȧga

Corvus kubaryi

## Mariana crow

Photo by Martin Kastner

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

A subspecies endemic to Guam, Rota, Tinian, and Saipan, the Micronesian starling is a cavity nester. Historically, the starling was found across all habitats in Guam but most commonly in forested areas 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. A 1981 survey determined that the starling in Guam was one of the most abundant bird species at that time 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
. Starlings are predominantly found in the AAFB housing area and nearby on Mount Santa Rosa. As of 2019, the island-wide population was estimated at 1,400 individuals, with 93-96% residing at AAFB 
(Pollock et al., 2022)
. The såli population at AAFB had increased approximately by 15-fold since the early 1990s. 

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other sources of mortality may include: human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, competition, disease, ungulates, feral cats and dogs, monitor lizard predation.

## HABITAT(S):

The starling utilizes all habitat types, and although they were once most common in forested areas, today they are most frequently found in Guam's urban areas (could include more detail here if you choose).

## GOAL:

To increase population numbers and distribution in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To promote successful nesting in AAFB and Islan Dåno', and other designated sites. To translocate birds from Andersen Air Force Base and Islan Dåno' to snake-free areas.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Small and extirpated populations: Explore the feasibility in implementing nest boxes across central Guam to support increasing reproductive success and population health. 

## Såli

## Aplonis opaca guami

Micronesian starling

## Egigi

Myzomela rubratra saffordi

## Micronesian honeyeater

Photo by Kalin Ocaña

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

This subspecies of honeyeater is endemic to the Mariana Islands. Historically, this species was abundant throughout Guam occupying all habitats including urban areas 
(Jenkins, 1983)
. In 1980, the declining population was found only in the Northwest field in AAFB 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
. By 1986, the bird was extirpated in Guam 
(Wiles et al., 1995)
. Currently, the honeyeater is found in neighboring islands of Rota, Saipan, and Tinian where healthy populations exist.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include habitat loss and modification, human exploitation, typhoons, pesticides, competition, disease, and predation by feral cats, dogs, rats and monitor lizards.

## HABITAT(S):

A versatile species, the honeyeater was found in various habitat types in Guam including strand forest, scrub forest, limestone forest, urban and agroforest areas.

## GOAL:

To restore the populations of honeyeaters in Guam.
OBJECTIVES: Obtain information to better understand the biology of the species. Enhance habitat in Guam to allow for reintroduction. Translocate honeyeaters from Rota or Saipan.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake, feral cat and rodent control at potential release sites. • Knowledge gaps: Explore partnership with CNMI to establish a project to study the natural history of the honeyeater to encourage research opportunities and increase monitoring efforts of the current population in Mariana Islands. • Small or extirpated populations: Conduct research into translocation to Islan Dåno'.
Partner with CNMI to increase efforts to survey and monitor populations in Rota and/or Saipan.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Mariana common moorhen is an endemic subspecies to Guam, Saipan, Tinian and Pagan. Before 1951, the population was noticeably abundant in freshwater areas 
(Baker, 1951)
. The species has experienced population declines in recent years. Results from a 1981 survey showed that the moorhen was rare, with only 2 birds accounted for during the survey 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
. By 1999, moorhens were found throughout the southern and central parts of Guam, with population estimated at 100-125 birds (DAWR, 1999). Currently, the population numbers about 90 birds 
(Takano, 2003)
. Sightings of adult pairs with chicks are being made in wetland habitats throughout the island.
THREATS: Major causes of decline are habitat loss and modification. Other causes include competition, human disturbance, development and encroachment of invasive vegetation, and predation by BTS and stray animals.

## HABITAT(S):

The Mariana common moorhen is abundant in freshwater habitats both natural and manmade throughout Guam.

## GOAL:

To restore and maintain a population throughout Guam's wetlands.

## OBJECTIVES:

To maintain wetland habitat and prevent encroachment by development and invasive species.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at potential sites, as well as control for other predators. 

## Pulattat

## Gallinula chloropus guami

## Mariana common moorhen

Photo by Martin Kastner
Gå'ga' Karisu/ Ga' kaliso/Ga'karriso

## Acrocephalus hiwae

Nightingale reed warbler

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Nightingale reed warbler is an endemic subspecies to Guam, Saipan, and Almagan. Historically, this bird was believed to be rare in Guam and restricted to wetlands 
(Baker, 1951)
. By the 1960s, the Nightingale reed warbler was known to occur in four locales: Agana Swamp, Atantano River Marsh, Masso River Mouth, and an undescribed location in Agat 
(Reichel et al., 1992)
. After 1969, this bird species was no longer found in these areas 
(Reichel et al., 1992)
. Presently, the Nightingale reed warbler is considered extinct in Guam.

## THREATS:

The principal cause of decline and extinction in the wild is brown tree snake predation. Other causes that may have contributed to the decline may include habitat degradation and loss due to human disturbance and development and ungulate damage, along with wildfires, typhoons, pesticides, competition, and disease.

## HABITAT(S):

Restricted to adjacent areas in and near fresh and brackish water marshes.
GOAL: To introduce a viable population of the Saipan reed warbler in Guam as a replacement species.
OBJECTIVES: Obtain information to better understand the biology of the species. Enhance habitat in Guam to allow for reintroduction. Translocate reed warblers from CNMI to Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement area-wide brown tree snake control in the wetlands in Guam. 

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The white-tailed tropicbird is found throughout most of the Pacific Ocean, except for its easternmost areas, and is also present in the Atlantic Ocean. Surveys in 1945 indicated a nesting colony in crevices along the cliff walls on northern Guam. In 1985, a small breeding population nested along the cliff line at Two Lover's Point, Guam. Currently, no breeding pairs have been recorded. However, this species continues to be observed around Guam mainly as a visitor from the neighboring island of Rota.
THREATS: Predation by the brown tree snake is the most likely reason for the loss of the resident population in Guam.

## HABITAT(S):

Foraging from reef flat to open deep sea, nest in crevice located on cliff walls.

## GOAL:

To restore a nesting population to northern Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To maintain snake-free habitats along northern cliff walls.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at potential roost and nest sites. • Habitat degradation and loss: To prevent loss of historic cliffline roost sites.
• Small or extirpated populations: Collect information on this species distribution and abundance. Identify potential nest and roosting sites in Guam and respond appropriately to promote nest success in these areas.

## Utak or Fakpe

Phaethon lepturus

## White-tailed tropicbird

Photo by Martin Kastner

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Pacific reef heron occurs in many areas of the tropical Pacific. This species is an endemic shorebird to Guam. Historically, a breeding population occurred throughout the western coast (Orote Peninsula) to Islan Dånó 
(Baker, 1951)
. In 1981, sightings were common in northern Guam and several individuals were seen along the Islan Dånó coast 
(Engbring & Ramsey, 1984)
. Sightings of individuals still occur around a large portion of the island, on offshore islets, and on Islan Dåno' 
(Thompson, pers. comm.)
.
THREATS: Probable causes affecting this species include snake and rat predation and habitat loss and modification.

## HABITAT(S):

Pacific reef herons are found on reef flats and occupying coastal strands and islets.

## GOAL:

To determine the population and breeding status of reef herons in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status of the Guam population. To identify nesting areas and roost sites and implement appropriate protective measures.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Determine population status: Investigate current population status for reef herons in Guam and locate nest and/or roost sites. 

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Brown booby occurs throughout the tropical Western Pacific, south to Australia, and throughout the Atlantic. Historically, brown boobies were not seen abundantly throughout Micronesia 
(Baker, 1951)
. Twentyfive birds were seen roosting on Orote Point from June to August 1997 
(DAWR, 1997)
.
Recently, incidental sightings of individuals ranging from the Orote peninsula to the north, extending to the eastern and southeastern shores of Guam have been made. A breeding population was known from Guam but was extirpated 
(Wiles et al., 2003)
, most likely due to BTS.

## THREATS:

Predation by the brown tree snake and rats may have caused this species to be extirpated from Guam.

## HABITAT(S):

Brown boobies nest on the ground or in cliff walls, and forage along reef flats, and are usually seen in flight along steep rocky cliffs.

## GOAL:

To restore a population in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

Predator control in areas identified as historic nesting and roosting locations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide brown tree snake control at historical roost and nest sites. • Small or extirpated populations: Survey coastal areas for potential brown booby roost sites.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Over 40 species of migratory shorebirds representing Charadriidae and Scolopacidae are known to winter in Guam 
(Reichel & Glass, 1991;
Wiles et al., 2000;
Engilis & Naughton, 2004)
. While a great majority of migrants fly over Guam 
(Williams & Williams, 1988)
, many individuals overwinter here as the island provides critical habitat essential to their survival. Wetland, estuaries, tidal flats, grasslands, and uplands are important habitats for migrant shorebirds and resident shorebirds 
(Engilis & Naughton, 2004)
.
THREATS: Predators and loss of habitat remain significant factors in reducing the number of migrants in Guam.

## HABITAT(S):

Mud flats, reef flats, and open fields are important habitats for many of the shorebirds.
GOAL: Restore and maintain habitat for migratory shorebirds.

## OBJECTIVES:

1. Determine the abundance and distribution of shorebirds. 2. Reduce brown tree snakes and rats in areas used by the different species.
3. Prevent habitat loss.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Monthly shorebird surveys: Conduct monthly surveys for 2 years to determine the abundance and distribution of migratory shorebirds in Guam. • Predator control: Where appropriate control predation on shorebirds.
• Habitat degradation and loss: Reduce or prevent the loss the habitat important to shorebirds.

## Dulili/Kǻlalang

Migratory Shorebirds
Photo by Martin Kastner

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The snake-eyed skink was historically widespread throughout Guam and the Marianas Islands 
(Rodda et al., 1991)
. The last recorded specimen in Guam was in 1969 
(Rodda et al., 1995)
, where it is believed to be extirpated; however, the species still occurs in Islan Dånó. Anecdotal observations indicate that the abundance of C. poecilopleurus has increased in Islan Dånó after a successful rat eradication effort in 2009.

## THREATS:

Possible causes of decline may include predation by introduced species and competition with introduced skink species.

## HABITAT(S):

Observed near coastal strands, often found in loose, sandy soil and on the trunks of coconut palms.

## GOAL:

To restore populations to coastal areas of Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status of this species in Guam and Islan Dånó. To identify causes of decline and mitigate threats.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand control measures for brown tree snake, musk shrew, feral cat, and rats in areas with preferred habitat for C. poecilopleurus. • Habitat degradation and loss: Prevent loss of habitat in areas deemed important to the skinks. • Small or extirpated populations: Identify lizard population abundance where they occur and consider the potential of captive propagation for future releases in protected areas in Guam.
Guali'ek hålom tåno' 

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

An endemic species to Guam, Islan Dånó, Rota, Tinian, Guguan, Alamagan, Asuncion, and Maug 
(Rodda et al., 1991;
McCoid, 1995;
Vogt & Williams, 2004
). The species has not been recorded in Guam since 1945 and there was some concern that it was extirpated from Islan Dånó following two cyclones in the early 1990's; however, it was rediscovered in Dånó after a successful rat eradication effort in 2009 and is now frequently observed in certain parts of the islet 
(Richmond et al., 2022)
. Successful ungulate eradication led to a dramatic rebound of E. slevini on Sarigan in the late 1990s 
(Kessler, 2002)
.

## THREATS:

Possible causes to the decline of this species may include the following: predation by introduced species; habitat loss; competition with non-native skinks (e.g., curious skink (Carlia ailanpalai) in Guam); and habitat conversion due to the presence of ungulates.

## HABITAT(S):

Forested areas; found on the forest floors, in old fields, and low on tree trunks.

## GOAL:

To restore the species to Guam and improve numbers on Islan Dåno'.
OBJECTIVES: To supplement the current population in Guam and Islan Dåno' from other islands (CNMI).

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Continue area-wide brown tree snake, monitor lizard, musk shrew, feral cat, and rat control on Islan Dåno' to increase abundance. • Habitat degradation and loss: Prevent loss of habitats deemed important to the skinks. • Small or extirpated population: Quantify population density and consider the potential of captive propagation for future releases in protected sites in Guam. Explore possible ways of habitat expansion on Islan Dåno'.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Azure-tailed skink is known from southern Guam in the vicinity of the Geus River, which drains into the Cocos lagoon, but specimens were collected at the time of the observation 
(Rodda et al., 1991)
. No specimens have ever been collected or observed since then, and the population is presumed to be extirpated.
Currently, it occurs only on Islan Dåno' and Rota and is still found throughout the Casuarina forests and forest margins, but its status on both islands is poorly understood. The question of whether E. cyanura is native to the Mariana Islands has never been fully resolved; however, given its scant occurrence records combined with numerous documented cases of incidental transport to other Pacific Islands 
(Bruna et al., 1996;
Klein et al., 2016)
 is probably non-native to the region. This species is easily confused with the Blue-tailed skink (Emoia caeruleocauda).
THREATS: Possible causes of decline for this species may include the following: predation and competition with introduced skink species.

## HABITAT(S):

Reportedly prefers hot, dry, open areas especially those near the coast 
(Bruna et al., 1996)
.

## GOAL:

To determine whether the species may be native to Guam using molecular markers.

## OBJECTIVES:

Determine status in Rota and Islan Dåno'.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide introduced species control of curious skinks, shrews, and rats.

## Guali'ek hålom tåno'

Emoia cyanura

## Azure-tailed skink

Photo by Nathan Sablan

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Moth skink is found in much of the western Pacific. In the Mariana Islands, it is found only in Guam and possibly on Rota, as unverified sightings were reported 
(Vogt & Williams, 2004)
. A native species to the Marianas, this species was not common in Guam. However, four individuals were collected on a single day in 1986. In the early 1990's, the Moth skink was known to occur at three areas: Hilaan Point, Haputo Beach, and Achae Point along the northwestern coast 
(Wiles et al., 1995)
. However, 
Vogt and Williams (2004)
 indicate it occurs in central parts of Guam and abundant in the Naval Ordnance Annex area, but there is conflicting information on its status.

## THREATS:

Possible cause to the decline of this species may include predation and competition with introduced skink species.

## HABITAT(S):

Moth skinks use large tree trunks with loose bark in forested areas.

## GOAL:

To determine the status of this species and take appropriate actions.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey population of the Moth skink in Hilaan Point, Haputo Beach, Achae Point, Naval Ordnance and other potential areas in Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide introduced species control of curious skinks, shrew, and rats. • Habitat degradation and loss: Prevent loss of key-forested habitats deemed important to the skinks. • Small and extirpated populations: Determine the status of this species in Guam and implement a captive breeding program for future releases in protected areas in Guam.

## Guali'ek hålom tåno'

Ornithuroscincus noctua

## Moth skink

Photo by Jonathan Richmond

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Micronesian gecko is native to the Micronesia Islands, including Guam, Rota, Tinian, and Saipan. Historic reports indicated that this species was abundant during the pre-human period in the Marianas 
(Pregill, 1998)
. This gecko was present through the post-war period in Guam and in the southern Mariana Islands 
(Rodda et al., 1991)
. Currently, the Micronesian gecko is considered rare and information on this species is lacking.

## THREATS:

Predation by brown tree snake, Oceanic gecko (Gehrya oceanica), and feral cats is most likely the major reason for the loss of this species.

## HABITAT(S):

Knowledge of this species' habitat preferences is poorly known. Historic records indicated specimens were found and collected in limestone forest and beach strands.

## GOAL:

To locate and determine status for this species.

## OBJECTIVES:

To conduct population surveys in historic sites as well as in limestone and beach strand habitats.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide control for predatory species, such as rats, cats, and snakes. • Small and extirpated population: Work closely with private individual stakeholders and explore the feasibility of captive propagation for this species. • Habitat degradation and loss: Prevent loss of key habitats for this species and implement a captive breeding program for future releases in protected areas in Guam.

## Guali'ek hålom tåno'

Perochirus ateles
