## Ayuyu

Birgus latro

## Coconut crab

Photo by Else Demeulenaere

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The coconut crab (Birgus latro), otherwise known in CHamoru as ayuyu, is an arthropod with varied significance in the Marianas. The species is a primarily nocturnal animal due to human harvesting. However, as observed on the uninhabited islands of Guguan and Asuncion, they are active both day and night due to the absence of human activity 
(Amesbury, 1980)
. Currently there is no data for population numbers of coconut crab in Guam.

## THREATS:

The primary threat to ayuyu is human activity, including overharvesting, coastal developments altering their habitats, and climate change effects decreasing habitat availability 
(Cumberlidge et al., 2022, p. 11)
. In Guam, another threat is the invasive coconut rhinoceros beetle which damages the coconut, a major food source for the crab, affecting the species' survivability and overall population health.

## HABITAT(S): Limestone forests and beaches

GOAL: Protect and restore sustainable populations of ayuyu OBJECTIVES: Increase the abundance and distribution of ayuyu by reducing human harvesting pressure, protecting critical habitats, and addressing decline of the species' main food source

## ACTION PLAN:

• Protect populations and habitats: Enforce no-take regulations and establish protected areas in key coastal and limestone habitats.
• Safeguard food resources: Support efforts to control the coconut rhinoceros beetle (CRB) to maintain healthy coconut groves
• Monitor and manage threats: Track population trends and reduce predators
• Community engagement: Raise awareness and promote culturally grounded stewardship to reduce overharvesting.

## Hypolimnas octocula marianensis Mariana eight-spot butterfly

Photo by Matthew Putnam

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Mariana eight-spot butterfly likely once occurred throughout the Southern Mariana Islands, as suitable limestone forest habitat and host plants are present on Guam, Rota, Saipan, and Tinian 
(Rubinoff & Holland, 2018)
.
Although the butterfly was historically recorded on Saipan 
(Samson, 1986)
, it has not been observed there for decades. Despite the presence of its host plants, Procris pedunculata and Elatostema calcareum, on Rota and Tinian, the butterfly has not been seen on those islands either. Today, the Mariana eight-spot butterfly is known to survive in small numbers on Guam. Swezey collected one single female from a Hibiscus flower on October 17, 1936 
(Moore, 2013)
. Of the fourteen species of butterfly recorded by 
Swezey (1942)
, Hypolimnas octocula marianensis appears to be the rarest. THREATS: Degradation and loss of limestone habitat due to development, ungulates, competitive invasive plant species, and predation. Ungulates, including both deer and pigs, are identified as the most significant threat because they graze on, trample, and uproot the butterfly's host plants. In addition, they alter the structure of the limestone forests and introduce invasive plant species. Invasive plant species also further contribute to the degradation of limestone forests. Several species have been documented as threats to the Mariana eight-spot butterfly or its host plants. Nonnative ants and predatory wasps prey on butterfly eggs, while slugs feed on host plants, potentially reducing suitable habitat (USFWS, 2021). Some of these predators are native, suggesting the butterfly may have evolved with certain natural threats 
(Moore, 2013)
, while others are introduced and may pose new pressures.

## HABITAT(S):

Limestone forests where the two host plants, Procris pedunculata and Elatostema calcareum (tupun ayuyu), occur. 

## GOAL:

## Vagrans egistina

## Marianas wandering butterfly

No photo available HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: This butterfly species was described from Guam and is endemic to the Marianas. In 1932, Swezey found this species rare but widespread in Guam 
(Schreiner & Nafus, 1997)
. In the 1970's, several specimens were collected from Maytenus thompsonii. Not much is known about the status in Guam.

## THREATS:

Causes for this species to be rare includes habitat loss of the host plants by introduced plant species, and predation of caterpillars by an ichneumonid wasp.

## HABITAT(S):

Limestone forests where the host plant, Maytenus thompsonii, occurs.
GOAL: To establish a viable population of Vagrans egistina in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Maytenus thomponsii habitats in Guam, determine seasonal population dynamics of V. egistina, and in the laboratory, culture V. egistina. To survey for natural enemies of Vagrans and study its biology. To release laboratory reared butterflies in habitats containing Maytenus.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Habitat degradation and loss: Identify and map localities with Maytenus thompsonii, and reduce and prevent the development of limestone forest, and possible encroachment of invasive plants in these areas. • Small or extirpated population: Conduct monthly surveys to determine its seasonality. To cultivate caterpillars of this species and rear them to the adult stage in a parasite and predation free enclosures for propagation. Release lab reared adults in limestone habitats where the host plant is abundant especially in conservation areas.
• Caterpillar predation: Investigate efficacy of controlling ichneumonid wasp in areas caterpillars occur.

## Akaleha'

Partula gibba

## Humped tree snail

Photo by G. Curt Fiedler

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The humped tree snail is endemic to Guam and the Northern Mariana Islands. It was discovered and collected in 1819 during the Freycinet Uranie expedition in 1817-1819 
(Crampton, 1925)
. Upon its discovery, this tree snail was considered to be the most abundant in Guam. In 1920, Crampton collected 3,204 to determine geographic differences in shell patterns in 33 of his 39 sites studied 
(Crampton, 1925)
. No significant studies occurred until the late 1980's and early 1990's. In 1989, historical sites were re-evaluated to determine population status. Thirty-four of Crampton's 39 sites, plus 13 new sites, were studied. The Crampton's sites visited in 1989 supported no snails and one of the 13 new sites supported a small population 
(Hopper & Smith, 1992;
Kanehira, 1936)
. In a 1995 survey, two humped tree snail populations were found in coastal areas 
(Smith, 1995)
. In the same year, USFWS surveyed 15 sites on the Naval Magazine Ordnance area and found an abundance of ground shells but no living populations 
(Smith, 1995)
. Currently, there are only about 100 individuals at the southern basin and slope of Haputo (NCTS, HERA) (C. 
Fiedler, pers. comm.)
. In 2025, the humped tree snail was awarded protection under the U.S. Endangered Species Act.

## THREATS:

The primary cause of decline for this species was from the predation by the introduced triclad flatworm, Platydemus manokwari and the invasive rosy predator snail (Euglanina rosea). Other causes of decline may include intensive use of pesticides, extensive collecting of snails by naturalists and explorers, and habitat loss due to land clearing for agricultural/commercial development, wildland grass fires, ungulates, typhoon disturbance, and climate change.

## HABITAT:

The Mariana tree snail prefers cool, shaded forest habitats with high humidity. They occur on a variety of plants, including trees, shrubs, herbaceous plants, and even ferns, and are more likely to be found on leaves and less often on stems, branches, and tree trunks. Sometimes, they are found on the ground.

## GOAL:

To conserve and restore the population of humped tree snails in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status of the species and if feasible, implement a captive breeding program. To determine the feasibility of captive propagation and implement a program.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct survey for snails: Conduct survey of areas previously known to harbor snails. Closely monitor the remaining population at Haputo for changes and presence of flatworm predators. • Introduced predators: In selected sites, control predatory snails and flatworms.
• Habitat degradation and loss: Reduce deer and feral pig numbers around known colonies, develop and implement reforestation techniques in Guam's conservation areas and national wildlife refuge overlay lands. • Small or extirpated population: Work closely with stakeholders, CNMI Fish, and Wildlife Service to monitor populations within the Mariana Islands and possibly translocate snails to Guam to supplement the existing population. Explore the feasibility of captive propagation of this snail. Examine the feasibility of predator free enclosures within appropriate habitats for propagation of this species. • Genetic techniques: Use modern genetic techniques to determine genetic variation of Guam's remaining population and compare with this species elsewhere in the CNMI.

## Akaleha'

Partula radiolata

## Guam tree snail

Photo by G. Curt Fiedler

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The Guam tree snail is the only endemic tree snail to Guam. In 1819, French naturalists failed to recognize specimens from this species as distinct from other partulid species inhabiting Guam 
(Crampton, 1925)
. During a return visit in 1828, zoologists collected samples of partulids but did not indicate population density 
(Crampton, 1925)
. Surveys in 1989 and 1991 were conducted to evaluate the population size of this species. At both times, this species was considered the predominant tree snail 
(Fiedler, 2015)
. Currently, the Guam tree snail is known to be the most abundant representation of native tree snails in Guam, occurring in habitats along the western and southeastern coasts of Guam. In 2015, when only 26 populations were known, the Guam tree snail was awarded protection under the U.S. Endangered Species Act. Since then, over 50 populations, some with thousands of individuals 
(Fielder, Demeulenaere, & Putnam, 2019;
Fiedler, 2015)
, have been identified across the island, though local numbers fluctuate, and total population size remains unquantified (U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, 2020b).

## THREATS:

The primary cause of decline for this species was from the predation by the introduced triclad flatworm, Platydemus manokwari and the invasive rosy predator snail (Euglanina rosea). Other causes of decline may include intensive use of pesticides, extensive collecting of snails by naturalists and explorers, and habitat loss due to land clearing for agricultural/commercial development, wildland grass fires, ungulates, typhoon disturbance, and climate change.

## HABITAT:

The Guam tree snail prefers cool shaded forested areas with high humidity. They occur on a variety of plants, including trees, shrubs, herbaceous plants, and even ferns, and are more likely to be found on leaves and less often on stems, branches, and tree trunks. Sometimes, they are found on the ground.

## GOAL:

To conserve and restore the Guam tree snail population in Guam.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status of the Pacific tree snail and continue annual population surveys. To provide a protected sanctuary for breeding and longevity to occur.
To determine the feasibility of captive propagation and implement a program.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: To implement an area wide control for P. manokwari in areas where this species is known to occur. To test flatworm preference for this partulid species. To identify other predators and implement control measures. • Habitat degradation and loss: To control and monitor land clearing and prevent wildfires to preserve the native forest habitat for this species. Reforest with native flora and control ungulates in the habitat where snails are found will allow the required preservation of this species. • Small or extirpated population: To conduct surveys in Guam to determine current population size, structure, and distribution of this species To develop a captive breeding program for this snail to restore the population and eliminate any possible extinction to Guam. To perform needed dietary studies with the species since it is the most common and widespread native tree snail in Guam. To determine the degree of diversity within and between populations to assess the genetic connectivity amongst populations. To track individual snails using telemetry to determine their ability to disperse and their activity patterns.

## Akaleha'

## Samoana fragilis

Fragile tree snail Photo by G. Curt Fiedler

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

In the Mariana Islands, the Fragile tree snail was reported in Guam and Rota. Historically, it was considered rare when discovered in 1819 during the Freycinet Uranie expedition of 1817-1819 
(Crampton, 1925)
. 
Smith et al. (2008)
 mentions a population at Pugua Point (NCTS, HERA). Three other Guam populations have been confirmed but not in large numbers with the highest count of 140 individuals (C. 
Fiedler, pers. comm.)
. In 2015, the fragile tree snail was awarded protection under the U.S. Endangered Species Act. Currently, Guam has seven known populations. They are however small and narrowly dispersed populations, typically under 100 individuals, except at Haputo Ecological Reserve (U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, 2020c). A newly discovered population, however, was estimated to have around 1,900 individuals (Mizerek pers. comm., Guam HCP 2024).

## THREATS:

The primary cause of decline for this species was from the predation by the introduced triclad flatworm, Platydemus manokwari and the invasive rosy predator snail (Euglanina rosea). Other causes of decline may include intensive use of pesticides, extensive collecting of snails by naturalists and explorers, and habitat loss due to land clearing for agricultural/commercial development, wildland grass fires, ungulates, typhoon disturbance, and climate change.
HABITAT: Fragile tree snail prefers cool shaded forest habitats with high humidity. They occur on a variety of plants, including trees, shrubs, herbaceous plants, and even ferns, and are more likely to be found on leaves and less often on stems, branches, and tree trunks. Sometimes, they are found on the ground.

## GOAL:

To implement a SSP for all native snails in Guam and continue annual surveys.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status of the fragile snail and continue annual population surveys. To provide a protected sanctuary for breeding and longevity to occur. To determine the feasibility of captive propagation and implement a program.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Introduced predators: Implement and expand area-wide control of P. manokwari.
• Habitat degradation and loss: Reduce deer and feral pig populations at colony and adjacent areas, develop and implement reforestation techniques in Guam's conservation areas and national wildlife refuge overlay lands. • Small or extirpated population: To conduct surveys in Guam to determine current population size, structure, and locales of this species. To develop a captive breeding program for this snail to restore the population and eliminate any possible extinction to Guam. Work closely with stakeholders and CNMI Fish and Wildlife Service to monitor populations within the Mariana Islands. To determine if the populations in Guam and Rota are the same species. To translocate the species from Guam to Rota dependent on if the populations are the same.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Cycas micronesica, also known as fadang, is a culturally and ecologically important species 
(Demeulenaere et al., 2021)
. It was one of the most abundant trees, mostly found throughout undisturbed native limestone forests and coastal strands (USFWS, 2024a). The species also occurs in ravine forests and savannas. Fadang is endemic to Guam, Rota, Palau, and Yap. The 2003 introduction of the invasive Asian scale, Aulacaspis yasumatsui, caused significant mortality of the species. As a result, in 2015, fadang was listed as endangered under the ESA. Currently, there are an estimated 11 populations with 290,000 wild individuals in Guam (USFWS, 2024a).

## THREATS:

Invasive Aulacaspis scale, habitat loss, fire destruction, predation by ungulates, inadequate regulatory mechanisms.
HABITAT: Limestone areas, coastal areas, ravine forests, and savanna summits.

## GOAL:

To prevent extirpation of the fadang population.

## OBJECTIVES:

To prevent further loss of cycads due to the Asian cycad scale infestation by the application of appropriate treatments. To propagate seedlings in a scale-free enclosure to prevent further fadang loss.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveying: Survey historical range of C. micronesica. Monitor current populations to assess health and stability. Determine suitable locations for reintroductions.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Tabernaemontana rotensis is endemic to Guam and Rota. The species was first recorded in 1936. At one point, the population in Guam was believed to be composed of only one adult tree. Recent observations have expanded that number to well over thousands of individual plants found in various parts of northern and southern Guam (DAWR). The population on the northeast portion of Guam is the largest possibly because of the presence of the Micronesian Starling, an important seed disperser. As of 2020, there were a total of approximately 15,300 wild individuals from 8 populations in Guam and 6 populations in Rota (USFWS, 2020d). This number was an estimated decline of 5,000 individuals from the species' listing in 2015.
THREATS: Herbivory, insect infestations, and lack of seed dispersal are major factors limiting its abundance.

## HABITAT: Native limestone forests

GOAL: To maintain a healthy population of T. rotensis.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the status and distribution of this species.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Population Assessment: Continue surveys for T. rotensis in historical locations and potential suitable habitats. • Threat control: Develop appropriate mitigation strategies to limit effects of wildlife, ungulates, and other invasive species.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

An epiphyte orchid known from Guam, Rota, and Tinian. Dendrobium guamense is thought to have once been widely distributed across Guam's native forests (U.S. Fish and Wildlife, 2015). As of 2022, 26 populations inhabit the Mariana Islands, approximating more than 13,000 individuals (USFWS, 2024d). Within the islands, Guam has 14 populations, Rota has 9 populations, and Saipan, Tinian, and Aguiguan each have 1 population (USFWS, 2024d).

## THREATS:

Presently, major threats include habitat loss, destruction from agriculture, urban development, nonnative animals and plants, typhoons, wildland fires, and predation by nonnative slugs.
HABITAT: Grow on trees where it's moist.

## GOAL:

To restore Dendrobium populations and minimize threats to the species.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey the island and determine the density and distribution of the orchid.
To protect remaining areas harboring the orchid. To propagate this species and outplant in conservation areas in southern and northern Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveys and inventories: Continue to survey for Dendrobium guamense in historical locations and potentially suitable habitat that has not been surveyed. Regularly monitor known populations in order to determine population trends.

(Stone 1970
).

## THREATS:

Presently, major threats include habitat loss, destruction from agriculture, urban development, nonnative animals and plants, typhoons, combined with ungulate browsing.

To restore E. bryanii populations and minimize threats to the species.

To survey the island and determine the density and distribution of E. bryanii.
To protect remaining areas harboring the species. To propagate this species and out plant in conservation areas in southern and northern Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveys and inventories: Continue to assess the status of known occurrences of Eugenia bryanii in historical locations and potentially suitable habitat including an island wide survey.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Endemic to the Mariana Islands, T. guamense, was historically found in shade in very low positions on trees and shrubs all over the island 
(Raulerson & Rinehart, 1992)
. T. guamense was also reported in Rota, Tinian, and Aguiguan. In 2015, the species was listed as threatened under the Endangered Species Act (USFWS, 2020i). Currently, there are nearly 100,000 individuals in Guam and 4,886 individuals documented in Rota in 2023 
(Federal Register, 2014)
.

## THREATS:

Major threats include habitat loss, destruction from agriculture, urban development, nonnative animals and plants, typhoons, fires, herbivory by slugs, and low genetic representation.
HABITAT: Shady forest ecosystem GOAL: Rediscover and restore T. guamense populations and minimize threats to the species.

## OBJECTIVES:

To locate species habitat and determine the density and distribution of T. guamense. To protect remaining areas harboring species. To propagate this species and out plant in conservation areas in Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveys and inventory: Continue surveys for T. guamense in historical locations and potentially suitable habitats.

## Cocos nucifera Coconut

Photo by Lauren Gutierrez

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Native to the Mariana Islands, Cocos nucifera is found nearly everywhere in Guam 
(Stone, 1970)
. Historically, C. nucifera earned the title of the "Tree of Life" as every part of the tree was used for food, shelter, building materials, and other needs.
Leaves were used as material for thatched roofs and for various tools such as rick sacks, fans, brooms etc. The meat and water of the nuts are used as a major food source and ingredient in many dishes. Today, the leaves are primarily used for artistic weaving purposes and as a way for CHamoru people to reconnect with their indigenous heritage. The nuts are still used as a staple ingredient in many food dishes. According to a 2013 survey conducted by the United States Department of Agriculture, there were an estimated 1.46 million coconut trees throughout the island (USDA, 2013). Another survey was conducted in 2024 (UOG, 2024) but the data is not yet publicly available.

## THREATS:

Major threats include the coconut rhinoceros beetle (Oryctes rhinoceros) and the Tinangaja disease.
HABITAT: Majority of habitats in Guam, including limestone forests, savannas, coastal, ravines GOAL: Protect and sustain healthy coconut trees in Guam OBJECTIVES: Conserve and restore key coconut strands, reduce impacts from pests, and promote community stewardship and traditional uses.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct targeted surveys to identify healthy coconut strands and prioritize conservation or restoration areas. • Implement integrated pest management (IPM) strategies, including CRB trapping, removal of infested trees, and sanitation to reduce breeding sites • Replant healthy coconut trees in areas such as parks and village recreation sites • Collaborate with cultural practitioners to promote traditional uses and knowledge related to the coconut • Develop and share outreach materials relating to conservation of the coconut tree and impacts of the CRB and Tinangaja disease.
• Establish long-term monitoring approaches to track health, pest presence, and regeneration success.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Native medicinal plants were historically abundant throughout Guam in all habitats. Over time, the health of these plants has been significantly reduced due to various threats such as land conversion and invasive species. Many species that were once widely distributed are now locally rare or fragmented in distribution. Comprehensive population assessments are limited or completely lacking and in general lack formal monitoring data. However, anecdotal reports and field observations suggest continuing declines in both wild abundance and regeneration potential for key species. Particular species of concern include, but are not limited to: HABITAT: Research habitat preferences using historical documents, herbarium records, and traditional ecological knowledge. Identify key ecological requirements (e.g., soil type, elevation, associated species).

## Species

## GOAL:

Gather and compile comprehensive information on the historic and current status, habitat requirements, and threats for each target species.

## OBJECTIVES:

• Identify and review available literature, herbarium specimens, and archival materials related to each species. • Consult with local experts, botanists, and traditional knowledge holders to gather ecological and historical insights. • Conduct targeted field surveys to locate remnant populations and document current distribution. • Develop a standardized data collection protocol to record life history traits and habitat conditions. • Prioritize species for conservation action based on rarity, threat level, and ecological and cultural importance.

## ACTION PLAN:

To be developed based on data gathered through the objectives. The action plan could include habitat protection, propagation research, ex-situ conservation, or restoration strategies.

## Lesser-known plants HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Dokduk is endemic to the Mariana Islands.

Historically it is a dominant tree in Guam's forest 
(Fosberg, 1960)
. A census conducted during 1989-1999 showed a decline because of introduced ungulates 
(Wiles, 2005)
. The fruits are an important highly nutritious food source for the fanihi and people 
(Ragone, 1997;
Safford, 1905;
& Demeulenaere et al., 2021)
.
THREATS: Dokduk faces difficulty reproducing as fallen fruits are consumed by introduced Philippine deer (Cervus mariannus) and feral pigs (Sus scrofa).
HABITAT: Dokduk is a common species in both limestone and volcanic substrate 
(Wiles, 2005)
.
GOAL: Monitor the species' population trends. Increase natural recruitment.
OBJECTIVES: Establish a monitoring program. Control ungulates to increase natural recruitment.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Education: Include dokduk as a high value tree for fauna and people in reforestation programs and community outreach. • Invasive species: Ungulate control to increase natural recruitment.
• Cultivation: Grow seedlings in nursery and outplant in the wild.

Balaenoptera edeni

## Bryde's Whale

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); listed in Appendix 1 of CITES; population information not available. In the early 1980s, Bryde's whales' abundance in the Western North Pacific was estimated to be 22,000 to 24,000 (NOAA, 2015b). Bryde's whale pods ranging from one to four individuals were encountered in the Mariana Archipelago 18 times during small boat surveys from 2010 to 2019 
(Hill et al., 2020)
.

## THREATS:

Seismic operations; collision with large vessels; entanglement in fishing gear (A female with a calf was observed approximately 250 km west of Guam with a line tightly wrapped overtop of her blow hole causing deep abrasions" 
(Hill et al., 2020
)); defense operations; pollution, including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea, leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues; over-fishing of prey species, particularly commercial species such as anchovy.

## HABITAT(S):

Temperate to tropical waters, both oceanic and inshore, bounded by latitudes 40°N and S, or the 20° isotherm. Sightings during most months suggest that Bryde's whales spend time in the Mariana Archipelago year-round 
(Hill et al., 2020)
.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); listed in Appendix 1 of CITES; and listed as Endangered under the Endangered Species Act. All stocks of Sei whales within U.S. waters are estimated to be below 600 individuals (USNMFS, 2020).

## THREATS:

Seismic operations; collision with large vessels; entanglement in fishing gear; pollution, including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills, and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea, leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues, though less serious for species rarely feeding in low latitudes; over-harvest for scientific studies, inbreeding, and illegal whaling.

## HABITAT(S):

Oceanic, undertaking long migrations between warm water breeding grounds and colder water feeding grounds, between 40°N and S. While Sei whales have been spotted near Guam, their activities or behavior in these waters remain uncertain. They have an unpredictable distribution pattern, unlike other large whale species, in which they may be seen in one area and then not return for years or decades 
(NOAA, 2024)
.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

## Bayena

Balaenoptera borealis

## Sei Whale

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); listed in Appendix 1 of CITES; population information not available. Humpback whales included in the Western Northern Pacific DPS are listed as endangered under the Endangered Species Act, but minimal data on these populations, especially around the Mariana archipelago, exists 
(Young et al., 2023)
. Preliminary abundance estimates between 2015 and 2019 report a yearly average of 61 (CV=0.21) individuals within the waters of Saipan, Tinian, and Aguijan 
(Hill et al., 2020)
.

## THREATS:

Harassment from whale watching and research vessels/aircraft, pleasure craft, swimmers and divers; coastal seismic operations; defense operations; collision with large vessels; entanglement in fishing gear/shark nets; pollution, including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea, leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues, though less serious for species rarely feeding in low latitudes.

## HABITAT(S):

Antarctic pelagic, in summer; temperate-subtropical/tropical coastal in winter.
The Western Northern Pacific DPS includes two units: the Philippines / Okinawa unit and the Marianas / Ogasawara unit. Sightings of newborn calves during small boat surveys confirm the Mariana Islands as a breeding location (USNMFS, 2020).

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals and to advance the recovery of the global species and to protect its habitats.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.
To minimize human-caused injury and death.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters. and eek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species. • Monitor potential impacts from military training and exercises.

## Humpback Whale

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); listed in Appendix II of CITES; population information not available. Estimating population size is difficult due to similar appearances between species. Nine Cuvier's beaked whales have been found stranded on Guam, Rota, or Saipan since 2007 
(Hill et al., 2020)
.

## THREATS:

Possible entanglement in drift nets and other nets set, lost or discarded in international waters; competition from expanding commercial fisheries, especially on pelagic squids; pollution leading to accumulation of toxic substances in body tissues, military operations including sonar.

## HABITAT(S):

Tropical (ca 22-32°C) to sub-polar (ca 1-8°C) deep oceanic waters.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

## Ziphius cavirostris

Cuvier's Beaked Whale HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); listed in Appendix I of CITES; and listed as endangered under the Endangered Species Act. population information not available. Due to their wide range and overlap with other stocks, population estimates are not reliable. However, there have been numerous sightings reported on Guam.
THREATS: Entanglement in fishing equipment, ship strikes, decline in prey populations, pollution, including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea, leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues, though less serious for species rarely feeding in low latitudes (USNMFS. Office of Protected Resources, 2020).,

## HABITAT(S):

Pelagic, offshore, in deep water; population centers found in temperate/tropical waters where breeding/nursing schools, and groups of young males, occur; concentrations found where seabed rises steeply from great depth (e.g. on 'steep-to' coasts and near oceanic islands, probably associated with concentrations of major food, deep-sea cephalopods, in areas of upwelling). Only adult males, usually solitary or in small loose groups, found in cold waters (i.e. north or south of ca 45°S).

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

Physeter macrocephalus

## Sperm Whale

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972); not listed in either Appendix I or II of CITES; population information not available due to their low sighting rate and difficulty to be identified.

## THREATS:

Seismic operations; collision with large vessels; entanglement in fishing gear; defense operations, pollution, including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea, leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues.

## HABITAT(S):

Oceanic, apart from colder waters, but more coastal than pygmy sperm whales.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

## Bayena

## Kogia breviceps

Pygmy Sperm Whale 

## THREATS:

Possible illegal and incidental catches in gill-nets and small numbers taken in the purse-seine nets of the eastern tropical Pacific; captured in low numbers in small cetacean fisheries in several places, including Japan, Indonesia and Sri Lanka; entanglement in drift-nets and in lost or discarded netting; pollution (including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea) leading to bio-accumulation of toxic substances in body tissues.

## HABITAT(S):

Pelagic and oceanic. Primarily tropical and subtropical areas but can be found in temperate waters. Inhabits warm waters (usually >25°C), mainly equatorial. Generally, in upwelling areas.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species. THREATS: Is long-lived and a top predator, making it highly susceptible to accumulating high levels of heavy metals and organochlorines; reduction of food resources by overfishing of prey species; entanglement in driftnets and in lost or discarded netting.

## Bayena

## HABITAT(S):

Oceanic, pelagic and neritic, in warm and cold waters; may be more common in cold, deep waters; often seen near seal colonies. Annual long-distance migration to the tropics for breeding/molting 
(Pitman et al., 2020)
.

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species. THREATS: Entanglement in drift nets and other nets set, lost or discarded in international waters; competition from expanding commercial fisheries; pollution leading to accumulation of toxic substances in body tissues. Individuals may be exposed to naval training, underwater U.S. Navy explosive sites, fisheries interactions, and tourism activities.

## Green Sea Turtle

Photo by David Burdick

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The green sea turtle is the most common sea turtle species found in the waters of Guam 
(Kolinski, 2005)
 THREATS: Predation on eggs and hatchlings by introduced species, fibropapilloma disease (a recent threat documented by DAWR biologists in 2020), human take of eggs and adults (mainly for food), loss of marine and nesting habitat, taken as bycatch, oil spills, development, public use of beaches, light pollution, marine debris, and climate change.

## HABITAT(S):

Adult green sea turtles feed primarily on seagrass and algae. They can be seen throughout the island in all marine habitats.

## GOAL:

Restore population to a sustainable level to meet recovery thresholds under the Guam and US Endangered Species Act.
OBJECTIVES: Assess population structure and size, assess, monitor, and protect habitat, and reduce illegal take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• THREATS: Predation on eggs and hatchlings by introduced species, human take of eggs and adults (for food and shells), loss of habitat, taken as by-catch, oil spills, development, light pollution, and marine debris, and climate change.

## HABITAT(S):

Hawksbill turtles feed primarily on sponges. They can be seen in all nearshore habitat types (NMFS-USFWS, 2013). Apra Harbor is an important foraging area.

## GOAL:

Restore population to a sustainable level to meet recovery thresholds under the Guam and US Endangered Species Lists.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess population structure and size, assess, monitor, and protect habitat, reduce illegal take through outreach and education. 

## ACTION PLAN:

## Tanguisson

## Cheilinus undulatus

Humphead Wrasse or Napoleon Wrasse HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Heavily fished by SCUBA equipped spear fishers, nighttime spear fishers. Adults are rarely seen. Juveniles are more frequently seen, especially in the marine preserves.
THREATS: Human take of adults and juveniles, habitat degradation

## HABITAT(S):

Adults inhabit outer reef slopes and channel slopes. Juveniles occur in coral-rich areas of lagoon reefs, particularly among thickets of staghorn Acropora thickets 
(Myers, 1999)
. Juveniles are found in Cocos Lagoon, Tumon Bay Marine Preserve, and Piti Bomb Holes Preserve (DAWR unpublished data).
GOAL: Sustain population to facilitate de-listing from CITES and to prevent listing on Endangered Species List.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: To provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting slow growth rate, to include in fisheries management plans.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Popular food fish represent up to 30% of the coastal fishery. This family is heavily fished using a number of techniques including gill nets and spearfishing.
THREATS: Overfishing, loss of habitat. Species of particular management need based on vulnerable life histories and patterns of decline are: Naso unicornis, Naso lituratus, Acanthurus xanthopterus, Acanthurus blochii.

## HABITAT(S):

Varied, adults generally in lagoons and seaward reefs. Juveniles often in protected, turbid, inshore waters 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage populations for sustainable fishing.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, and manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. 

## HABITAT(S):

Varied, adults generally in lagoons and seaward reefs although some occur exclusively in reef flat and shallow lagoon areas dominated by seagrass or dense algae beds. Juveniles are often in protected, inshore waters including reef flats and lagoons, but may also be found on seaward reefs 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage populations for sustainable fishing.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, and manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting biology. To include in fisheries management plans.

Siganidae Rabbitfish HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Popular food fish, this family is heavily fished using a number of techniques including talaya, hook and line, gill net, and spear. Juveniles recruit in large schools and form a culturally important fishery. Juveniles are taken with tekin (gill net), and talaya (throw net), while adults are more frequently taken with hook and line or spear.
THREATS: Overfishing, loss of habitat. Most commonly harvested species include Siganus spinus, Siganus argenteus, and Siganus punctatus.

## HABITAT(S):

Reef flats, lagoons and bays, and fore reefs 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage populations for sustainable fishing.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, manage take through outreach and education.

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: To provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting biology. To include fisheries management plans.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Popular food fish taken with hook and line, spear, and gillnet. Some species are shunned due to chronic incidence or perception of ciguatera toxin.

## THREATS:

Overfishing, loss of habitat HABITAT(S): Varied, adults generally in lagoons and seaward reefs although some occur as well as reef flat and shallow lagoon areas, including seagrass, mangroves, and shallow sandy areas. Some species are the principal fisheries species taken from deep reefs (>500 ft.). Juveniles often in protected, inshore waters including reef flats and lagoons. Some species are frequently found in brackish or freshwater habitats 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage populations for sustainable fishing.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. Adults may be found on reef flats, as well as seaward reefs, up to 300 feet deep 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage populations for sustainable fishing.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: To provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting rapid growth rate. To include in fisheries management plans.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Not traditionally taken as a food fish by the Chamorro population. Sometimes taken for food by people from other islands. Butterflyfish are some of the most popular species taken for the home aquarium trade, although many species do poorly in captivity.

## THREATS:

Overfishing by the aquarium trade, loss of habitat

## HABITAT(S):

Many species are closely associated with coral reefs. Most are found on reef flats or seaward reef slopes. Some species are obligate coral feeders, and as such, are indicators of a reef's health. Some species are found on deep reefs (> 200 feet) 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage and maintain current population status.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: To provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting biology. To include in fisheries management plans.

## Ababang

Pomacanthidae Angelfish

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The stocky hawkfish (Cirrhitus pinnulatus) is often fished for food. Other species are taken incidentally by hook and line, but for the most part this is not a targeted family. Some species are very popular in the home aquarium trade and are at risk of over collection.
THREATS: Overfishing (C. pinnulatus), aquarium collection, loss of habitat. Collection of these species for the aquarium trade often leads to the destruction of the corals they inhabit.

## HABITAT(S):

Hawkfish are closely associated with coral reefs. Members of this family inhabit lagoons, reef fronts, submarine terraces, and seaward reefs up to at least 100m. Some species within this family are found exclusively with certain coral species such as antler coral (Pocillopora eydouxi) and Acropora species 
(Myers, 1999)
.

## GOAL:

To manage and maintain current population status.

THREATS: Overfishing, loss of habitat.

.

To develop educational materials highlighting slow growth rate. To include in fisheries management plans.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Manta rays are listed as vulnerable to extinction on the IUCN Red List and are included in Appendix II of both CITES and the Convention on Migratory Species (CMS). From 2008 to 2024, 64 individual manta rays have been documented in Guam, representing a small population compared to other groups, which are estimated to consist of about 300 individuals. Guam's reef manta rays target surgeonfish spawning aggregations to feed on fish spawn, marking the first documentation of this behavior 
(Hartup et al., 2013)
. One juvenile manta ray in August 2019 was documented off Rota Harbor and then recorded in Guam in June 2020 suggesting a mixing of populations and need for shared management.

## THREATS:

Reef manta rays are targeted for their gill rakers, commonly used in traditional medicine in Western Asia. They also get caught as bycatch in commercial fishing nets. The popularity of scuba diving spots that attract manta rays often leads to unmanaged and irresponsible behavior. Manta rays are ram-jet filter feeders and planktivorous, and both their feeding patterns and abundance are affected by climate change, which in turn impacts reef manta rays.

## HABITAT(S):

Reef manta rays are found in tropical and subtropical regions of the Indian and western Pacific Oceans. In Guam, these rays are found in coral reefs, sea banks, and epipelagic and mesopelagic zones with time-at-depth spent repetitively at 200-350 meters with deepest depths below 600 meters.

## GOAL:

Maintain population for delisting from CITES and to avoid listing on the Endangered Species List.

## OBJECTIVE:

To manage and increase population.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess the current population structure and size.
• Gather baseline data on population size, demographics (age and sex), and growth rates.
• Understand the horizontal and vertical movements of reef manta rays, along with genetic and distribution information regarding Guam's population. • Collaborate with CNMI on the reef manta ray population.

## Hafula'

## Mobula alfredi Reef Manta Ray

Photo by Julie Hartup

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The oceanic manta ray is currently listed as endangered due to declining populations from fishing and habitat loss. The oceanic manta ray has been recognized for its significant role in marine ecosystems, but its population is declining due to overfishing, by-catch, and environmental changes. The giant manta ray's status is "Endangered" due to a significant population decline (50-79% in the past three generations) and their continued threats potentially furthering the decline. The giant manta ray is also protected by the Convention on Migratory Species (CMS) ( 
2011
) and the Convention on the International Trade in Endangered Species (CITES) ( 
2013
), which are global conservation treaties. In Guam, rays are protected under Bill No. 44-31, which makes it unlawful for anyone to possess, sell, offer for sale, take, purchase, barter, transport, export, import, trade, or distribute ray parts. Each manta ray features a unique spot pattern on its ventral side that allows for tracking through photogrammetry.
THREATS: Overfishing, bycatch, environmental impacts HABITAT(S): Oceanic manta rays inhabit mostly warm, tropical and sub-tropical oceans and are often found in some coastal regions, seamounts and banks, and open ocean environments.
GOAL: Document and maintain population for delisting as an endangered species and from CITES.
OBJECTIVES: Document, manage and increase population. Increase awareness, reduce threats, and promote sustainable ecotourism diving practices.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Develop and enforce regulations to minimize fishing impacts, promote ecotourism, and educate the public about manta ray conservation. • Implement protective measures, conduct research, and engage local communities in conservation efforts. • Gather baseline data on population size, demographics (age and sex), and growth rates.
• Understand the horizontal and vertical movements of oceanic manta rays, along with genetic and distribution information regarding Guam's population. • Collaborate with CNMI on the oceanic manta ray population.
• Implement protective measures, conduct research, and engage local communities in conservation efforts.

## Hafula'

## Mobula birostris

Oceanic manta ray THREATS: Primary threats are bycatch in commercial fisheries, where these rays are unintentionally caught in gillnets and trawl nets, leading to injury or death. Additionally, targeted fishing for their gill plates, which are highly valued in traditional medicine and as a delicacy in some Asian cultures, exacerbates their vulnerability. Habitat degradation, including the destruction of coastal ecosystems such as coral reefs and mangroves, further impacts their feeding and breeding grounds. Climate change also poses a serious threat, as rising ocean temperatures and acidification can disrupt their migratory patterns and affect the availability of prey.

## HABITAT(S):

The sicklefin devil ray (Mobula taracapana) inhabits warm, tropical, and subtropical oceans, primarily residing in coastal and open ocean environments. These rays are often found in areas with abundant plankton, their primary food source, which they filter from the water using their specialized gill rakers. They tend to frequent continental shelves and can be seen near coral reefs, where they may engage in feeding and social behaviors. Sicklefin devil rays are known for their migratory patterns, traveling long distances in search of food and suitable breeding grounds. They are typically found at varying depths, from the surface to deeper waters, depending on the availability of prey and environmental conditions. The health of their habitat is crucial for their survival, as changes in water quality, temperature, and habitat structure can significantly impact their populations and overall ecosystem dynamics.
GOAL: Document and maintain population for delisting from CITES and to avoid listing on the United States Endangered Species List.
OBJECTIVES: Document, manage, and increase the population while increasing awareness and reducing threats.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Develop and enforce regulations to minimise fishing impacts, promote ecotourism, and educate the public about Mobula taracapan conservation. • Implement protective measures, conduct research, and engage local communities in conservation efforts. • Gather baseline data on population size, demographics (age and sex), and growth rates of Mobula taracapan. • Understand their horizontal and vertical movements, as well as genetic and distribution information, while collaborating with CNMI on implementing protective measures, conducting research, and engaging local communities in conservation efforts.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Coral cover, primarily composed of zooxanthellate Scleractinian species, on Guam's shallow coral reefs was historically estimated around 50-60% across the island's reef zones 
(NOAA, 2008)
. However, repeated disturbances such as mass bleaching events have led to drastic declines with average live coral cover at 25% or less 
(NOAA, 2021)
.
THREATS: Climate change, land-based sources of pollution (sedimentation, pollution), overharvesting of herbivorous fish populations, crown-of-thorns outbreaks, recreational misuse, and disease.

## HABITAT(S):

Highly variable, reef flats to up to 100m deep.

## GOAL:

To maintain or expand Guam's hard coral resources.

## OBJECTIVES:

Conduct assessments and monitoring of hard coral resources in Guam's waters. Take actions to limit impacts to coral resources (passive restoration) and continue active coral restoration for areas where removal of negative impacts is not sufficient to restore the ecosystem services of corals.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: Support the Guam Long-term Coral Reef Monitoring Program's existing in situ monitoring of coral percent cover, species composition, and rugosity within the High Priority Reef Areas and at island-wide bleaching response and recovery sites. The GLTMP no longer conducts coral colony size and condition surveys due to capacity constraints, but the re-introduction of these surveys could occur if program capacity is expanded.
• Protect habitat: Implement a variety of watershed restoration actions aimed at improving the quality of water draining into coastal waters. Compile existing water quality data, conduct a gaps analysis, and support the development and implementation of nearshore coral reeffocused water quality monitoring efforts. Provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE

## Cho'cho'

Hard Coral

## Scleractinia

Photo by David Burdick and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. Develop and effectively implement the regulation of the harvest of reef fishes, with particular attention on herbivorous fish species. Support the regular monitoring and control of the crown of thorns sea star. • Reduce take through outreach and education: Educate residents on the need for re-vegetation, raise public awareness on the impacts of wildland arson, and educate recreational users. • Active coral restoration: Outplant stony coral in areas where passive restoration is insufficient to restore ecosystem services provided by coral reefs. The University of Guam Marine Lab has active coral restoration for staghorn species including: Acropora pulchra, 
A. muricata, A. acuminata, A. Aspera, A. teres, A. austera, A. virigata, A. vaughani

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Populations have expanded in some areas in the last thirty years but seem to be declining at present. These corals serve as an important habitat for a number of marine invertebrates.

## THREATS:

Land-based sources of pollution (sedimentation, chemicals), climate change, destruction by shell collectors, and disease.

## HABITAT(S):

Habitats vary greatly from reef flats to over 100 m (330 ft.). Malalcyonacean soft corals, such as Sclerophytum (formerly Sinularia) spp. are conspicuous and abundant Guam's on shallow reef flats, while neptheids and "gorgonians" are typically found below 25 m Guam's seaward slopes.

## GOAL:

To maintain or expand Guam's soft coral resources.
OBJECTIVES: Take actions to limit impacts to coral resources and conduct assessments and monitoring of soft coral resources in Guam's waters.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: conduct in situ surveys of Guam's coral resources including percent cover, species composition, and disease. • Protect habitat: Implement a variety of watershed restoration actions aimed at improving the quality of water draining into coastal waters. Compile existing water quality data, conduct a gaps analysis, and support the development and implementation of nearshore coral reeffocused water quality monitoring efforts. Provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: educate local residents on need for revegetation, raise public awareness of the impacts of wildland arson, and educate recreational users.

## Acropora globiceps

Photo by David Burdick HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: : Within U.S. waters, Acropora globiceps occurs in Guam, CNMI, American Samoa, and the Pacific Remote Island Areas. The species was identified in 1999 and again in 2014 in Guam's waters 
(NOAA, 2015)
. This species was listed as threatened under the U.S. Endangered Species Act in 2014.

## THREATS:

Ocean warming, disease, ocean acidification, sedimentation plumes, land-based source pollution, recreational misuse (anchoring, stepping on corals, etc.).

## HABITAT(S):

Upper reef slopes, reef flats, and adjacent habitats in depths ranging from 0 to 20 m, with a preference for clear, exposed reef areas and depths < 8 m.
GOAL: 1. To determine locations and abundance in Guam. 2. To develop and implement adaptive management actions to prevent further decline of species.
OBJECTIVES: Take actions to limit impacts to coral resources and conduct assessments and monitoring of hard coral resources in Guam's waters.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: Support the Guam Long-term Coral Reef Monitoring Program's existing in situ monitoring of coral percent cover, species composition, and rugosity within the High Priority Reef Areas and at island-wide bleaching response and recovery sites. The GLTMP no longer conducts coral colony size and condition surveys due to capacity constraints, but the re-introduction of these surveys could occur if program capacity is expanded. • Protect habitat: Implement a variety of watershed restoration actions aimed at improving the quality of water draining into coastal waters. Compile existing water quality data, conduct a gaps analysis, and support the development and implementation of nearshore coral reeffocused water quality monitoring efforts. Provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. Develop and effectively implement the regulation of the harvest of reef fishes, with particular attention on herbivorous fish species. Support the regular monitoring and control of the crown of thorns sea star.
• Reduce take through outreach and education: To educate residents on the need for revegetation, raise public awareness on the impacts of wildland arson, and educate recreational users.

## Actinopyga varianas

Surf red fish

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The surf red fish (Actinopyga echinites), sometimes also called the Pacific white-spotted sea cucumber, has been historically abundant in Guam 
(Kerr et al. 2017)
. It is also one of the most commercially valuable species 
(Purcell et al. 2012)
 and may be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as Data Deficient and hence may be vulnerable or threatened globally.

## THREATS: Overfishing

## HABITAT(S):

The wave-battered outer margins of reef flats GOAL: Sustain population and to prevent listing on Endangered Species List.

## OBJECTIVES:

To manage and increase population to allow sustainable fishing ACTION PLAN: Reduce take through change in regulations.

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: The black teatfish (Holothuria whitmaei), sometimes also called the teated sea cucumber, has been fairly abundant in Guam

). However, it is also one of the most commercially valuable species 
(Purcell et al. 2012
) and appears to be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as endangered.

## THREATS: Overfishing

) and appears to be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as data deficient and so may already be vulnerable or endangered.

.

## Hima

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: To provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce harvesting of giant clams through outreach and education: To develop educational materials highlighting slow growth rate. To include in fisheries management plans.

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Seagrass beds are widely distributed across Guam's reef flats, where they play an important ecological role. However, these resources have experienced significant declines due to disturbances such as dredging, increased sedimentation, and marine heatwave events. A satellite image analysis conducted by NOAA in 2005 estimated that seagrass covered approximately 3 km² of Guam's nearshore reef environment 
(NCCOS, 2005)
. Since that assessment, the total seagrass area has decreased by an estimated 22% 
(LaRoche et al., 2018)
.
THREATS: Sedimentation, marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, outbreaks of nuisance species, habitat loss, disease, and recreational impacts.

## HABITAT(S):

Reef flats and lagoons.
GOAL: Protect, maintain, and where possible, expand the extent and improve the health of Guam's seagrass resources.
OBJECTIVES: Evaluate seagrass density and monitor long-term trends in their distribution across Guam's nearshore marine environments.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess population structure and distribution: Conduct field surveys of Guam's seagrass resources to document species composition, population structure, density, and spatial extent. • Protect critical habitat: Restore vegetation in watersheds that drain into coastal bays with significant seagrass beds. Construct designated walkways to minimize physical disturbance in areas with high seagrass cover. • Minimize anthropogenic impacts through outreach and education: Raise community awareness about the importance of watershed re-vegetation and the impacts of wildland arson on coastal ecosystems. Educate recreational users on the ecological value of seagrass beds, particularly their role in supporting fisheries.

## Lo'u/Chå'guan tåsi

Enhalus acoroides, Halodule uninervis Halophila nipponica

## Sea Grasses

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Rhodoliths are free-living, calcifying red algae that can form dense aggregations (beds). They are internationally recognized as unique habitats and biodiversity hotspots. Despite their ecological importance, rhodolith communities remain understudied in tropical regions, and little is known about their diversity and ecological roles in Guam. Under degraded water quality or following major disturbances, some species of calcifying red algae can outcompete corals and opportunistically overgrow reef surfaces. The documented species diversity of calcifying red algae in Guam is among the highest in the world, and the level of endemism for this group in the Mariana Islands is expected to be high 
(Mills et al., 2022)
. Species of calcifying red algae are the deepest recorded plant life on Earth. Guam's mesophotic and rariphotic communities support extensive cover of these algae. These algae play a key role in reef carbonate budgets, particularly in areas where coral cover is low or has declined 
(Schils, 2023)
.
THREATS: Marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, habitat loss, and disease.

## HABITAT(S):

Habitat builders across reef flats, crests, and forereefs, from shallow to mesophotic depths.

## GOAL:

Advance understanding of how the unique diversity of Guam's calcifying red algae contributes to the persistence of healthy, carbonate-accreting tropical reef systems.

## OBJECTIVES:

Investigating the species diversity and ecological function of calcifying red algae in relation to shifts in herbivore populations and coral cover.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Characterize the species diversity of calcifying red algae across reef habitats and identify indicator species that reflect reef health. Use these indicators to monitor ecological condition and guide management efforts. • Preserve healthy calcifying red algal communities to support reef resilience and recovery after disturbance. • Prioritize the conservation of rare and potentially endemic calcifying red algal species unique to the Mariana Islands. • Document the ecological and biological diversity of rhodolith communities, which remain largely unexplored in the region.

## Lo'u/Acho' tåsi

## Rhodophyta: Corallinophycidae, Peyssonneliales

Calcifying Red Algae HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Red algae represent the most diverse group of seaweeds in Guam. Historically, Guam's seaweed flora was believed to consist mainly of widespread Indo-Pacific species. However, recent phylogenetic studies have revealed that many species have limited geographic distributions, with a significant number likely endemic to Guam and the Mariana Islands. In recent decades, large-scale outbreaks of nuisance seaweeds have become an increasing concern in the Mariana Islands. In Guam, massive blooms of Chaetomorpha vieillardii began in 2012 and were later observed on Saipan. In 2024, Saipan experienced a significant outbreak of a Palisada species, which altered reef community composition. These blooms tend to outcompete native seaweeds and other benthic organisms, disrupting the ecological balance of reef systems across the region. While short-term shifts in seaweed communities also occur naturally following tropical storms, these nuisance blooms represent a more persistent and damaging disturbance. Genetic studies indicate that several nonnative seaweed species have become naturalized in Guam's waters.
THREATS: Marine heatwaves, water quality degradation, habitat loss, community-level shifts driven by outbreaks of nuisance species, introduction and spread of invasive species, and overharvesting.

## HABITAT(S):

Lagoons, reef flats, patch reefs, forereefs, and estuarine environments.

## GOAL:

Preserve the unique diversity of Guam's seaweed communities as a sustainable resource for people and their vital role in supporting healthy reef ecosystems.
OBJECTIVES: Generate a DNA-barcoded reference library of Guam's native seaweed flora and document the presence of introduced species. Conduct a resource assessment of ecologically and culturally important seaweeds to (1) support the conservation of key reef ecosystem functions and
(2) identify candidate species for sustainable mariculture in Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Collaborate with local and federal government stakeholders to establish a comprehensive baseline of Guam's seaweed diversity, supporting efforts to detect and prevent the introduction of non-native species and to identify indicator species for ecological disturbances and environmental change. Given its role as the main entry point for

## Lo'u/Chå'guan tåsi

Rhodophyta (red seaweeds), Ulvophyceae (green seaweeds), Phaeophyceae (brown seaweeds)
Seaweeds; Marine Macroalgae maritime traffic and the ongoing military buildup, Apra Harbor should be prioritized for intensive surveys. • In parallel, engage with the public and local stakeholders to explore the potential for sustainable seaweed mariculture in Guam, including a valorization assessment to identify species that are both biologically suitable for cultivation and economically viable for sustaining local livelihoods.
There are many threats affecting Guam's native flora and fauna species. Some of the major threats to native species include but are not limited to:
1. Loss of key habitat types 2. Degradation of key habitat types because of invasive animals and plants 3. Limited information on many native species 4. Habitat and species management issues 5. Wildfire 6. Typhoons 7. Climate change
The loss of key habitat types across Guam has been driven primarily by military expansion, urban development, infrastructure construction, and large-scale quarrying operations. These activities have led to habitat fragmentation, reduced native forest cover, and the direct removal of critical ecosystems such as limestone forests. Beyond habitat loss, Guam's ecosystems are experiencing ongoing degradation due to invasive animals and plants. Species like the brown tree snake (Boiga irregularis), feral cats (Felis catus), rats (Rattus spp.), invasive amphibians, and ants prey upon or compete with native species, while invasive plants outcompete native vegetation and alter ecosystem structure and composition. For example, high densities of feral cats in the Munitions Storage Area, AAFB have hampered reintroduction efforts of the Guam rail (Gallirallus owstoni) (DAWR unpublished notes).
Compounding these threats is the limited biological information available for many of Guam's native species. This lack of data hinders effective conservation planning and limits understanding of species' specific habitat needs, population trends, and threats.
Habitat and species management challenges further complicate recovery efforts. For instance, reintroduction projects, such as those involving the endangered Guam rail (Gallirallus owstoni), face setbacks from persistent invasive predator populations and insufficient habitat quality in managed areas.
In addition to these direct threats, wildfires contribute to the degradation of grassland and forest ecosystems, promoting the spread of fire-adapted invasive plants and further reducing habitat suitability for native species. Typhoons, which regularly impact Guam, cause both immediate habitat destruction and long-term ecological shifts, compounding pressures on already vulnerable species.
Finally, climate change acts as an escalating threat. Rising temperatures, shifts in rainfall patterns, and increased storm intensity are expected to further degrade habitats, exacerbate species declines, and complicate conservation efforts.
Habitat loss and degradation, invasive species impacts, knowledge gaps, management challenges, wildfire, typhoons, and climate change form an interconnected series of threats that must be addressed to ensure the survival of Guam's native species and ecosystems.

## Loss of Key Habitats

Guam's rapid military expansion, housing development, and infrastructure projects are driving significant loss and fragmentation of native habitats. Military construction, residential clearing, and quarrying for limestone are reducing forest cover and threatening species recovery, particularly for endangered species and SGCN.

## Military expansion and training

The Department of Defense has proposed the relocation of Marines from Japan to Guam in the next 10 years 
(DON, 2010)
. With expansion comes a high demand for training and testing exercises 
(DON, 2015)
. Combined, military activities on the island will become more challenging for conservation due to more areas being cleared for construction (i.e. live-firing range), minimizing hectares needed for species recovery on GNWR Overlay lands.

## Development

A result of the Military build-up happening is the high demand for housing. DAWR had reviewed, at average 20 clearing and grading permits per month, for residential use (DAWR, unpublished data). Many private landowners have developed their properties to meet the housing demands, creating more fragmented forested areas, especially in northern Guam (DAWR, unpublished data). As a result of development, the total amount of forested areas will decline. This will become more of a challenge for delisting and downlisting ESA species, and SGCN in Guam.
For instance, in 2015, approximately 20 acres of the Cotal Conservation Area were cleared for a wind turbine pilot project intended to test the potential for renewable energy production for 30-40 homes. Although this project explored sustainable energy solutions, its location within a designated Conservation Area underscores the critical need to strengthen legal protections for conservation lands-an effort that remains both vital and challenging. The goal is to identify, secure, and protect additional conservation areas suitable for species recovery and management.

## Quarrying

Increased military and urban development, along with infrastructure expansion, are major drivers of habitat loss in Guam. Beyond land clearing for residential, commercial, and military purposes, large-scale quarrying for limestone aggregate, critical for military construction and infrastructure projects while also keeping up with the local demand for housing and businesses, has removed entire ridges and limestone formations. Pinnacle karren, a unique limestone habitat that supports many endangered species and SGCN, is especially threatened through this quarrying activity.

## Degradation of Key Habitats

The degradation of Guam's native habitats has been significantly accelerated by invasive animals and plants. Non-native species introduced intentionally or accidentally have disrupted ecological processes, outcompeted native species, and altered the structure and function of critical habitats. Invasive plants such as Leucaena leucocephala (tangantangan) and Antigonon leptopus (chain of love) rapidly colonize disturbed areas, forming dense thickets that outcompete native vegetation and hinder forest regeneration. At the same time, invasive animals, including feral pigs, deer, cause direct damage to native flora through browsing, trampling, and predation, while contributing to soil erosion and habitat degradation. The brown tree snake (Boiga irregularis) whipped out most of Guam's native forest birds, disrupting natural seed generation. Together, these invasive species degrade key habitat types such as limestone and ravine forests, savannas, wetlands, and coastal habitats threatening Guam's biodiversity and undermining conservation efforts aimed at protecting endangered species and SGCN. Addressing invasive species impacts is therefore central to restoring and sustaining Guam's remaining native ecosystems.

## Invasive Animals

Brown Tree Snake
In the 1940s, the brown tree snake was accidentally introduced to Guam after World War II 
(Rodda et al. 1992
, Richmond et al. 2015)
. The ecological damage caused by this snake to the island environment is well documented 
(Savidge 1987
, Jaffe 1994
, Conry 1988
, 
Engbring and Fritts 1988
, Wiles 1987a
, Rodda and Fritts 1992
, Rodda et al. 1992a
, 1997)
. The snake is largely responsible for the extirpation or decline of the island's resident bird species 
(Savidge 1987
, see Appendix 6). In 2020, the brown tree snake was also discovered on Islan Dåno'. This nocturnal and arboreal snake is capable of taking advantage of a variety of habitats and prey species 
(Rodda et al. 1992b
(Rodda et al. , 1999b))
. Only two of 12 native forest bird species continue to persist in the wild in Guam: the Micronesian starling (Aplonis opaca guami) and the Mariana swiftlet (Aerodramus vanikorensis bartschi), along with two wetland species of birds, the Mariana common moorhen and the yellow bittern. Historically, the resident species (Guam's native, non-migratory forest bird species) were found throughout the forests of Guam 
(Seale 1901
, Baker 1947
, Marshall 1949
, Jenkins 1983
