). The Micronesian megapode (Megapodius laperouse), Mariana mallard (Anas platyrhyncos), and White-browed rail (Poliomnas cenereus) were extirpated prior to the introduction of the brown tree snake 
(Engbring and Fritts 1988)
.

## Rats

Rats are a serious problem throughout the Pacific 
(Atkinson 1985
, Milberg and Tyrberg 1993
, Buden 2000)
. They are a major hurdle to recovery of many avian species because of their predation of eggs and nestlings and are known to be predators of other small native animals such as lizards, insects, and snails. Rats can be found in all habitat types but are most abundant in urban areas in Guam. In forested areas of Guam, rats are not as abundant as on islands in the CNMI due to predation by the brown tree snake.

## Amphibians

Guam historically had no native amphibian species, but the introduction of non-native amphibians has led to rapid and widespread colonization across the island. These introduced populations pose multiple threats to Guam's native species, both through direct predation on native species and by serving as an additional food source for the invasive brown tree snake.
Since 1937, at least 13 frog and toad species have been introduced to Guam. Of these, at least five have established breeding populations: the cane toad (Rhinella marina, formerly Bufo marinus), the spotted narrow-mouthed frog (Kaloula pulchra), the greenhouse frog (Eleutherodactylus planirostris), the green tree frog (Litoria falax), and Gunther's amoy frog (Rana guentheri).
One species of particular concern is the coqui frog (Eleutherodactylus coqui). This species is especially problematic because of its direct metamorphosis from egg to frog and it is not restricted to aquatic breeding sites. The coqui's high-pitched call, reaching up to 90 decibels, also adds to its nuisance. In April 2004, two live coqui frogs were found-one at a resort in central Guam and another at a plant nursery in Tumon. These discoveries led to changes in import regulations for live plants from Hawaii. However, despite stricter rules, two more coqui frogs were later intercepted, one associated with a shipment of pallets from the Big Island of Hawaii and another with imported bromeliads. The establishment of coqui frog populations in Hawaii and Florida has led to millions of dollars in losses for the horticulture industry, declines in real estate values, and impacts on tourism 
(Bliss 2015)
. Although Guam remains free of an established coqui population, the island is considered highly vulnerable to similar pathways of introduction, especially through imported live plants from infested areas. Recognizing this risk, Guam initiated a public awareness campaign in 2004, along with frog call surveys and inspections aimed at early detection.
Other amphibian introductions have had negative consequences as well. Greenhouse frogs were discovered in Guam in 2003, and surveys suggest they now inhabit nearly the entire island. The barking frog (Eleutherodactylus spp.), likely introduced via aquaculture, has become a nuisance due to its loud call. Residents in southern Guam have reported disturbances from frog choruses and, in some cases, resorted to using caustic chemicals to manage them. The cane toad was intentionally introduced in 1937 as a biological control agent for insect pests and the garden slug (Laevicaulis alte) and is now found island wide. More recently, two additional species, the crabeating frog (Fejervarya cancrivora) and the Asian grass frog (Fejervarya limnocharis), have expanded their ranges on Guam. Notably, the crab-eating frog can tolerate brackish and seawater, potentially threatening native crab populations in coastal habitats. Beyond the nuisance factor, these amphibians impact Guam's native ecosystems by indirectly supporting the brown tree snake population and consuming native fauna.

## Insects

Guam's native cycad (Cycas mariannensis) population has declined substantially dueto the introduction of the Asian cycad scale, Aulacaspis yasumatsui. The infestation causes the tree to lose its leaves. Cycads are only able to replace leaves twice before dying, usually occurring after a few months of being infected with the insect. There is a species of moth (Chilades pandava) that lays its eggs on the new emerging leaves of cycad. The larvae consume the emerging leaves of the cycad and further exacerbates the problem of recovery for this species.
Discovered in 2007, the coconut rhinoceros beetle (Oryctes rhinoceros) is a large scarab beetle that feeds on coconut and other palms. The adult beetles bore holes into the crowns of coconut palms and feed on the sap, leading to the palm tree's mortality. Larvae are mostly found on composing soil, which makes it difficult to maintain a compost pile without harboring beetles. In southern Guam, a boring hole from an adult beetle was also observed on the native Pandanus.
(Tibbatts, pers. comm.) Since its discovery, adult beetles have affected nearly all the coconut palms on the island.
In November 2011, the little fire ant (Wasmannia auropunctata), LFA, was discovered in a landfill in northern Guam 
(Raymundo 2012)
. Since then, about 11 sites have been identified harboring LFA in Guam. Currently, efforts to inspect sites critical to SGCN are needed due to the continuous transporting of green waste between sites. LFA is known to swarm and prey on insects, snails, lizards, geckos, birds, and all animals it encounters.

## Fish

In addition to habitat alteration, the introduction of non-native fish species poses a significant threat to Guam's native freshwater fish populations (Table 
10
). At least eleven non-native fish species have become established on the island, arriving through various pathways. Some, such as snakeheads and catfish, were introduced for aquaculture. Others, like guppies and swordtails, were released or escaped from home aquaria. Still others, including Gambusia and tilapia (Oreochromis mossambicus and Tilapia zilli), were deliberately introduced for biological pest control.
These invasive fishes compete with native species for food and habitat or prey on them directly. Native predators such as eels, flagtails, and sleepers, rely on small fish and aquatic invertebrates, while native omnivores (e.g., Awaous sp., Stenogobius sp., and Sicyopus sp.) feed on both plant and animal matter. Native herbivorous genera like Stiphodon and Sicyopterus graze on algae covering river substrates. Although species like Awaous and Anguilla are broadly distributed across the Indo-Pacific, others including Stiphodon, Stenogobius, and Sicyopus, have restricted ranges and are only known from the southern Mariana Islands.
While some native species can navigate reservoirs and reproduce, their larvae require access to the ocean soon after hatching to survive. Young fish trapped upstream of dams are unlikely to reach the sea, leading to reduced recruitment. Studies by DAWR have shown that rivers flowing into dammed reservoirs support lower biodiversity compared to undammed rivers both within and outside the Fena watershed.
Although data remained limited, anecdotal evidence gathered by DAWR suggests that nonnative fishes are affecting native species. In rivers with dense non-native populations, native fish tend to be less abundant, or exhibit altered distributions. For example, tilapia competes with native species for food, while predators such as Cichla, Channa, and Clarias, feed on native fish and invertebrates.
Despite these concerns, there are currently no active control measures targeting established nonnative fish. Guam DAWR and USFWS do regulate new species introductions through a permitting system and shipment inspections. However, more comprehensive efforts, including early detection, prevention, and enforcement, are needed to protect native freshwater biodiversity from the continued threat of invasive species.

## Aquatic Snails

Guam has at least seven species of non-native freshwater snails (Table 
10
). It is unknown if the introduced snails have had an impact on native flora or fauna, though herbivorous species probably fed on native aquatic plants. The introduced snails can pose a human health risk. Two introduced species (Pomacea and Pila) are known vectors for schistosome parasites of humans. Additionally, some species are agricultural pests in other parts of Asia, especially where aquatic crops, such as rice, are grown.

## Other Invertebrates

Introduced snails have been a major factor in the reduction of native snail populations, along with predation by flatworms (Platydemus manokwari) 
(Hopper and Smith, 1992)

## Invasive Plants

Invasive plant species have profoundly altered the composition, structure, and resilience of Guam's native ecosystems, both terrestrial and aquatic.
During the Forest Inventory Analysis (Lazaro et al. 2013), the top five invasive plants species were Vitex parviflora, Leucaena leucocephala, Heterospathe elata, Antigonon leptopus, Mikania micrantha, Averrhoa bilimbi, and Epipremnum pinnatum. Spathodea campanulata and Tabebuia heterophylla are two other invasive tree species taking over conservation sites in Guam. These species contribute to habitat degradation by outcompeting native plants, altering nutrient cycles, and changing fire regimes. Control and removal efforts are ongoing but challenging due to their aggressive growth and widespread distribution.
Vitex parviflora (Agalondi) is invading both limestone and ravine forests across Guam, outcompeting native tree species. Its rapid growth and ability to form dense stands prevent the regeneration of native plants, altering forest structure and reducing biodiversity, further threatening habitat quality for native wildlife. While its spread is widespread, certain areas, especially in the northwest of Guam and southern Guam are showing more severe infestations. Without targeted management, Vitex parviflora is likely to continue displacing native species and altering key forest ecosystems across the island.
Following World War II, large-scale reseedling efforts by the US military introduced tangantangan (Leucaena leucocephala) across Guam's barren landscape. This fast-growing tree species continues to dominate many areas, outcompeting native species and slowly changing soil conditions that make reforestation and habitat restoration increasingly challenging (FSRD unpublished data).
After major disturbances, such as typhoons or wildfire, fast-reproducing invasive species quickly colonize open areas and prevent slower-growing native species from re-establishing. For example, the palma brava (Heterospathe elata) has taken over many of Guam's southern ravines and valleys after brush fires, where chiute (Cerbera dilatata), and lalaha (Pouteria ovovata) were historically dominant 
(Stone 1970)
. Over time, these invasions may lead to a complete replacement of native vegetation and loss of critical habitats.
Vines such as chain of love (Antigonon leptopus, kadena) and dodder (Cuscuta campestris, mai'ågas) are extremely aggressive, smothering native trees and shrubs by blocking sunlight and competing for water.
Guam's freshwater ecosystems face similar threats from invasive non-native aquatic plants (Table 
10
). Many of these species were introduced through various pathways, including pet trade, aquaculture, and deliberate introductions by government entities.
While some species, such as duckweed, water lettuce, and water hyacinth remain confined to one or two water bodies, water lilies are widespread across ponds in private yards and public parks throughout Guam. The most problematic aquatic plant is Hydrilla, which is widespread in southern Guam. The plant clogs pumps at the Ugum Water Treatment Plant, requiring costly annual maintenance (Ron Topasna, pers. comm.).
Beyond invasive aquatic plants, freshwater ecosystems also face serious threats from human activities. Unregulated clearing of riparian vegetation raises water temperatures, while incidents of chemical contamination degrade water quality, reduce habitat suitability, and contribute to harmful algal blooms. Illegal practices, such as river bleaching can also result in large-scale mortality of native aquatic life, drastically delaying recovery of these species.
Together, these factors contribute significantly to the ongoing transformation of Guam's landscapes and waterways, threatening the island's unique biodiversity and reducing the ecological services these habitats provide.

## Disease and Pathogens

Disease and other pathogens can have a serious effect on the natural resources on an island. In today's global community, borders are minimized and diseases that affect humans and animals can be transmitted in a matter of hours via commercial aircraft. Guam is a hub for all goods and services moving to and from the US mainland, Asia, and the rest of Oceania. This makes Guam and other areas of Micronesia susceptible to diseases and pathogens that affect wildlife. West Nile Virus (WNV) is a major concern for all wildlife managers as well as public health officials. On the US mainland where the virus is widespread, the mortality of crows infected is 100% 
(Komar et al. 2003)
. If this virus is established in Guam or in the CNMI, the effects may be disastrous for the Mariana crow and other forest species already in jeopardy. 

## Habitat and Species Management Issues

The loss of key habitats throughout the island through development is a problem that will have long-term effects on the restoration of SGCN. The major factors exacerbating habitat loss are ungulates, stochastic events, and the introduction of invasive plant and animal species. Nothing can be done to control stochastic events such as typhoons and wildfires. However, we can slow or halt the loss of habitat by controlling the other factors. Probably the most difficult and laborintensive factor to control is damage by feral ungulates. These animals alter the forest composition by browsing on the vegetation (deer and water buffalo) or by uprooting it (pigs).
Most of the native flora is preferred by grazers because they do not possess the chemical and physical defenses found in many introduced plants. This form of artificial selection allows invasive plant species to dominate. One exception to this trend is in some areas of Pati Pt. where the native Ochrosia mariannensis, a plant not favored by deer, have established monotypic stands (DAWR unpublished notes).
Ungulates have been in Guam since the arrival of the first European inhabitants of the island 
(Wiles et al. 1999)
. They have become an important part of the culture for the native Chamorro people of Guam. The three ungulate species common in the wild are the Philippine deer, feral pig, and Asiatic water buffalo (Bubalus bubalis). A variety of habitats provide for these animals including native forest, wetlands, shoreline, grasslands, and a mosaic of other habitat types 
(Stone 1970)
. Management practices need to address the need for conservation action while fulfilling cultural needs 
(Wiles et al. 1999
).

## Wildland Fire

Based upon our Guam Forestry and Soil Resources Division Fire Data and Mapping Efforts from 1979 to present, Guam has averaged roughly 730 wildfires a year, which is an annual average of approximately 4% of our island area burned annually (Pacific Fire Exchange, 2025) (Figure 
4
; Figure 
5
). 4% is a huge percentage when considering the size of our island compared to the Continental U.S (DOAG-FSRD, 2025). Fire plays a large role in altering the native forest vegetation cover in Guam. Due to the moist conditions, fire is not a prevalent natural process.
Certain fires are deliberately ignited by poachers, an unlawful activity unrelated to traditional or cultural practices, in an effort to attract wildlife to the new vegetation that emerges after a burn. Human-induced fires have impacted Guam for several millennia and continue to this day, leading to altered vegetation cover in savannas and grasslands that have since adapted to the prevailing fire regime. These altered vegetation types contribute to increased erosion following fires. Burned areas can yield 4 to 5 times more sediment than unburned savannas, and savannas/grasslands generally produce more sediment than densely forested areas.
Wildfire is a primary disturbance that affects forest and watershed health and is a keystone issue that is linked with other identified stakeholder issues. Fire is a non-native disturbance and directly interferes with the establishment and expansion of native forests, threatens standing forests, leads to accelerated erosion, the delivery of sediment pollution to surface waters and domestic water supplies, and contributes to the decline of the coral reef system. The goal of the SWAP is to align with the mission of Guam Forestry to support terrestrial habitats, reclaim badlands, and restore native forests. Rehabilitation of native forests is a necessary step in the management and recovery of species of concern. 

## Typhoons

Typhoons occur frequently in Guam, averaging about six storm events per year, with most either making landfall or passing directly over the island. Only a small number of storms veer away before reaching Guam. The Pacific typhoon season typically begins in April, peaks in August and September with the most severe storms and extends through November. This frequent and intense disturbance regime plays a significant role in shaping Guam's habitats and affecting its native species.
Typhoons can cause widespread defoliation, canopy breakage, and uprooting of trees, particularly in forests already compromised by invasive species. Invasive trees and shrubs dominating many of Guam's secondary forests, such as Vitex parviflora and Spathodea campanulata are not adapted to withstand typhoon-force winds. These species often exhibit irregular and delayed post-typhoon sprouting, disrupting typical successional recovery. They typically get uprooted faster than the native species, which are more adapted to typhoons.
Furthermore, the structural integrity of forests in Guam is compromised by ungulates, such as feral pigs and deer, which trample seedlings and compact soils. This results in reduced recruitment of native plants after storms, leaving disturbed areas vulnerable to further colonization by invasives.
The loss of native seed-dispersing birds, largely due to predation by the invasive brown tree snake, exacerbates these effects. Without adequate seed dispersal, native plant species struggle to recolonize cleared areas, slowing post-typhoon forest regeneration.
Species of Greatest Conservation Need, including endemic plants, forest birds, and freshwater species, and marine species face compounded threats following typhoon events:
• Endemic and native plants suffer from direct damage and from competition with rapidly colonizing invasives.
• Land snails and native reptiles may experience population declines due to habitat loss from canopy opening and soil erosion.
• For freshwater and marine species, increased sedimentation and debris influx into streams, wetlands, and reef ecosystems after typhoons degrade aquatic habitat quality.
• Native birds and fruit bats, already in critically low numbers, are vulnerable to direct may lose remaining habitat due to canopy destruction and food availability.
While typhoons are natural ecological processes in Guam, their effects are now intensified due to altered forest composition, introduced herbivores, and the loss of critical ecological interactions such as seed dispersal. This hinders the natural resilience of Guam's habitats and puts additional stress on SGCN already facing steep declines.

## Climate change

Climate change is significantly altering Guam's natural ecosystems with effects being seen in all environments: terrestrial, marine, and freshwater. The island's unique biodiversity is threatened by rising temperatures, increasingly extreme weather events, sea-level rise, coral reef loss, challenges to food security, threats to public health, and more 
(Keener et al., 2012)
.
Changing precipitation patterns and warming temperatures are affecting the range of plant and animal species, pushing them into smaller or less suitable habitats with limited access to resources. Native forests are extremely vulnerable to invasive species, storm damage, and anthropogenic effects such as wildfires.
Warming sea temperatures lead to coral bleaching, weakening reef structures and reducing habitat for fish and other marine species. Overharvesting of food fishes decreases the overall numbers and health of our fish species. More frequent flooding and higher levels of erosion affect coastal communities and threaten nesting beaches for sea turtles. Ocean acidification further disrupts coral health and growth, negatively impacting biodiversity that communities depend on for food and livelihoods.
Climate change further impacts mangrove systems and reduces the availability of freshwater through saltwater contamination of aquifers. Freshwater resources are at risk from altered watershed dynamics and reduced rainfall, impacting the wetlands and streams that support our aquatic plants and animals.
In supporting our SGCN, it is vital that we work towards long-term ecosystem resilience with proactive management, vulnerability assessments, habitat restoration and management, invasive species control, and climate-smart conservation planning.
The conservation actions outlined in this chapter were selected based on their feasibility and effectiveness in minimizing or eliminating the threats affecting the Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and their habitats. The actions presented here follow a multi-faceted approach, organized by ecosystems, threats, and goals so that multiple species benefit from each action. While the species-specific actions listed in the SGCN species accounts and Table 
11
 address individual needs, they are closely aligned with the broader conservation strategies in this chapter. This approach reduces redundancy in addressing issues species-by-species and enables more holistic and efficient conservation planning. These broader actions also reflect the urgency and immediacy of interventions needed for certain SGCN.
These action priorities are not fixed; rather, they serve as adaptable placeholders that reflect current knowledge and feasibility. A more detailed prioritization framework will require further analysis using Best Management Practices provided by the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) prior to the next SWAP revision. In the meantime, the most urgent conservation priority for Guam is securing legal protection for remaining suitable habitats. Without such protection, many of these lands are at risk of being repurposed for development, especially given the island's ongoing economic challenges.

## Ecosystem-Based Actions

Terrestrial The need to assess the state of habitats throughout the island is vital to understanding the rehabilitation needs of these areas before any reintroduction of native fauna can occur. This assessment will inform necessary actions, such as improved reforestation strategies and predator and ungulate control. The following objectives will be pursued to assess the current state of forests in Guam, with the aim of enhancing degraded habitat or maintaining native forests, as necessary:
• Develop plans to improve habitats in conservation areas, including, but not limited to, reforestation, fire prevention, and invasive vegetation control.
• Assist the Forestry and Soil Resources Division in developing forest recovery plans.
• Formulate a list of SGCN flora with cultural and ecological value to be used in propagation and outplanting.
• Improve man-made habitat at Masso reservoir for wetland species.
• Establish mechanisms to protect native forests from urban development.
• Develop sustainable harvest regulations for medicinal plants and timber within the Conservation Lands.
• Conduct research on SGCN to assess habitat needs and potential climate change impacts on their viability. Marine Guam's coral reef ecosystems are among the most biologically diverse in the world, supporting over 5,000 marine species, many of which are vital to local fisheries and essential fishing practices. Early aquatic management by DAWR focused on individual fishery species but by the mid-1980s, a significant decline in reef fish abundance prompted a shift toward ecosystem-based management efforts. This transition led to the establishment of Guam's marine preserves and the adoption of the Guam Coral Reef Initiative. Through partnerships like the Guam Coral Reef Initiative Coordinating Committee (GRICC), DAWR has helped identify major threats to reef ecosystems, such as coral diseases, unsustainable fishing, and land-based pollution.
To continue these efforts in addressing these challenges, the following objectives will be implemented:
• Conduct research monitoring reef fish, habitats, climate impacts, and management effectiveness, with emphasis on heavily fished species and coral reef fisheries.
• Assess the effectiveness of marine preserves and continue long-term coral and watershed monitoring.
• Develop tools to support restoration in tropical coastal areas and explore adaptive management for seagrass and mangrove habitats.
• Research climate change trends and impacts on coral reef ecosystems and fisheries.
• Strengthen enforcement of Guam's fishery laws, marine preserve regulations, and stormwater permitting.
• Enact laws to hold parties accountable for coral reef damage and pollution.
• Create and implement sustainable policies for managing nearshore fisheries.
• Develop fishing licenses and fee systems for commercial and non-commercial use.
• Implement management actions to protect marine Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SOGCN).
• Continue and expand coral reef restoration efforts, including nursery enhancement and outplanting in Tumon Bay and other priority reef areas.
• Reduce damage from harmful land use practices such as off-roading and agriculture.
• Expand watershed restoration in priority areas (Manell-Geus, Piti-Asan, Tumon Bay/Yigo-Tumon), and upgrade stormwater infrastructure in urban and tourism zones.
• Enhance community involvement in science-based fisheries management and marine conservation.
• Engage the public, businesses, and leaders in reducing land-based pollution and adopting sustainable practices.
• Foster education and outreach programs and increase public awareness around climate adaptation and citizen science.
• Collaborate with the tourism sector to promote sustainable practices, educate visitors, and develop a climate adaptation plan for Tumon.
• Enforce existing plans that support responsible tourism and recreation.
• Engage stakeholders in promoting low-impact recreation on reefs.
• Continue implementation of Local Action Strategies (LAS), including pollution reduction, fisheries management, and outreach.
• Maintain and manage Guam's marine preserves.
• Integrate socioeconomic factors into coral reef planning and promote alternative livelihoods to reduce pressure on reef resources. Freshwater Guam's freshwater ecosystems support a distinct group of aquatic species with many depending on natural stream flow and uninterrupted migration pathways to complete their life cycles. Species exhibiting rheotaxis migrate upstream to feed or spawn but are often obstructed by manmade barriers such as dams. When they reach these barriers with low or stagnant flow, they face increased predation and reduced habitat suitability. Even if upstream migration is successful, many species with amphidromous life cycles rely on downstream drift of juveniles to the ocean for survival. If these young individuals are trapped in these same areas, such as Fena Reservoir, they are not likely to survive. Research by DAWR has shown that rivers feeding into reservoirs support significantly lower diversity of native aquatic life compared to unimpeded streams. To address these challenges, the following objectives will be pursued:
• Assess the impacts of dams and other manmade barriers on native freshwater aquatic organisms and their life cycles
• Conduct a comprehensive biological inventory of freshwater taxa
• Evaluate the presence and impacts of invasive species on native freshwater taxa
• Develop and implement conservation programs to protect native freshwater organisms and their habitats
• Identify and rehabilitate freshwater habitats suitable for reintroduction of SGCN species
• Conduct vulnerability and risk assessments on freshwater ecosystems and the native species they support
• Map and evaluate natural freshwater wetlands, including assessments of habitat extent and changes in response to drought, heavy rainfall, and breeding requirements of species such as the Mariana common moorhen and freshwater invertebrates.

## Threat-Based Actions

Control of Limiting Factors Many of the threats affecting SGCN are exacerbated by ecological and biological limiting factors, such as invasive predators, ungulates, and destructive invertebrates. These factors reduce habitat quality, hinder species recovery, and prevent successful reintroduction or population growth. The following objectives will be pursued to control limiting factors affecting SGCN:
• Assess the abundance and distribution of invasive predators in Conservation Areas and priority habitats for future SOGCN reintroduction and recovery efforts of SGCN.
• Implement targeted control of invasive predators, including brown tree snakes, feral dogs and cats, monitor lizards, and rodents, in areas critical to SGCN recovery. Control strategies will be coordinated by DAWR, USDA, USGS, and other relevant partners.
• Evaluate the feasibility of a managed hunting program to reduce populations of ungulates in conservation and recovery habitats.
• Review and strengthen biosecurity protocols to prevent the introduction and spread of invasive species that threaten SGCN and their habitats.
• Develop and test effective control methods for priority invasive invertebrates, such as little fire ants (LFA), coconut rhinoceros beetle (CRB), and Asian cycad scale, within areas supporting SGCN species.
Law Enforcement of Natural Resource Laws and Regulations As stated previously, the Department Law Enforcement Section has the authority to enforce laws and regulations pertaining to the natural resources of Guam (Table 
2
). The ability of the Department's law enforcement officers to interdict individuals breaking laws and regulations has been hindered due to several factors. These factors include shortfalls in manpower, equipment, and public education about the rules and regulations governing natural resources in Guam. By far, the biggest obstacle for enforcement is public outreach. The public must be properly educated on the laws and the reasons for the laws that protect natural resources. To aid in the protection and enforcement of natural resource laws and regulations we will implement the following objectives:
• Protect Guam's endangered species and SGCN from illegal harvesting or incidental take by enforcing Guam's natural resource regulations and developing regulations for SGCN not protected under current regulations.
• Protect Guam's Marine Preserves and Conservation lands.
• Educate the public of Guam's natural resource regulations.
• Create and maintain a volunteer conservation officer program to aid with monitoring activities in Conservation and other public lands.
• Maintain and promote Conservation Officer law enforcement skills.
• Hire and train additional Conservation officers to protect Guam's biological resources.
Climate Change The Pacific Islands are among the countries most vulnerable to climate change globally. Coastal communities are particularly vulnerable to even small changes in climatic variables, especially rainfall patterns, tropical storm patterns, ocean temperature, and to sea level rise. Vulnerability results from high population densities and growth rates, and scarce natural resources, which are exacerbated by poorly developed and unmaintained infrastructure. As most harvested coral reef organisms are ectotherms (cold-blooded), it is crucial to formally study the observable changes in species' biology in addition to monitoring critical habitats and how they are impacted by changes in climate. Monitoring and adapting to climatic variables are needed to respond and minimize the threats to natural resources. To aid the efforts in protecting and minimizing the climatic variables of natural resources we will implement the following objectives:
• Participate in the Guam Climate Change Task Force (GCCTF) and the Guam Climate Change Advisory Committee (GCCAC), established under Governor Calvo's E.O. 2015-08.
• Assist in a Guam Vulnerability Assessment to Climate Change Plan (funded under TAP-Guam-2016-2, Grant No D16AP00023)
• Take the lead in coordinating a section of the Guam Vulnerability Assessment plan that addresses vulnerability and risk with regard to critical habitat for endangered and threatened species.
• Identify funding for utilizing the dynamically downscaled model of climate impacts in Guam (REF) with Software Assisted Habitat Modeling (SAHM) for VisTrails 1 in order to visualize impacts of climate change on critical habitats for threatened and endangered species.
• Improve the technical capacity of DAWR with regard to tools, specifically, geographic information systems and remote sensing in order to improve decision making, monitoring, and visualization of changes in critical habitats.
• Participate in working groups to establish adaptive management goals and actions to help minimize threats and address impacts for climate change to SGCN and habitats. DAWR have participated in discussions with the Coral Initiative Task Force about climate change impacts occurring in Guam's coral reefs and marine life.
• Develop and implement policies to address protection for coral reef communities and other at-risk habitats in Guam.
Recreation Activities within the Conservation Areas The local Conservation Areas have limited accessibility for the general public. Hunters, hikers, and off-road enthusiasts are the main users of these areas. The Anao Conservation Area is a great example of what a limestone forest should resemble. However, many individuals do not know about this Conservation Area because there are no signs indicating how to get there. The area is mainly used by the hunting community and must be accessed through private lands. By creating more opportunities for the public to get out and experience these areas firsthand, we can educate and instill a sense of stewardship in our natural resources. By providing greater accessibility through the creation of trails into and campsites on conservation lands, we can ensure that future generations experience and appreciate the wonders of Guam's natural resources. The GNWR's visitor center at Ritidian Point provides the opportunity for the public to experience nature and culture. This is the direction we should take with the conservation lands established by the Government of Guam. To assist in creating recreational activities with local conservation areas, we will implement the following objectives:
• Allow public hunting access to game species in Conservation Areas • Develop signage identifying Guam's Conservation Areas highlighting key habitat types, important fauna, geologic formations, and other key aspects of the Conservation Area.
• Allow compatible public activities in Conservation Areas with proper enforcement.

## Goal-Based Actions

Reintroduction and Restoration of SGCN to Designated Habitats The reintroduction and restoration of SGCN to designated habitats is the ultimate goal of all the management and conservation efforts put forth in the SWAP. The following objectives will be implemented for particular species and for all SGCN:
• Establish an inventory of conservation areas for caves and identify other potential cave sites for brown tree snake control and translocation of Mariana swiftlet.
• Determine the genetic similarity and suitability of CNMI and Hawaiian populations of Mariana swiftlets for translocation and reintroduction purposes and to perform reintroductions in Guam if possible.
• Examine and determine the status of SGCN in conservation areas and other suitable habitats.
• Reintroduce native fauna and flora identified as SGCN into conservation lands, GNWR Overlay and other suitable areas (i.e., Safe Harbors).
Captive Breeding and Translocation Captive breeding and translocation are essential management tools for natural resource managers who work with small populations. Currently, DAWR propagates two species of birds, the Guam rail and Micronesian kingfisher. The DAWR facility can house and support 144 rails, and 16 kingfishers. The Department envisions the creation of a new larger facility that would support conservation efforts in Guam and throughout Micronesia. Guam has the infrastructure that would support such a facility and could be the "hub" of Micronesia for captive propagation. Most of Guam's SGCN would benefit from a large facility which would have facilities for mammals, birds, reptiles, gastropods and other invertebrates that need captive propagation. For example, a captive rearing program for Guam's tree snails needs to be established. The University of Guam has initiated captive rearing for the Eight-Spot butterfly, under the supervision of Dr. George C. Fiedler and in collaboration with Dr. Else Demeulenaere, Associate Director of the UOG CIS & SG Island Conservation Lab. This work is being further developed by Matthew Putnam as part of his master's thesis. A pilot program to start captive rearing was funded through SWAP. The progeny from captive breeding efforts would be released back into the wild. In addition to the benefits for Guam's SGCN, the new facility could serve the region as a refuge and breeding center for all terrestrial species in peril throughout Micronesia.
The beginning and/or continuation of captive breeding (propagation) and translocation efforts of regional endemics and indigenous species for Guam and Micronesia will be addressed by the following objectives:
• Maintain and expand captive propagation facilities to further serve the needs of Guam and Micronesia's SOGCN.
• Determine the need to captive breed other SGCN. Implement captive propagation of endemic species of animals and plants for release into the wild continue captive breeding of Guam rails and Micronesian kingfishers.
• Establish protections of wild SOGCN flora from pests.
• Determine the feasibility of translocation of shared SGCN from the CNMI to support landscape-scale conservation.
• Provide an adequate diversity of SGCN plant species and sufficient numbers of individual plants for planting in Conservation Areas. Collect seeds and seedlings from the wild for transplantation to Conservation Areas.
• Establish cooperative agreements with CNMI-DFW to coordinate our conservation actions and standardize monitoring techniques for species shared between Guam and the CNMI, such as fanihi, haggan, and various seabird species. These agreements can also facilitate discussions on potential translocation of additional species between the CNMI and Guam.
Public Awareness and Education The need to foster an informed population about conservation and the importance of natural resources to the island is critical to the success of any long-term conservation efforts. Guam DAWR and its partners in the SWAP have identified a lack of public awareness as a priority threat to many of our conservation actions. Guam has experienced success in creating public awareness for coral reef issues through Education and Outreach Local Action Strategies (EO LAS) developed by the Guam Coral Reef Initiative (GCRI) Coordinating Committee. For instance, the Guam Visitors Bureau (GVB) and the tourism industry previously worked with GCRI to create a tourism professional certification and resources to promote reef-friendly practices. are now working with the natural resources agencies to market Guam's coral reefs, particularly, the marine preserves. This new awareness of the economic value of our coral reef resources is beginning to create a sense of stewardship in the industry, absent during the economic boom of the 1980s and recession of the 1990s. The goal of the education and outreach local action strategy (EO LAS) is to increase awareness of the need for the protection of Guam's coral reefs through improved efforts in the community, in the classroom, and with policy makers. Other venues for introducing conservation awareness and outreach are the Island Pride Campaign and publications such as Man, Land and Sea. The Island Pride Campaign is a program that combines educational and environmental activities with fun events to teach children to love the island's resources and instill a sense of stewardship. The quarterly publication of Man, Land and Sea which is published through BSP is another outlet for public/private education and awareness campaigns.
Under DAWR, significant outreach and education projects were developed and implemented from 2012 to 2016. First, they developed a marine preserve campaign for Piti Bomb Holes Preserve, named "Piti Pride Tepungan Wide" with an extension of the campaign to Tumon Bay Marine Preserve was well received. Second, a ridge to reef conservation program called, "Help Save Guam's Reefs" remains as an on-going effort island wide. With financial and technical support from Rare, an international, non-profit conservation organization, the DAWR Aquatic Education Resource Information and Education Officer received training in communication in conservation. GCRI has several staff members dedicated to community outreach. Each year, they conduct dozens of school visits and outreach events, reaching thousands of Guam's residents. They also run the Eyes of the Reef citizen science program, which trains community members to identify and report threats to Guam's reefs. In addition, GCRI supports the Guardians of the Reef educational program, where high school students are trained to teach 3rd graders about the importance of coral reefs and how to protect them. This program has been running for over 10 years, with our 2025 cohort recently completed. Key training components consisted of campaign design, social marketing strategies, and socio-economic result analysis. One of the five marine preserves and its community was selected for the campaign based on drafts of the DAWR Limits of Acceptable Change report and the Conservation Action Plan (CAP) for Piti Bomb Holes Preserve. Using Knowledge, Attitude, and Practice/Perception (KAP) surveys and analysis, the campaign, "Piti Pride Tepungan Wide" was created with a campaign mascot named "Piti Pete", an orange spine unicorn fish, Naso lituratus. The campaign identified target audiences of fishers and resource users to lead the community using this marine preserve towards improving their activities in the water to reduce negative impacts to fish habitat. Piti Pride was successful at implementing a Pride Line number, sometimes referred to as a hotline number, to increase communication from the public to DAWR resource managers. In addition, new partnerships in Aquatic Education were created with local and federal resource management agencies, as well as private marine sport businesses and other local government agencies, not only to increase awareness of the benefits of marine preserves but also to train their employees, colleagues, and customers to be more diligent in its protection. Some outreach activities included collaborating with multiple enforcement entities to improve communication and monitoring support of the marine preserves; increasing education of marine preserve benefits to tour guides and tourism related businesses and local agencies by offering trainings to demonstrate how to teach others to reduce damage to fish habitat; and presenting "Piti Pride" to educators and the general public at schools, events, and other outreach activities.
After analysis of the results and impacts of the "Piti Pride" campaign, Tumon Bay Marine Preserve was selected as the next conservation campaign site to continue the efforts from the Pride Line, 688-DAWR. To enhance sustainable tourism, GVB has offered to add marine conservation in their Guam Community College Tour Guide Certification Course, to replace and/fix conservation signs, and to support community awareness of marine preserve guidelines. As another "Piti Pride" campaign partner, the University of Guam Sea Grant has offered some financial support to continue the marine preserve campaigns. Part of the support includes trainings, producing a new logo and signs for the Tumon Bay Marine Preserve campaign. A difference from the "Piti Pride" campaign, the local community will be carrying out their outreach activities themselves to ensure sustainability of conservation efforts. Starting with the efforts of the 2018 coral reef management fellow, BSP, GCRI, and partners recently have created a series of educational signage for popular beaches throughout Guam that feature information on coastal stewardship and water safety. Signs have been installed at Tumon Bay and Piti Bomb Holes (Tepungan), along with other locations such as Talo'fo'fo' Bay and Hagat Marina.
Other recent educational efforts include: • GCRI Education and Outreach Strategy: The 2024-2026 coral reef management fellow, Sabrina Medina, is currently based out of GCRI and working on gathering input and data from educators and students to improve future education and outreach programs. The information will be compiled into a report that will feature best practices and strategies to better support student and public programs. Eyes of the Reef: this program started back in 2015 and was recently relaunched after the pandemic. The program provides training for community members to identify coral reef threats such as bleaching, marine debris, and invasive/nuisance species. Community members are encouraged to report any threats via a form to allow natural resource managers to respond.
• Seagrass Flower Citizen Science: this campaign shared flyers with various water users such as fishers, landowners, and recreationists to help spot seagrass flowers around the island and support attempts to find seeds for restoration purposes. Additionally, "paint and learn" events were held in the Inalahan and Piti mayor's office community centers to help raise awareness of seagrasses and the citizen science campaign.
• DOAG summer interns: since 2022 the Department of Youth Affairs has offered summer internships to HS students and placed them in government agencies. DOAG-DAWR has hosted numerous summer interns, and led various field trips, hands-on activities, and educational presentations for them.
• Additional GCRI efforts: field trips, classroom presentations, career day festival booths, lesson plans, videos, website and resource portal, presentations at local educator symposiums,
To increase awareness of the general public and private industry, the following objectives will be implemented:
• Explore and evaluate social marketing methods to engage public participation and involvement for preserving Guam's wildlife and habitats.
• Develop, implement and utilize existing programs to increase public awareness of natural resources and issues pertaining to them.
• Develop outreach campaigns that educate the public and private industry of the value of preserving Guam's wildlife and habitats.
• Develop a plan to address public awareness and education of conservation issues-create products that target audiences with appropriate messages, formalize presentations, increase public service announcements, and enhance messages by including a call to action and facilitate public involvement in conservation efforts.
• Increase collaboration between environmental educators on island, including K-12 educators, higher education, local government, and other informal educators.
• Establish an internship program to implement conservation awareness programs Legal Protection for Habitats and Wildlife Guam has several local laws providing protection for native flora and fauna. The Department has the authority to enforce and submit changes for the adjudication of the laws that govern Game, Forestry, and Conservation (5 GCA, Chapter 63, PL-6-85). In addition to ensuring the authority to enforce these laws, Guam code (5 GCA, §63121 and §63101-63117) also provides a list of species that are to be protected. The Endangered Species Act, 5 GCA §63208, allows for the adjudication of an endangered species list for Guam. The responsibility to promulgate the Endangered Species list falls to the Department who then provides the list to the Attorney General of Guam and the Legislature for adjudication.
In an effort to provide maximum legal protection for the habitats for preserving and enhancing the recovery and/or restoration of wildlife to Guam, the following objectives will be implemented:
• Develop cooperative agreements with federal and military partners, such as US Fish and Wildlife Service, US Air Force, and US Navy to include federal and Guam Conservation Lands as part of the Guam National Wildlife Refuge Overlay. Develop cooperative agreements for management, research and protection of endangered species and species of greatest conservation need.
• Explore and pursue safe harbor agreements with private landowners adjacent to Conservation Land and other areas where wildlife may benefit.
• Determine boundaries for all the Conservation Lands, and to further pursue the possibility of incorporating previously proposed conservation areas.
• Develop a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with the CNMI partners to facilitate the conservation restoration measures of shared (historically) fauna and plants.
• Formulate regulations that prioritize climate change within island sustainable development strategies and planning.
• Identify other Government of Guam lands for the use in species recovery efforts.
• Research and propose regulations that protect excess lands from unnecessary loss or destruction or inappropriate usage. The conservation actions outlined in Chapter 4 represent Guam's strategic response to the threats and challenges facing its Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and their habitats. However, effective conservation requires more than action alone; it also demands careful evaluation. Chapter 5 outlines how Guam will monitor the outcomes of these actions, assess the condition of species and ecosystems over time, and incorporate new scientific information into future planning. This adaptive approach ensures that conservation efforts remain effective, data-driven, and responsive to emerging threats and opportunities. Together, these chapters form an integrated framework for conserving Guam's biodiversity through both targeted action and continuous learning.

## Monitoring and Evaluation

The approach to monitoring and evaluation is based on expanding existing efforts in order to be cost effective, in light of limited financial resources 
(Schoonmaker and Luscombe 2005)
. For new efforts, the approach will be to focus on relevant, realistic, and effective monitoring and evaluation that is cost-effective, sustainable, and has minimal impact to native ecosystems. The SWAP encompasses monitoring and evaluation in nine areas:
1) Surveys of species and habitats 2) Effectiveness of proposed strategies 3) Ten-year revision of the SWAP 4) Develop habitat suitability models to identify and prioritize areas capable of supporting target species, providing a science-based foundation to guide conservation actions, habitat restoration, and management efforts. Monitoring Habitats and Conservation Areas The SWAP will entail the management and monitoring of restorative actions within conservation areas in Guam. Many conservation areas in Guam, both on military and government of Guam lands, are in dire need of restoration as a result of ungulate damage 
(Wiles et al. 1999)
. Conservation actions proposed by existing plans to rehabilitate designated forest habitats are essential for restoring these areas to habitat conditions favorable to native fauna species.
Loss of native habitat around Guam is driven by multiple factors including fire and ungulate damage, development, and invasive species. Some areas heavily impacted by ungulates have changed into monospecific plant communities that consist of primarily plants that are less palatable to ungulates. For example, O. marianensis is a native species that easily develops monotypic stands because deer do not favor them. Much of southern areas of Guam are covered with Miscanthus floridulus because this grass species quickly flourishes in areas made available by repeated burning. The non-native palm, Palma brava, outcompeted the native ravine forest species and is now dominating in some areas.
Goal: To monitor the progress of restoration efforts of habitats listed in the conservation strategies.
Objective 1: Develop a GIS map of historic and current distribution of SGCN for Guam.
Objective 2: Develop maps that illustrate the reduction of habitat degradation and loss as control measures for invasive flora and fauna are implemented in conservation areas.
Vegetation GIS Maps for Guam • Establish and strengthen partnerships with DOAG's Forestry Division, US Forest Service, FSRD, and private forestry to provide support and assistance for a completed vegetation analysis for Guam. This research provides an assessment on habitat availability at a broad scale and will serve as a baseline for native forest restoration.
Monitoring long-term trends in forest types in Guam to aid managers to propose effective management to recover habitat and SGCN species.
• The map will be updated as information is made available. The map will be used to identify important trends in habitat restoration in Guam.
Monitoring Native Forests • Conduct plant surveys (transects and counts) to determine the status and efficacy of conservation efforts for habitat in key areas. Vegetation transects will be used to assess the condition of the conservation. The abundance and diversity of the types of plant species in the conservation areas will be documented.
• Determine the status of conservation areas to determine the abundance of various native species of plants.
• Historic information will be compiled to provide baseline information on habitats and be compared to current trends and conditions.
• Information on conservation areas (and high valued habitats, to be identified) will be used to specifically determine what actions will be taken to improve the habitat for fauna and flora goals. Many areas principally need a reduction in feral deer and pig populations.
• Develop and implement reforestation techniques in Guam conservation areas and national wildlife refuge overlay lands.
Many of Guam's flora and fauna are extirpated, extinct, or found only in captivity or in low numbers. For those species still extant in Guam, monitoring of these populations as conservation actions are implemented will be important to conservation evaluations.
To ensure that observations are consistent and appropriate for the target species or habitat type, personnel will be trained in tailored identification and survey methods. Such training will include identification of calls for certain species, recording of the number of animals observed, and use of standard bird survey techniques incorporated in 
Ramsey and Scott (1981)
.
Goals: Monitor the long-term population trends of SGCN.
Objective 1: Conduct colony and/or island-wide counts indicating population trends over a five-year period.
Objective 2: Conduct colony and/or island-wide population surveys that evaluate the relationship between SGCN abundance and ongoing efforts to improve the quality and extent of limestone and ravine forest habitats.
Terrestrial Mammals • Continue to conduct quarterly counts of all colonies at a minimum. As new colonies are found they need to monitored quarterly as well
• Continue to conduct an island-wide survey every 5 years using randomly stratified fixed survey stations.
• Continue efforts with Joint Region Marianas, GNWR, and NPS to work collaboratively in managing the recovery of fruit bats in Guam, especially in military installations, Ritidian refuge, and NPS lands.
Native Birds • Direct Monitoring -A combination of radio telemetry and re-sighting banded birds will continue to be used for monitoring for extant bird populations, as well as captive reared birds that are released into the wild. DAWR currently conducts quarterly auditory playback surveys and aims to implement long-term camera monitoring. The Department will also resume using radio telemetry within the next year. In the future, as captive flocks of Guam rails and Micronesian kingfishers are large enough for release into the wild in Guam, monitoring with these methods will be valuable in determining the survivorship of released birds. DAWR also actively monitors Micronesian starlings and common moorhens (as staff are available). Information from these surveys will guide future conservation actions for the protection of these two species.
• Playback Surveys -Previously, playback surveys were and important tool for monitoring the population of Marianas crows in Guam. These surveys involve playing tape-recorded calls at survey points a minimum of 300 meters apart for 2.5 minutes followed by a 2-minute silent period to record numbers, distance, and plumage condition. When crows are reintroduced to Guam playback surveys for them will be reinstated. Playback censuses are important in monitoring Guam rails released on Rota and Islan Dåno'. Other re-introduced species of birds could be surveyed by this method as well.
• Roadside Counts -Annual May roadside counts along 23 survey routes around northern and southern Guam will continue to monitor the status of native and non-native bird species. Survey points are spaced 600 meters apart along roadways, and the number of birds heard and seen during a five-minute survey period at each station is recorded. This survey will expand to additional areas of Guam as populations of birds increase, and the frequency of counts may increase depending on need.
• Swiftlet Surveys -Mariana swiftlets will be surveyed by counting the number of birds within the cave and then counting the number of birds entering and exiting the cave during a 1-to-2-hour period before dark (dark is defined as one hour after sunset). The swiftlet population for a cave is then estimated by subtracting the number of departures, from the number of arrivals, and adding the estimated number of swiftlets in the cave prior to the count. All counts are conducted with at least one observer at each cave entrance for a particular colony. In some cases, two observers are utilized, two counting the number of swiftlets entering a cave and the third person counting the number of birds exiting. In addition to cave exit surveys, surveys should be carried out to search for new caves being used by Mariana swiftlets.
• Shorebird Surveys -Survey for migratory shorebirds, August through May, along shorelines (Togcha, and Dungca's Beach) and grassy fields to determine the abundance of specific shorebirds. Areas historically monitored will be surveyed initially to establish current baseline information and serve as a means of comparison. Other appropriate areas will be added. Information from Annual Marianas Audubon Christmas bird counts and other pertinent sources will be incorporated into the database on shorebird abundances in Guam.
• Develop and implement a Citizen Science group to get community involved in birding and teach individuals the best management practices in sharing field observations.
• Investigate impacts of prolonged weather conditions to breeding moorhens and invertebrates.
Terrestrial Reptiles • Conduct annual surveys to determine the status of each SGCN reptiles. These surveys will be conducted in Conservation Areas to monitor the status of each area, and the effectiveness of conservation measures. Additionally, surveys will be conducted in areas known to historically harbor these lizards.
Terrestrial Gastropods • As the populations of remaining native snails are very small, visual surveys of native snails in areas known to contain them will be conducted to determine the current population numbers. Initial surveys will be conducted in the Hila'an, Tanguisson and Haputo areas and other areas where they might occur.
• Conduct annual surveys in key areas to determine the status of each species to determine the efficacy of conservation efforts. Insects • Conduct annual assessments on the status and distribution of Guam's native butterflies needing immediate conservation attention.
• Conduct annual surveys of other native insect species needing close monitoring.
• Identify sites where consistent, long-term monitoring can be conducted for H. o. marianensis. Host plant health assessments and monitoring will be performed at these sites alongside H. o. marianensis population surveys.
• Perform updated surveys of sites where H. o. marianensis were identified. These surveys will help form a better understanding of the species' current population dynamics.
• Maintain a population of captive H. o. marianensis to protect against extinction and to augment wild populations where possible.
• Identify host plant-dense sites where H. o. marianensis may be introduced to create populations.
Terrestrial Plants • Conduct regular assessments of the status, distribution, and population trends of terrestrial plant SGCN.
• Conduct periodic surveys of lesser-known or data-deficient terrestrial plant species to improve knowledge and inform management needs.
• Identify priority areas across Guam where consistent, long-term monitoring can take place.
• Integrate habitat quality assessments into species monitoring efforts, including vegetation health, invasive species presence, and habitat connectivity.
• Develop and maintain captive populations, seed banks, or other ex situ conservation measures for terrestrial plant SGCN to reduce extinction risk and support future reintroduction efforts if necessary.
• Implement and support habitat restoration projects in degraded areas to increase habitat suitability for terrestrial plant SGCN.
• Collect and store seeds of SGCN plant species, maintain stocks in nurseries, and outplant at appropriate sites.

## Freshwater Surveys

Major threats to Guam's aquatic species include fishing, pollution, invasive species, development, recreation, and disease. Because freshwater and marine resources are intimately linked, impacts in the upper reaches of Guam's watersheds affect freshwater organisms and are amplified throughout the watershed and impact marine organisms as well. Partners such as the FSRD, GEPA, NPS, and the University of Guam are seeking insight into the problems facing Guam's aquatic resources. Together these organizations are tackling issues such as soil erosion rates, revegetation of badland areas, sedimentation on nearshore reef communities, pollution levels in coastal waters, and settling and recruitment rates of corals.
Guam's native freshwater flora and fauna are threatened by habitat loss due to sedimentation, eutrophication, and increased water flow caused by bank alteration and loss of riparian vegetation. Because of their amphidromous life histories, all of Guam's native fish species are vulnerable to stream blockage. Monitoring these species in their native habitat will allow DAWR to monitor the existing stocks and to evaluate improvement due to management activities.
Goal: To monitor the long-term population trends of freshwater SGCN.
Assessing Impacts of Dams • Conduct a long-term study of the effect a manmade dam (Fena) has on the native freshwater fauna of Guam, as almost all of Guam's native freshwater fauna is amphidromous, with a marine larval stage in their life history before they return to freshwater to spend their adult lives. Dams affect native fauna. A dam acts as a physical barrier to some native organisms. Organisms not able to pass upstream of the dam are limited to the reaches of rivers below the dam.
• Survey seven Guam rivers annually to monitor the effect a dam has on native fauna. The three rivers that feed into the Fena reservoir (Maagas, Almagosa, and Sadok) are classified experimental, and the river below Fena dam (Maulap) and three other rivers outside the Fena watershed (Ylig, Lonfit, and Manenggon) are the controls.
• In each river, a 200-meter section will be chosen, and divided into 20 10-meter segments. Within each segment, a quadrate will be randomly chosen and surveyed, for a total of 20 sample sites per river.
• Data collected at each quadrate will include species identification, number and size of individuals of each species observed, activity of observed animals, two most dominant substrate types, percentage of canopy cover over quadrate, area of quadrate, depth of water in quadrate, and average water velocity in quadrate, as well as observers, location, date and time.
Baseline River Inventory • Continue biological river inventory for Guam to obtain baseline biological data important for management of our freshwater resources. Of over 100 named rivers and streams, DAWR has biological information on about 67 rivers. DAWR will be surveying rivers with no known biological information.
• Data collection includes visual surveys, as well as specimen collection using net and electrofishing. DAWR now has biological information for 67 rivers, and eventually intends to collect information on all 101.
Freshwater river surveys • The freshwater fishery resources will be monitored by surveying seven rivers in three watersheds each year for analysis and comparison between watersheds by using appropriate parametric or non-parametric tests.
Reservoir Fisheries Monitoring • Monitor the freshwater fishery in Fena Lake by conducting a stock assessment, using electrofishing and mark-recapture methodology, to determine species, species density, and other environmental parameters.
• The freshwater fishery resources in Masso reservoir will be monitored by conducting mark-recapture studies on a yearly basis to collect biological information of the freshwater fisheries resource.
Monitoring Wetland Habitats • A map of the historic and current distribution of Guam's wetlands will be compiled.
These maps will show trends of the distribution of the wetlands in Guam. Mitigation wetland sites, ponding basins, and other man-made ponds will be included in the inventory.
• As these areas encompass a large area on Guam, broad scale monitoring will be used to assess the conditions of these habitats. Appropriate partnership will be developed as some of these areas are privately or federally owned.
• Habitat suitability models will be evaluated for their ability to predict species presences, focusing on moorhens and other native freshwater organisms. By identifying areas with the highest likelihood of supporting target species, these models can inform site selection for habitat restoration or protection efforts. Model outputs will guide conservation actions, such as prioritizing wetland enhancement, controlling invasive species, or restoring hydrology, thereby increasing the suitability of these areas for native species and enhancing overall habitat quality.

## Guam Environmental Monitoring and Assessment Program (EMAP) -Surface Waters

• Continue participation in the development of the Guam Environmental Protection Agency's EMAP protocols for surface waters of the Western Pacific eco-region. This program will monitor many parameters of river habitat health including general water chemistry, physical habitat parameters, periphyton community structure and abundance, biomass and chlorophyll, fish community structure and abundance, macroinvertebrate community structure and abundance, sediment chemistry, fish tissue chemistry, water column contaminants and rapid habitat and visual stream assessments. DAWR staff will also assist GEPA with biological surveys.
Goal: To protect vital marine habitats and the life they contain.
Objective 1: To compile marine trend information to identify serious threats to Guam's marine resources.
Objective 2: To determine water quality indicators important to coral reefs to help manage Guam coral reefs, thereby benefiting the marine life.
Objective 3: Participate in the National Coral Reef Monitoring Program (NCRMP) to improve the understanding of coral reef ecosystems, evaluate and reduce adverse impacts, enhance coral reef ecosystem-based fisheries management and conservation through cooperation with partners (federal and local agencies and non-governmental organizations), and provide scientific information needed to establish, strengthen, and manage MPAs (NOAA, Pacific Islands Fisheries Science Center, Coral Reef Ecosystem Division website 
http://www.nmfs.hawaii.edu/crd
).
Quantifying and Assessing the Effects of Sedimentation on Fish Abundance, Fish Diversity, and Benthic Habitats including corals • Assess Piti Bomb Holes Preserve and Fouha Bay, areas with current and future erosion control projects, and two appropriate control sites for detailed monitoring.
• Conduct fish counts, timed-swim counts, and video-transects on 24 permanent transects located in the 20', 30', 40', and 50' depths of the fore reef slopes and reef flats of Piti Bomb Holes Preserve, Fouha Bay, and two control sites.
• Install and monitor thirty sediment traps monthly each year during the study at Piti Bomb Holes Preserve, Fouha Bay, and two control sites in order to quantify sediment load at impacted sites.
Analyzing and Assessing Recreational Impacts on Coral Reef Habitat and Determining a Carrying Capacity within Marine Preserves • Hire a Marine Preserves Biologist and fisheries technician.
• Investigate and assess the impacts of recreation activities within Tumon Bay and Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserves on coral reef habitat by linking recreational activities to their effects on the abundance, diversity, and distribution of fishes, corals, macroinvertebrates, and marine plants as well as substrate cover, water clarity, temperature, dissolved oxygen, pH, and damage or pollution related to recreational activities and comparing the effects to appropriate control sites.
Guam Environmental Monitoring and Assessment Program -Coastal Waters • Implement an Environmental Monitoring and Assessment Program (EMAP), to be conducted by GEPA and assisted by DAWR staff. The EMAP program will assess the physical and chemical condition of Guam's Marine water using standardized methods and a suite of environmental indicators. This information will be used to rank the relative importance of various stressors on the affected resource types.
National Coral Reef Monitoring Program (NCRMP) • Guam DAWR will continue participation in MARAMP cruise trips. The MARAMP is intended to be a long-term monitoring program with research cruises scheduled biannually. The cruise usually comprised of staff from the NOAA Coral Reef Ecosystem Investigation Program, DAWR, NPS, and the University of Guam Marine Laboratory to conduct a variety of ecological and oceanographic assessments, including the following:
• Benthic Habitat Mapping: multi-beam surveys, single beam QTC surveys, geodetic control, towed diver surveys, and TOAD towed camera surveys,
• Fish, Turtle, and Marine Mammal Surveys: belt transects, stationary point counts, towed diver surveys, roving diver surveys, and hydroacoustic surveys,
• Benthic Surveys (corals, other inverts, algae): belt transects, towed diver surveys, roving diver surveys, and TOAD towed camera surveys, and
• Oceanography: closely spaced CTDs, drifters, subsurface temperature, ADCP transects, CREWS/SST buoys, current/wave moorings.

## Marine Preserve Monitoring

Guam DAWR fish population surveys • In 1997, Guam established five marine preserves around the island amounting to 11.8% of Guam's shoreline. Guam DAWR will continue to sample the fish populations and reef communities in two of the preserve areas and control sites to determine the effectiveness of the preserve system. These studies focus on the fish species targeted for consumption and for indicator species such as butterflyfish.
• As in prior years, sixty-six (66) permanent belt transects (50m x5m) will be surveyed on the reef flats and fore reef slopes of two preserve sites, Piti Bomb Holes Preserve and Achang Reef Flat Preserve, and three control sites, Asan Bay, Cocos Lagoon, and Pago Bay. Two sets of transects will be on the fore reef slope at the 20', 30', 40', and 50' depth contours. Eight transects are on the reef flat at each site representing distinct microhabitats (seagrass, coral/algal/rubble, and sandy bottom).
• Fish communities will be surveyed using two different visual survey techniques along each transect. Density will be assessed using a visual fish census along a strip transect. Two fish counters following the 50 m long permanent transect, each counting all target fish within 2.5 m of their side of the transect. All target fish within this 250-m2 area will be scored on data sheets based on their species and size class. Three size classes will be used based on the fork length of the fish (<15 cm, 15 cm-30 cm, >30 cm). The strip transect method will be complemented by a timed visual survey in the same area. At each site, fish counters will record the species and size class of all fish encountered in the area during a 30-minute interval.
• Data will be analyzed statistically using two-tailed paired t-tests 
(Sokal and Rohlf, 1995)
 to compare fish densities and diversity over time within each study site. The Shannon diversity index will be used to calculate an index number for species diversity and evenness at each site for both pre-and post-implementation data. A higher index number indicates greater diversity. If the assumptions of analysis of variance (ANOVA) are not met, even after transformations, a nonparametric test will be conducted 
(Sokal and Rohlf, 1995)
. • The priority reef areas selected for long-term monitoring by a group of managers and biologists at the program's inception include the seaward slope in the Tumon Bay Marine Preserve and a control site in East Agana Bay, the seaward slope of the Achang Reef Flat Marine Preserve and a control site along the eastern Cocos Barrier Reef, and the seaward slope of Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve. While the program currently does not monitor a control site for the Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve, the National Park Service has conducted regular monitoring of the reef in the adjacent Asan Bay. Additionally, a recent collaborative effort between the NOAA Pacific Islands Fisheries Science Center and the GLTMP yielded a significant amount of reef fish survey data for reef areas along the seaward slope in the Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve and Asan Bay. A report is available at: 
https://repository.library.noaa.gov/view/noaa/61256/
.
• Stationary point count fish surveys, benthic photo transects surveys, macroinvertebrate belt transect surveys, and chain-length rugosity surveys are carried out on a regular basis at numerous (20 or more) 30 m-long transects within each priority reef area. The Tumon and East Agana sites are visited annually, while the Piti, Achang, and Cocos-East sites are visited biennially.
• The most recent report published by the GLTMP is available at: 
https://www.guamcoralreefs.info/s/GLTMP-Decade-of-Change-Report-Aug2023.pdf
• While the analyses of GLTMP data conducted in-house provide an indication of status and trends within each priority area, it is recommended that researchers be hired to integrate the fish and benthic datasets collected by the GLTMP, as well as other available data, to evaluate the relationship between the fish and benthic communities at each site and, if appropriate, make explicit comparisons between the marine preserve sites and the nominal control sites.
• In addition to the monitoring of priority reef areas, the GLTMP have participated in multiple island-wide coral bleaching response survey efforts and in 2021 established 20 permanent coral bleaching response and recovery sites at shallow (5 m) reef front sites around the island. At least one of these sites occurs in the Pati Pt., Tumon Bay, Piti Bomb Holes, and Achang Reef Flat marine preserves. Currently, three 30 m-long benthic photo transects are surveyed at each of the sites during and between bleaching events. Fish surveys are not currently conducted at the island-wide sites, but if program capacity increases it may be possible to add fish and macroinvertebrate surveys. Guam Environmental Monitoring and Assessment Program -Coastal Waters • Compare fish densities and diversity over time within each study site. The Shannon diversity index will be used to calculate an index number for species diversity and evenness at each site for both pre-and post-implementation data. A higher index number indicates greater diversity. If the assumptions of analysis of variance (ANOVA) are not met, even after transformations, a nonparametric test will be conducted 
(Sokal and Rohlf, 1995)
.
• This program will assess the biological condition of Guam's marine water using standardized methods. Reef fish, macroinvertebrates, macroalgae, and coral abundance will be recorded at 50 randomly selected sites throughout Guam's waters. This program will be complemented by water quality monitoring and sediment analysis.

## Predator and Invasive Species Surveys

The introduction of non-native species to Guam, whether intentional or accidental, has had a negative impact on native species and their environment. The introduced brown tree snake caused significant damage to Guam's native vertebrate fauna. The recovery of native fauna hinges on the control of the snake. However, efforts to eradicate the snake must be combined with habitat restoration, captive breeding and translocations, and removal or control of other predators such as cats, dogs, and rats. Monitoring the abundance of the brown tree snake is important in determining the efficacy of brown tree snake control measures. In addition, monitoring the presence and distribution of other invasive species must also be emphasized.
Goal: To determine population trends and evaluate effectiveness of ongoing control of introduced predator invasive species that limit the recovery of SOGCN on Guam.
Objective 1: Monitor monthly abundance of brown tree snakes and other predator species in Conservation Areas designated for restoration and where brown tree snake control is being implemented to evaluate the efficacy of tree snake control measures.
Objective 2: Identify the abundance and distribution of invasive species in Conservation Areas and remove these species.
• Implement area-wide multi-species predator control by trapping and/or barriers in conservation areas slated for release of SOGCN.
• Continue to support studies into improving predator control techniques.
• Assess predator control threshold for non-federally listed forest bird species and other SGCN.
• Monitor the abundance of invasive species in Guam and determine feasible methods to be employed to reverse the threat for SGCN.
• Implement area-wide multi-species predator control by trapping and/or barriers in conservation areas slated for release of SGCN.
• Continue to support studies into improving predator control techniques.
• Assess predator control threshold for non-federally listed forest bird species and other SGCN.
