• Monitor the abundance of invasive species in Guam and determine feasible methods to be employed to reverse the threats for SGCN.

## Effectiveness of Measures

To effectively achieve the goals and objectives identified in the Guam State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP), the Department must strategically allocate conservation resources. Numerous partners and stakeholders carry out conservation actions within their own organizational boundaries and funding resources, each aligned with specific mandates or purposes.
To align and track these efforts, the Department will develop project-specific result chains (AFWA, 2011) that visually represent how specific actions are expected to lead to desired outcomes and ultimately contribute to the overarching goals of SWAP and its partner organizations. For example, the Department launched a biosecurity awareness campaign encouraging boaters traveling from the main island to Islan Dåno', an area free of rodents and brown tree snakes (BTS), to inspect their vessels for these invasive species. Similarly, a partner agency like the Guam Invasive Species Council Office may initiate a campaign urging boaters traveling to the CNMI to inspect and clean their boats to prevent the spread of BTS and marine invasives such as algae.
While implemented by different agencies and in different contexts, these actions address common threats (e.g., invasive species), and the result chains for these efforts may be very similar. This illustrates the importance of coordination and consistent evaluation frameworks.
In Chapter 3, the identified threats are broad, spanning multiple conservation approaches and requiring flexibility depending on the agency carrying out the action. Figure 
19
 illustrates the process that will be adopted by DAWR, in collaboration with technical expert partners, to evaluate the effectiveness of actions and ensure they are contributing meaningfully to conservation goals.
To measure the effectiveness of conservation actions, the Department employs a Three-Level Approach:
Level 1: Initial implementation of the conservation action Level 2: Evaluation and refinement of methods based on outcomes Level 3: Adaptive management to modify strategies and optimize success This approach allows for ongoing learning and adjustment, ensuring conservation actions remain responsive, relevant, and results driven.

## Data Management and Archiving

Faunal and Vegetation survey data will be archived with DAWR. The information collected will be analyzed and reported in State Wildlife Grant reports, to include information on species abundance and distribution, and on habitat trends. Statistical analyses (either parametric or non-parametric) will be used to determine significant changes in abundances 
(Sokal and Rolhf 1995)
.
Wildlife Tracking and Reporting Actions for the Conservation of Species (TRACS) will be incorporated for sharing information of projects funded under SWG funds. TRACS is designed to effectively monitor trends for species and habitat conservation. More importantly, it serves as a vehicle to engage partners and stakeholders to contribute recommendations to improve and adapt management on projects.
Goal: Management and dissemination of compiled field notes and resource information. 

## Establishment of a Monitoring Group

As mentioned in the 2005 and 2015 SWAPs, establishing a monitoring group is essential to support the synthesis of information collected through the monitoring of SGCN and their habitats. Unfortunately, this group was not formed in the ten years following the 2015 plan. Moving forward, efforts will focus on assembling a group of technical experts to review project objectives, approaches, and results. The monitoring group will include representatives from relevant local and federal agencies such as the Department of Agriculture, GCMP-Marine, NOAA, USDA-NRCS, USFWS Ecological Services, Guam Community concern for native species was clear throughout the responses. The ko'ko' (Gallirallus owstoni) was the most frequently mentioned, appearing in 24% of responses. Native plants, especially the niyok (Cocos nucifera), were also commonly submitted. Other notable species included the fanihi (Pteropus mariannus mariannus), sihek (Todiramphus cinnamominus cinnamominus), and haggan (Chelonia mydas). People also mentioned coral, reef fish, birds, and "boonie dogs," reflecting a sense of care for Guam's biodiversity and its deep cultural significance, especially for the CHamoru people.
Invasive species were the top concern, particularly the brown tree snake (Boiga irregularis) and the coconut rhinoceros beetle (Oryctes rhinoceros), both of which have severely impacted native ecosystems. Habitat loss from development, land clearing, and feral ungulates were also frequently mentioned. These responses show widespread public awareness of the threats facing Guam's wildlife.
Respondents made connections between species survival and conservation actions.
Commonly cited priorities included pest control, healthy forests, stronger enforcement, habitat restoration, and public education. Some people brought up the importance of schoolbased programs and community outreach, which shows strong overlap between public knowledge and effective conservation strategies.
The ko'ko' was often quoted as a symbol of hope and natural heritage, especially in its decline due to the brown tree snake. Some respondents demonstrated an understanding of ecological interdependence, for example, how bird loss can lead to beetle overpopulation and coconut tree decline. Cultural concerns were also discussed, including the impact of rhino beetle damage on traditional materials still used in CHamoru practices. Frustration over gaps in enforcement, particularly around land clearing and invasive species control, was a recurring theme. Military land use was also mentioned as a challenge. Some responses reflected confusion around species status, emphasizing the need for expanded environmental education.
People's interest in Guam's wildlife often stemmed from personal experiences, cultural and family ties, or classroom experiences. Conservationists, elders, teachers, and agency staff were named as knowledgeable sources. When asked what other species they wanted to learn about, respondents mentioned everything from marine life to terrestrial plants, showing curiosity for future outreach.
The survey results provide clear guidance for the 2025 Guam SWAP. Community members, especially youth, want to protect native and culturally significant species, address invasive threats like the brown tree snake and coconut rhinoceros beetle, and restore habitats. There is strong support for environmental education and better enforcement of conservation laws.
The survey results are a solid foundation for shaping conservation efforts that reflect the priorities, knowledge, and values of Guam's people. The Department engages the public in conservation and education efforts of the Plan through a variety of programming, outreach events/workshops, and community groups.
Youth Programs • Upon teacher or school request, presentations are conducted at schools with the goal of educating and encouraging students on how to respect and conserve island resources. Over 100 presentations are given each year on topics such as endangered/threatened species, invasive species, Guam's watersheds, and marine/reef ecosystems. Additionally, the Department donates vegetables to schools that make requests for classroom and/or school-wide gardens.
• Guam's youth have the ability to learn about freshwater species, habitats, and sustainable fishing practices through programs such as the annual Kid's Fishing Derby, which began in 1990, and the annual Masso' Reservoir Youth Fishing Derby, which was started in 2013.
Outreach Events • At annual community events, such as Earth Day and Charter Day, interactive educational booths are set up by the Department. During annual Arbor Day festivities, the Division of Forestry engages students and community members in the planting and care of native trees. Additional tree planting events are held in the Cotal Conservation Area, Geus-Manell, Pago Bay and Masso watershed areas.
• The Division of Forestry coordinates with landowners to provide native species and information/support for landowners wishing to replant with indigenous trees.
• The Division of Forestry partners with DAWR and other organizations to continue the promotion of the Munnga ma songge Guahan ("Don't Burn Guam") campaign with a goal of addressing wildfires during the island's dry season. This campaign continues to create opportunities for students and community members, specifically residents in the south, to be involved and informed on Guam's restoration and conservation efforts.
• An annual Pig Hunting Derby was started in 2012 by the Department to educate and instruct the community on safe hunting practices while encouraging assistance in ungulate control, specifically the feral pigs that have a low harvest interest. The first derby has only 10 hunters with 30 harvests. Since then, the two-day event has grown to include over 100 hunters with an average of 60 pigs harvested.
• The weekend following the Derby, a free public event called Pork in the Park is held to encourage awareness on how to safely prepare and enjoy feral pork. Culinary teams compete for various prizes while the public is afforded the opportunity to taste the feral pork. The Department pairs with a local hotel for additional food preparation. The Department also has locally partnered with the USDA-WS, Water and Environmental Research Institute, school conservation groups, Farm-To- As was the case in 2015, the critical status of the island's native ecosystems and the importance of protecting and recovery of native species are acknowledged as the foundation of the SWAP. Identifying and protecting suitable habitats in Government of Guam owned lands and intact native communities is recognized as a high priority and a major focus for conservation efforts. On an ecological level, the 2025 Guam SWAP takes a habitat management approach and landscape view that accounts for the complex relationships among species and their habitats, and the significant need for change and adaptability. This revision also incorporated the Guam and CNMI collaboration for landscape conservation across the Mariana Islands. The comprehensive approach in 2025 was to build on information from the 2015 SWAP by identifying cooperative efforts and implementing new information from the past ten years.
Much of the 2015 SWAP remained relevant and was the foundation on which to continue conservation efforts detailed in this revision. The overall structure of the document was retained as much as possible. The content of the 2015 SWAP was reviewed, evaluated, and updated by DAWR staff, the University of Guam Center for Island Sustainability and Sea Grant Island Conservation Lab, and various species experts who may use the plan for funding or collaborative efforts for specific projects. Special attention was paid to knowledge gaps, new threats and challenges, and changes in conservation strategies and management approaches. There was also a focus on climate change effects and the need to strengthen legal frameworks set in place for conservation efforts.
Emphasis for the next 10 years will focus on improving efficiency in collecting ongoing data and research, public outreach approaches identifying new species and habitat threats, and establishing action priorities for addressing outlined conservation needs in the SWAP. The 2025 Guam SWAP will be fully reviewed by 2035 with interim performance reports (reviews) every year of projects funded by State Wildlife Grants. The interim performance reports will be used as a tool for DAWR's technical experts to evaluate the success or failure of management objectives, adjust management actions accordingly, and review project proposals (need, objectives, and approach) addressed in the SWAP. The revision process will take place through the following actions listed below:
• The proposed management actions for Guam's SGCN will be updated as new information is gathered from these reviews. The Department will coordinate, with technical experts, a complete assessment of the strategy on a needed basis. The findings will be compiled and shared using all available media for public review and comments that will be incorporated in the future revision of the plan. • The Department and its partners will use the original eight elements and other criteria available at the time of revision. DAWR will consult with public and private stakeholders to clearly describe the revision process. • A review of the priority levels will be made to assure SGCN needs (conservation actions) are being addressed. The Nature Conservancy threat assessment tool will be utilized to aid in the determination of the priority levels for SGCN and conservation actions in the plan. Rankings will be established and clearly defined, being determined at the species or group level (i.e. hard corals).
• The Department will publish research papers with data collected during SGCN conservation activities to support the selection of future priority species. These publications will also serve to educate the public about historical, current, and trending species needs to gain more informed community feedback for future actions and research areas.
• The Department will seek to hire a SWAP coordinator to engage conservation partners (local agencies, federal agencies, stakeholders and non-government groups) and seek their participation at the beginning of the revision of the SWAP. The coordinator will be tasked to facilitate coordination between these partners to summarize goal and objective outcomes and compile new information on species, habitats, threats and actions for SGCN. • By the next review of the SWAP, DAWR will also focus on creating partnerships with various cultural groups to improve upon information regarding culturally valued species, with special considerations for the sensitivities surrounding certain cultural practices. Their input and participation will be key to updating information on culturally valued SGCN and gaining input for the potential inclusion or removal of other species based on traditional knowledge. • The next revision shall start at least two years before the tenth year and be completed by the end of the tenth year. The countdown to revision shall commence on October 1, 2025, and the full revision shall be completed and submitted for review and approval by October 1, 2035.

[FIGURE: Fish and Wildlife  Service, 2005). Regular, more consistent, and concerted survey and monitoring efforts did not begin in earnest until 2014. Guam's fanihi population remained small and fluctuating, ranging from about 15 individuals in 2014 to around 82 in 2020 (Guam Department of Aquatic and Wildlife Resources, 2020). Currently there are less than 200 bats throughout Guam with a few known roost colony sites. While fanihi are found in several areas across the island, including southern mountains and mixed forests, they are primarily concentrated in the north, where annual monitoring efforts are focused (Vice pers. comm.).]

[FIGURE: Photo by Kalin Ocaña]

[FIGURE: The species has highly adapted to urban environments and has been found to successfully nest and reproduce in building and other man-made structures. Outside of AAFB, small populations have been observed in northern and central Guam with 30-40 individuals near Yigo and Dededo, 20-30 individuals in Hagåtna, 10-20 individuals in Tamuning-Tumon-Harmon, and up to 10 individuals in other areas. In addition, an isolated resident population of approximately 30-40 individuals lives on Islan Dåno' (Cocos Island).]

[FIGURE: Muniappan collected three specimens on a limestone plant, Procris pedunculata, at Hila'an Point, on August 1975, one specimen from the AAFB on April 1982 and two specimens at Hila'an Point in September 2001 (Guam Agricultural Experiment Station Collection). Adults, eggs, and/or larvae have been documented on host plants at Ritidian, AAFB Northwest Field cliffline, AAFB cliffline, Pagat, NCTS (Pugua Point, Haputo ERA), Hila'an, Asiga, Tanguisson Beach, and Guam racetrack (C. Fiedler, pers. comm.). H. o. marianensis was listed as endangered under the U.S. Endangered Species Act on October 1, 2015. The Mariana eight-spot butterfly's status is in decline (USFWS, 2015c). Currently, the butterfly has been recorded from 6 to 19 locations (USFWS, 2021).]

[FIGURE: documented a few trees growing in southern Guam on volcanic soils. This species was federally listed as endangered onFebruary 18, 1987 (52 CFR  4907-4910). The only adult tree known in Guam was severely damaged from Typhoon Mawar in 2023 and died soon after. There are over 40 adult trees on Rota. In Guam, only outplanted juvenile trees remain with some that have flowered but none have fruited. Efforts to outplant håyun lågu are ongoing with GNWR staff and other partners with about 50 seedlings and about 200 on DoD lands in Guam. A genetic study by Demeulenaere revealed that the Guam and Rota populations possess unique genotypes. One of the recovery goals is to preserve genetic variation within each population. A second goal is to experimentally establish gene flow between the two genotypes to enhance overall genetic diversity. This research is being conducted by the University of Guam's Center for Island Sustainability and the Sea Grant Island Conservation Lab.]

[FIGURE: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972) and listed in Appendix II of CITES. Most sighted species in Guam with group sizes of up to 135 individuals. Surveys conducted from 2010 to 2013 have catalogued 307 individuals encountered across the Mariana Islands archipelago, including Guam (Hill et al., 2020).THREATS: Incidental catch in gill-net fishery and shrimp trawls; purse-seine netting in the eastern tropical Pacific; captured in small numbers in directed fishery in Solomon Islands; entanglement in drift-nets and in lost or discarded netting; pollution (including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea) leading to bio-accumulation of toxic substances in body tissues. Potential exposure to US Navy detonation sites and proximity to tourism activities.]

[FIGURE: Protected under the Marine Mammal Protection Act (1972) and listed in Appendix II of CITES. Periodic sightings have occurred but no current population estimations for Guam or the Marianas. THREATS: Incidental catch; direct and indirect catch in Sri Lanka and the Solomon Islands; large numbers taken by the Japanese drive fishery; entanglement in driftnets and in lost or discarded netting; pollution (including increasing amounts of plastic debris at sea, oil spills and dumping of industrial wastes into waterways and the sea) leading to bioaccumulation of toxic substances in body tissues. HABITAT(S): Pelagic. Deep water and outer edge of the continental slope. Tropical, subtropical and warm temperate waters. Possible vagrants recorded in colder waters. Encounters were primarily 54-342 km offshore with depths greater than 2,300 m.]

[FIGURE: . It is listed both federally and locally as endangered. Estimated average abundance found approximately 22 nesting females from 2008 to 2010(Seminoff et al., 2015) and 140 to 300 individuals from 2008 to 2012(Van Houtan et al., 2016). Between 2013 to 2019, there were around 230 green sea turtles recorded in Guam's waters(Gaos et al., 2020). Sightings are recorded throughout the year, but groups of individuals are often seen in Manell Channel and Cocos Lagoon, particularly during December to February and May to June (NMFS-USFWS, 1998a). Nesting occurs year-round but may vary by season. Currently identified important nesting sites in Guam include Islan Dåno' and the Northwestern coastline from Falcona Beach to Tarague Basin, along with Dadi Beach and Spanish Steps in Naval Base Guam.]

[FIGURE: HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Popular food fish. Juveniles recruit in large schools and form a culturally important fishery. Juveniles are taken with tekin (gill net), and talaya (throw net), while adults are more frequently taken with hook and line or spear.THREATS:Overfishing, loss of habitat HABITAT(S): Juveniles are found in reef flats and bays, usually over sandy substrate.]

[FIGURE: Wrasses are a popular food fish in Guam. They are frequently taken on hook and line, with tekin (gill nets), and with spear. Wrasses are carnivores, feeding on reef invertebrates and smaller fishes.THREATS: Overfishing, loss of habitat HABITAT(S): Wrasses are found in almost all coral reef habitats, from seagrass beds to sandy reef flats, to seaward reef slopes and deep reefs (> 500 feet). In some habitats, wrasses are among the most abundant species found.]

[FIGURE: STATUS: Historically, giant clam populations were abundant throughout the Marianas and across Micronesia and the Indo-Pacific. Significant declines in populations were caused by decades of intense overharvesting for food, aquarium trade, and use in carving for traditional adornment. By the late 20 th century, wild populations were severely reduced or locally extirpated. THREATS: Overfishing, loss of habitat HABITAT(S): Reef flats and seaward reefs to a depth of at least 60 feet. GOAL: Achieve long-term population stability to meet recovery thresholds under CITES and Endangered Species Act.]

[FIGURE: Guam Long-term Coral Reef Monitoring ProgramThe Guam Long-term Coral Reef Monitoring Program (GLTMP) is a jurisdictional coral reef monitoring program operated by the University of Guam Marine Laboratory and funded by the NOAA Coral Reef Conservation Program and the Guam Coral Reef Initiative. The program involves the regular, intensive collection of data for a suite of coral reef ecosystem health indicators at priority reef areas around Guam, as well as critical support for coral bleaching response and other activities carried out by the multi-partner Guam Coral Reef Response Team.]

[FIGURE: University of Guam Reef Flat Monitoring Program • The University of Guam's Reef Flat Monitoring Program (RFMP), led by Dr. Laurie Raymundo and supported by the NOAA CRCP, Guam CRI, and GLTMP, collects coral size and condition data on a semi-annual basis at five shallow reef flat sites on the centralwestern coast of Guam. Two of the RFMP sites are located within locally managed preserves, including one in the Tumon Bay Marine Preserve and one in the Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve. Sites at West Hagåtña Bay, Tanguisson Pt., and Luminao Reef are also monitored. The results of the most recent analysis of RFMP data are presented in the "Decade of change on Guam's coral reefs" report available on the guamcoralreefs.info website.]

* Government of Guam has recognized the need to protect and preserve the natural resources of the island. In 1956, the Department, in cooperation with other government agencies, was given the authority to control and manage land and water areas set aside as conservation areas.In 1982, PL 16-62  reestablished the authority of the Department over Conservation Reserves having joint jurisdiction with the Department of Parks and Recreation. The development of a Master Plan for Park and Conservation Land (DOA and DPR 1999) was completed and followed the criteria that these lands had no claims, ownership, or interest attached to it. Approximately 20% of Guam has been designated as local or federal conservation lands. The Government of Guam has identified four areas for conservation: Anao, Bolanos, Masso, and Cotal Conservation areas. An additional 20 areas, totaling approximately 4.40 km2 or 1087 acres (Draft Master Plan for Park and Conservation Land*

[TABLE:  . The Rosy Wolf Snail (Euglandina rosea) and Gonaxis spp. were purposely introduced in the late 1950s to control Achatina. It's likely Euglandina itself caused a lot of decline in native snail species. The flatworm came in later (1977-1984), and seems to, at least, have wiped out Euglandina and Gonaxis and is still doing a number on all terrestrial snail fauna in Guam. Freshwater snails Pila conica, Pomacea canaliculata, Sinotaia magniciana, Lymnaea, Physa, and Planorbis spp. compete directly for food and habitat with native genera Clithon, Neritina, Neritodryas, Septaria, Tarebia, Melanoides, Stenomelania, and Thiara.]

[TABLE: • The Guardians of the Reef Program: This program trains high school students to teach elementary school students about coral reefs. Along with the help of marine biology or biology science teachers, HS students learn how to lead multiple educational activities, including stories and songs. The program was started by Dr. Romina King during her time as a coral reef management fellow, and it has run for over 10 years. The program has produced alumni that pursued marine science related careers into the future. • GCRI virtual field trips: Virtual field trips are guided interactive adventures with clickable elements and feature all local photos and videos of Guam's environment. GCRI currently has virtual field trips for southern Guam watershed, seagrasses at Inalåhan Pools, mangroves at Sasa Bay Marine Preserve, and corals at Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve. The virtual field trip project was first launched during the pandemic with efforts from 2020-2022 coral reef management fellow and Simon Sanchez marine biology teacher (Ms. Mel Blas) to help students explore Guam's environment when unable to go in person. It continues to provide a way for students to experience environments when bussing is limited, liability issues prevent in-water activities. Students can experience Guam's wildlife and learn about ecosystem services without costly VR technology or requiring the ability to swim. The virtual field trips are adaptable for use at outreach booths or in classrooms. Accompanying scavenger hunts allows teachers to use them as assignments, Virtual field trips have been shared with teachers at various local educator symposiums and at other public tabling events.]

## Maps

The tree fern has always been considered rare and is found in southern Guam 
(Stone, 1970)
 in ravines and savanna habitats where they are especially vulnerable to wildfires and habitat degradation because of the introduction of invasive species. THREATS: Presently, major threats include typhoons and wildland fires reducing the habitat in which this species lives. Habitat degradation is due to invasive plant species outcompeting native plants.
HABITAT: Southern hills of Guam, wet ravines and muddy drainage slopes.

## GOAL:

To restore tree fern populations.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey the island and determine the density and distribution of the fern. To protect remaining areas harboring the tree fern. To propagate this species and out plant in conservation areas in southern Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Small or extirpated population: Identify locations of species and collaborate with the University of Guam or people who are knowledgeable of fern propagation. Protect existing populations from wildland fires. Conduct studies into the ecology and biology of this species. Begin propagating this species for outplanting.

## CHacha

Cyathea lunalata

## Tree fern

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Historically widely distributed in Guam and Rota.
In 1992, this species was reported to occur in large mat-like formations on trees all over the island of Guam 
(Raulerson & Rinehart, 1992)
. In 2020, there were approximately 420 individuals in Guam from 5 populations. As of 2024, there were an estimated 10,000 individuals in Guam (USFWS, 2024c)
THREATS: Major threats include habitat loss, destruction from agriculture, urban development, nonnative animals and plants, typhoons, wildland fires, and predation by nonnative slugs.
HABITAT: Grow on trees mostly in limestone and ravine habitats.

## GOAL:

To restore B. guamense populations and minimize threats to the species.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey the island and determine the density and distribution of the orchid.
To protect remaining areas harboring the orchid. To propagate this species and outplant in conservation areas in southern Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveys and inventory: Continue surveys in historical locations and potentially suitable habitats that have yet to be surveyed. Regularly monitor. • Small or extirpated population: Identify locations of species and collaborate with the University of Guam or people who are knowledgeable of orchid propagation. Protect existing populations from wildland fires and other threats. Conduct studies into the ecology and biology of this species. Propagating this species for outplanting.

## Bulbophyllum guamense

Photo by Lauren Gutierrez

## Tinospora homosepala

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: A climbing vine, T. homosepala was historically known to be endemic to Guam and very rare 
(Stone, 1970)
. As of 2024, there are an estimated 150 wild individuals 
(Federal Register, 2014)
.

## THREATS:

Major threats include habitat loss, nonnative animals and plants, typhoons and genetic limitations because of the possible loss of potential sexual reproduction.
HABITAT: Native forests GOAL: Restore T. homosepala populations and minimize threats to the species.

## OBJECTIVES:

To locate species habitat and determine the density and distribution of T. homosepala. To protect remaining areas harboring the species. To propagate this species and outplant in conservation areas in Guam.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Surveys and monitoring: Survey historical range of T. homosepala. Survey all sites with suitable habitat. Determine suitable locations for reintroductions. Monitor extant populations for reproduction to determine sex ratio. • Ungulate monitoring and control: Use ungulate exclusion fences and continue eradication efforts to protect habitats. • Invasive nonnative plant monitoring and control: Continue control of invasive plant species. • Captive propagation: Continue and maintain collection of individuals and propagation efforts for genetic stock maintenance and reintroduction. • Research: Conduct genetic studies to determine genetic variation within and between populations to plan an effective breeding program.

## Atot

Stiphodon sp.

## Stream Goby

Photo by DAWR HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Only known from the southern Mariana islands. The most abundant fish in almost all freshwater habitats in Guam and has a marine larval stage. Must reach salt water during early life history.
THREATS: Introduced fish and plants, degradation of water quality.
HABITAT: Generally found on hard substrates, most common in medium to fast flowing water. Herbivorous, mainly feeds on algae attached to rocks and clay.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population status.

## OBJECTIVES:

To control introduced predators, maintain water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To maintain information on its status in Guam.
• Protect habitat: control sedimentation to protect hard substrates. Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed.

## Atot

Sicyopus sp.

## Redbellied Goby

Photo by DAWR

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

Only known from the southern Mariana islands.
Widespread, but relatively uncommon. This species has a marine larval stage and must reach salt water during early life history.

## THREATS:

Introduced species, degradation of water quality.
HABITAT: Generally found on hard substrates, most common in medium to fast flowing water. Omnivorous, feeds on small aquatic invertebrates as well as some algae.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population status.

## OBJECTIVES:

To control introduced predators, maintain water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species in Guam. • Protect habitat: Control sedimentation to protect hard substrates. Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow life history to be successfully completed.

## Atot

Stenogobius sp.

## Marianas Goby

Photo by DAWR HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Discovered in 1994. Widespread in Guam and relatively common. Omnivorous, feeding on plants and animals, it sifts from the sand. This species has a marine larval stage and must reach salt water during early life history. As very few endemics seem to fare well, its placement in SOGCN is warranted.
THREATS: Loss of habitat, degradation of water quality.
HABITAT: Lower reaches of rivers on soft sandy or muddy substrates.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population status.

## OBJECTIVES:

To control loss of habitat, maintain water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species in Guam.
• Protect habitat: To control loss of riparian vegetation and to limit loss of substrate.
Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow life history to be successfully completed.

## Umatan

## Kuhlia rupestris

Flagtail HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Indigenous to the western and southern Pacific.
Found in freshwater lentic habitats, it has a marine larval stage and must reach the salt water during its early life history.

## THREATS:

Introduced fish and diseases.
HABITAT: Rivers and streams across the western and southern Pacific.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population.

## OBJECTIVES:

To protect from habitat loss and introduced competitors and diseases.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species in Guam. • Protect habitat: Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed. • Invasive species control: removal of freshwater invasive species that may have a negative impact to umatan island-wide.

## Asuli

Anguilla marmorata

## Giant Marbled Eel

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Widespread throughout the Indo-Pacific. They are common food species for many Pacific indigenous peoples. This species migrates to the ocean to spawn. The young eel then returns to freshwater after an extended larval stage.

## THREATS:

Overfishing and habitat modification.
HABITAT: Tropical rivers and streams. Spawns in the western Pacific.

## GOAL: Maintain current population.

## OBJECTIVES:

To protect from overfishing, maintain habitat to allow passage to spawning grounds.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Protect habitat: Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed. • Harvest regulation: Investigate the take of the species to help develop a management plan to maintain sustainable fisheries.

## Uhang

Macrobrachium spp.

## Tahitian prawns (2 species)

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Common to abundant in rivers in Guam, especially in upper reaches. Omnivores, feeding on detritus, plant and animal material. All species have a marine larval stage. Commonly trapped for food by local anglers.
THREATS: Introduced predators, loss of habitat, overfishing, and destructive fishing methods.
HABITAT: Practically all freshwater habitats in Guam.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population.

## OBJECTIVES:

Maintain substrate and water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To maintain current information on the status of this species in Guam. • Protect habitat: Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed.

## Varunid crabs

## Varunidae

Freshwater crabs (> 3 species)
HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Common and widespread in Guam, especially in the upper reaches of rivers. Most found in fast moving waters on rocky or hard substrates. All species have a marine larval stage. Must reach salt water during early life history. These crabs feed on algae and other benthic material.
THREATS: Introduced predators, loss of water quality.
HABITAT: Rocky and hard substrates in rivers throughout the Pacific.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population.
OBJECTIVES: Maintain substrate and water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species in Guam. • Protect habitat: Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed.

## Akaleha'

Neritidae Nerite snails (12 species)
HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Nerite snails are common and widespread in Guam, especially in the upper reaches of rivers. This snail has a marine larval stage. Must reach salt water during early life history. Feeds on algae and other benthic material.
THREATS: Introduced predators.
HABITAT: Rocky and hard substrates in rivers throughout the Pacific.

## GOAL:

To maintain current population.

## OBJECTIVES:

To maintain substrate and water quality.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the current size and distribution of the 12 species of snails. • Protect habitat: Ensure water quality is maintained and passage to the ocean is maintained to allow for spawning to be successfully completed.

## Akaleha'

Thiaridae Thiarid snails (5 species)
HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Some of the species of this snail are common to abundant in rivers in Guam, especially lower reaches. However, some species are very limited in distribution and abundance. The snails give birth to live young with no marine stage. The snails feed principally on detritus and organic debris.
THREATS: Introduced predators, loss of habitat.
HABITAT: Soft substrates in rivers and lakes.

## GOAL:

To maintain current populations.

## OBJECTIVES:

To determine the distribution and abundance of the various species of snails.
To maintain substrate and water quality of the river and lake environments.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To survey the various rivers and lakes in Guam to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of snails. • Protect habitat: To protect the aquatic environment from land-based pollution by the implementation of programs that minimize siltation of rivers and streams.

## Guafak uhong/ Umok sensonyan

Ceratopteris thalictroides subsp.
gaudichaudii Water Fern

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The native water fern is common in Fena Lake. Elsewhere, it is widespread, but nowhere common in Guam. This water may be an endemic species.
THREATS: Introduced predators and loss of habitat.

## HABITAT(S):

Slow moving or stagnant water, ponds, lakes.
GOAL: Identify species and maintain current population.

## OBJECTIVES:

To protect from habitat loss and from predators. To determine the taxonomic status of this species.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species. Protect habitat: Ensure habitat is kept free of invasive plants and animals.

## Chaiguan gaddok

## Potamogeton mariannensis

Pond Weed HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Chaiguan gaddok is endemic to Guam 
(Costion & Lorence, 2012)
. This species of pondweed is widespread, but nowhere is common.
THREATS: Introduced plants and animals, loss of substrate HABITAT(S): Usually found in slow moving stretches of rivers with soft sandy or muddy substrate.

## GOAL:

To preserve suitable habitat for this species.

## OBJECTIVES:

Remove and/or control potential threats

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species. Protect habitat: Remove introduced predators, control loss of substrate.

## Nipa

## Nypa fruticans

Mangrove palm or Nipa palm
Photo by Caley Jay Chargualaf HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Nipa is a palm species that was introduced from the Philippines 
(Safford, 1905)
 and naturalized. The species is typically found in estuaries, especially along Guam's eastern coast 
(Fosberg, 1960)
. Nipa has been found in 13 of the island's rivers and the Department has also outplanted palms along Masso River. The species is culturally important as it is used as thatch for roofs 
(Safford, 1905)
 but individuals must first obtain a permit from the Department's Forestry Division to collect it.
THREATS: Introduced plants and animals. Overharvesting.

## HABITAT(S):

Found in river mouths, where they are exposed to estuarine conditions.

## GOAL:

To preserve suitable habitat for this species. Prevent overharvesting.

## OBJECTIVES:

Remove and/or control potential threats.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To determine the status of this species.
• Protect habitat: Remove introduced species. 

## Dugong dugon

## GOAL:

To determine the importance of Guam's waters to the conservation of marine mammals.

## OBJECTIVES:

To survey Guam's waters annually to determine the abundance and distribution of the various species of marine mammals and make appropriate management recommendations.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Conduct quarterly aerial surveys around Guam's waters to determine the abundance and distribution of the marine mammal species in Guam's waters and seek technical assistance from federal partners to gain information about these species.

## Atuhong

Bolbometopon muricatum

## Bumphead Parrotfish

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: Stocks have steadily declined in Guam. Sightings are exceptionally rare.
THREATS: This species feeds substantially on live coral, requires healthy reef habitat for survival, and is threatened by the loss of this habitat. Due to its habit of sleeping in schools, it is also vulnerable to commercial spearfishing.

## HABITAT(S):

Clear outer lagoon and seaward reefs at depths of 1 to at least 30m. In unfished areas may enter outer reef flats at low tide 
(Myers, 1999)
. Its life cycle is dependent on the availability of and connections between particular habitats.

## GOAL:

To recover the population to historic levels.

## OBJECTIVES:

Assess current population structure and size, protect habitat, and manage take through outreach and education.

## ACTION PLAN:

• Assess current population structure and size: To collect baseline population size, demography (age and sex), genetic, and distribution information for Guam's stocks. • Protect habitat: Provide for adequate protection of habitat in ACOE and Guam Seashore Clearance permit conditions. • Reduce take through outreach and education: To teach fishers conservation by educating them not to catch fish below reproductive size. To develop educational materials highlighting slow growth rate. To include bumphead parrotfish in fisheries management plans.

## Balåti Actinopyga echinites

Deepwater red fish

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The deepwater red fish (Actinopyga echinites), also called the brown fish has been historically abundant in Guam 
(Kerr et al. 2017)
. It is also one of the most commercially valuable species 
(Purcell et al. 2012)
 and may be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as vulnerable to extinction globally.

## THREATS: Overfishing

## HABITAT(S): Sandy reef flats

GOAL: Sustain population and to prevent listing on Endangered Species List.

## OBJECTIVES:

To manage and increase population to allow sustainable fishing ACTION PLAN: Reduce take through change in regulations.
Photo credit: 
https://www.inaturalist.org/observations/106308693
 with permission © Marisa Agarwal CC BY-NC 4.0

## Balåti

Holothuria fuscogilva

## Brown teatfish

HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS: The brown teatfish (Holothuria fuscogilva), sometimes also called the white teatfish, has never been abundant in Guam 
(Kerr et al. 2017
). However, it is one of the most commercially valuable species 
(Purcell et al. 2012)
 and may be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as vulnerable to extinction globally.

## THREATS: Overfishing

## HABITAT(S):

The deep outer reef slope, usually below 20 m depth on sandy bottoms.
GOAL: Sustain population and to prevent listing on Endangered Species List.

## OBJECTIVES:

To manage and increase population to allow sustainable fishing.

## ACTION PLAN:

Reduce take through change in regulations.
Photo credit: 
https://www.inaturalist.org/observations/193921046
 with permission © Claire Goiran CC BY-NC 4.0

## Balåti

## Thelenota ananas

Pineapple sea cucumber

## HISTORIC AND CURRENT STATUS:

The pineapple sea cucumber (Thelenota ananas), sometimes also called the spiked sea cucumber, has been fairly abundant in Guam 
(Kerr et al. 2017
). However, it is also one of the most commercially valuable species 
(Purcell et al. 2012
) and appears to be disappearing from Guam's waters as a result, just as it is elsewhere in its range. In the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, it is currently listed as endangered.

## THREATS: Overfishing

## HABITAT(S):

The shallow outer reef slope, usually above 20 m depth on sandy bottoms.
GOAL: Sustain population and to prevent listing on Endangered Species List. Marine Surveys

## OBJECTIVES:

There are a number of methods for assessing fish stocks in marine waters. Guam DAWR uses a variety of these methods to assess the impacts of fishing and effectiveness of the marine preserves as a fisheries management tool. These methods include creel surveys and in situ monitoring.
Goal: Management of Guam's Marine Fisheries Resources, Offshore Fisheries Participation, Effort, and Harvest Surveys
• Monitor the health of Guam's reef, bottom, and pelagic fishery resources by conducting 192 offshore surveys each year at the three largest boat launch facilities on island.
• Continue gathering limited biological data that will add to a long-term historical data base in Guam's fish species by conducting 192 offshore surveys over a one-year period at the three largest boat launch facilities on island.
Inshore Fisheries Participation, Effort, and Harvest Surveys
• Monitor the health of Guam's reef and bottom fishery resources by conducting 192 inshore surveys each year along the coastline of Guam.
• Continue gathering limited biological data that will add to a long-term historical database in Guam's fish species by conducting 192 offshore surveys over a one-year period along the coastline of Guam.
• Monitor the health of Guam's reef and bottom fishery resources by conducting 24 aerial surveys each year along the coastline of Guam.
Visual Stock Assessment Surveys of Marine Preserves and Control Sites • Evaluate the effect on sport fish populations caused by the creation of five marine preserves where fishing is restricted or prohibited by conducting fish counts and timedswim counts on at least 25 permanent transects located in reef flat and lagoon habitats in Achang Reef Flat Marine Preserve, Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve, Tumon Bay Preserve, Asan Bay, Pago Bay, and Cocos Lagoon over a one-year period.
• Evaluate the effect on sport fish populations caused by the creation of five marine preserves where fishing is restricted or prohibited by conducting fish counts, timedswim counts, and video-transects/quadrant on at least 25 permanent transects located at the 20', 30', 40', and 50' depth contours of the fore reef slopes in Achang Reef Flat Marine Preserve, Piti Bomb Holes Marine Preserve, Tumon Bay Preserve, Asan Bay, and the backside of Cocos Lagoon, over a one-year period.
Marine Habitat Monitoring and Evaluation Since healthy fish populations require healthy reef habitat, DAWR and its partners also monitor the coral reef habitat through several projects to determine the impact of landbased sources of pollution and other threats to healthy coral reef habitats.
National Wildlife Refuge (GNWR), USDA-Wildlife Services, the Department of the Navy 
(DON)
, USGS-BRD, and the University of Guam (UOG). In addition, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), graduate students, and researchers will be invited to participate. The Department will initiate this process by sending invitation letters to request participation and involvement. This group will be responsible for reviewing the progress of on-going projects by evaluating methodologies and data collected to determine whether conservation actions are effectively addressing key limiting factors. Based on their assessments, the group will help develop adaptive management recommendations to improve project outcomes. By establishing this monitoring group, new information will be shared amongst technical experts. The group will also evaluate the effectiveness of conservation strategies in addressing priority actions for species and habitats using adaptive management principles and the result chain approach. At the beginning of the revision process, the Department and UOG CIS and SG ICL identified potential stakeholders from various federal, state, and local agencies. The UOG CIS ICL team sent emails requesting stakeholder participation and an initial meeting was held to present the approach to the SWAP revision including candidate species to be added, how the team would go about collecting updated data and information, and a general timeline to meet the deadline of October 1, 2025. Throughout this process, stakeholder agencies were invited to provide concerns, input, and assistance.
Various partners assisted in the revision of the SWAP, while the Department and UOG CIS teams facilitated the comprehensive review of the plan. These partners were involved through review and editorial contributions. They contributed reviewed, commented on, and edited portions of the 2015 SWAP that were relevant to their field of expertise or provided input through personal communication (Table 
12
). Once feedback was received, the suggested changes were incorporated into an overall working document for the 2025 SWAP.

## Mariana Landscape-scale Conservation

While the Guam SWAP and CNMI SWAP exist independently, the plans share many species of greatest conservation need. Species found in the Mariana archipelago have distribution ranges across the islands, not accounting for political or geographic borders. The respective Guam and CNMI SWAP teams recognized the importance of establishing landscape-scale conservation priorities across the Mariana Islands.
Collaboration with the CNMI Division of Fish and Wildlife (DFW) began in September 2024 when representatives from Guam DOAG-DAWR, UOG CIS and Sea Grant, and CNMI DFW began meeting to discuss the need to produce a Marianas landscape-scale conservation framework. Building on these efforts, representatives from CNMI DFW, Guam DOAG-DAWR, and UOG CIS and Sea Grant held a two-day interisland workshop in April 2025. In this workshop, the teams set out to identify opportunities for collaboration toward conservation actions for shared priority SGCN. The group set out to specifically identify (1) regionally important species, (2) priority areas for conservation work, and (3) relevant regional companion plans. The results of the workshop are detailed in Table 
13
. Marianas Avifauna Conservation Plan (does not include Guam) Additional needs identified Cross-jurisdiction support Gap analysis of the species that reside in both Guam and the CNMI but that were identified as SGCN by one plan but not the other Identification and support of projects with multispecies benefits Establishment of standard protocol/procedures and data needs for shared SGCN Research on impacts of non-native species on native SGCN (biosecurity) Funding to link monitoring with restoration projects Identification of established conservation actions in both SWAPs that cover landscape-scale needs discussed in the interisland workshop

## Public Participation

The approach to gaining community input for the SWAP involved presenting at conferences, tabling at various outreach events, and sharing an online survey to garner responses concerning species needing conservation. UOG CIS ICL and DOAG-DAWR hosted a public forum event early in the revision process. Despite a low turnout at the public forum, there were many other opportunities to garner public engagement with the SWAP. The teams made their presence through presentations and tabling at several events such as the Conference on Island Sustainability, Marianas Terrestrial Conservation Conference, Feral Swine Perimeter Fence Program workshop, DOAG Loves GU event, and the Spring Farmers Market event. At each of these events, attendees were able to view several outreach materials, ask questions about the plan, and fill out an online survey to provide feedback on species needing conservation. Through tabling and presenting, the DOAG-DAWR teams were able to interact with a total of over 500+ event participants.

## Survey Results

In Spring 2025, the UOG CIS and Sea Grant ICL, in collaboration with the Guam DAWR, launched a public Google Survey to gather community input for the 2025 State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP). The goal was to ensure the plan reflects the environmental concerns and priorities of Guam's residents.
After a public forum in January, the survey link was shared widely across the island through WhatsApp chats, Instagram, outreach events, classrooms, and at the 2025 Center for Island Sustainability Conference. QR codes were displayed at three different conferences and reposted by local organizations. In total, 312 people participated in the survey. 

## Next Steps

The Department will look into improving approaches in gaining community feedback for the SWAP revision. A key aspect will be revitalizing the Department's website to increase accessibility for the public to view the SWAP and provide comments throughout the plan's term. The Department is also committed to continuing their presence in outreach events and conferences to promote public involvement in the SWAP. Stories of sightings and killings of 9 whales in one season were recorded in the southern Mariana Islands. Two whales were reported about 100 m off the reef margin at Uruno Point on February 25, 1978. Three were sighted off the west coast of Guam on February 13, 1991. A group of three was photographed off Saipan in February 1991. A mother and calf were sighted off the east coast of Rota in late February 1991. A group of six or more was photographed at the entrance to Apra Harbor in January 1996.

Eads, pers. comm. Eads, 1991
Darling & Mori, 1993 Stinson,
 Appendix B: List of potential Scleractinia corals of opportunity (COO):
Corals of opportunity (COO) are colonies or fragments that have been detached from the reef by wave action, storms, ship grounding, etc. COO fragments may be damaged or in poor health, and depending on the species, may not thrive in a nursery setting (e.g., Heliopora coerulea) or be appropriate for nursery inclusion (e.g., Millepora platyphylla [fire coral]). Using COO to start a coral nursery is not recommended, but an established nursery can be augmented by COO. Using COO in an established nursery can expand the diversity of species in the nursery without causing additional stress to wild reefs and provide refuge to fragments that may otherwise die. Larger COO fragments or colonies may be better suited to a more permanent nursery structure, such as spiders or ropes. Nearly any coral that does not pose a risk to human health (e.g., fire coral) and is not mobile (e.g., Cycloseris) has the potential to be included in a nursery as a COO.
Potential target species to be included in the nursery were selected based on the factors listed below. This list may change as more data becomes available.
• Ease of collection: How hard will this species be to collect and include in the nursery? Can wild stock be collected by snorkeling from shore, or will it require a boat and/or SCUBA? Will specialized tools be required? • Availability: How easy will it be to locate this species? Is there a known population?
• Abundance: Is the source population very small? Are there nearby colonies of the same coral, or is this the only colony in the vicinity? Is this a coral that would benefit from inclusion in the nursery? Is this a coral whose population is very small and needs safeguarding? • Depth: At what depths is this coral species normally found? • Environment: Will this coral thrive in the nursery and planned outplant site? Or does it prefer a different environment (e.g., shallow reef flats, turbid forereefs, etc.)? • Growth rate: Will this coral grow quickly enough in the nursery to meet project goals?
• Growth type: Which nursery structure is best for this type of coral? Does the nursery structure have space for this species? Is this a species that will support our restoration goals? Is this growth type already well-represented within the nursery? • Site diversity: Can multiple coral species be collected at a site to increase efficiency?

## Appendix C: Table of potential Scleractinia species for inclusion in coral restoration

## Species Justification Acropora granulosa

Delicate, fast-growing species, good habitat for fish and inverts. Very uncommon.

## Acropora humilis

Fast growing, sturdy species. Uncommon.

## Acropora latistella

Fast growing, abundant in some places, likely to do well in the nursery and outplanted into shallow areas.

## Acropora surculosa/hyacinthus

Fast growing, sturdy species, difficult to find.

## Acropora valida

Fast growing species, fairly common.

## Acropora abrotanoides

Hardy. Alveopora sp.
Reported to grow quickly but is difficult to find.

## Asterospicularia randalli

Only one known location on Guam--this species would benefit from a genetic reserve.

## Diploastrea heliopora

Hardy, iconic species. Likely to grow slowly.

## Echinopora pacificus

Rugose, relatively fast growing according to aquaria forums. Euphyllia cristata E. cristata is a better choice because it has more compact corallites than E. glabrascens Galaxea fascicularis
Common, but seems to grow quickly and has done well in nurseries from other studies.

## Galaxea horrescens

Uncommon species, likely to grow quickly and similar to G. fascicularis.

## Gardineroseris planulata

Hardy, but slow growth rate. Does not appear to have been in a nursery before. Good species to compare to wild growth rate.

## Goniastrea pectinata

Hardy, easy to source.

## Goniastrea stelligera

Hardy, easy to source.

## Goniopora fruticosa

Relatively fast growing, according to aquaria forums. Easy to find.

## Isopora palifera

Abundant at Jinapsan beach, sturdy reef flat species.

## Leptoria phrygia

Common, hardy, easy to source, may not grow quickly, good candidate for spider nurseries.

## Lobophyllia hemprichii

Would take as a coral of opportunity but not a target species due to size of corallites and slow growth. Iconic species, hardy. Would be a good candidate for spider nurseries.

## Lobophyllia corymbosa

Would take as a coral of opportunity but not a target species due to size of corallites and slow growth. Iconic species, hardy. Would be a good candidate for spider nurseries.

## Montipora verrucosa

Fast-growing.

## Pachyseris speciosa

Uncommon, foliose growth form not represented by other species. Likely to grow quickly.

## Pavona decussata

Common reef flat species, likely to grow quickly Pavona duerdeni Similar to P. clavus in growth form, and much easier to find. Growth rates may be low though.

## Pavona explanulata

Encrusting coral, a growth form not represented.

## Platygyra daedalea

Iconic species, mounding Plerogyra sinuosa Chernin et al. found this near Nimitz Beach in 1977. Very rare.

## Pocillopora grandis

Uncommon but easily sourced at the mouth of Apra Harbor. Grows quickly and provides good habitat for fishes and invertebrates. Likes ropes.

## Pocillopora meandrina

Common, colorful, supports fish and inverts, likely to grow quickly.

## Pocillopora setchelli

Common, colorful, supports fish and inverts, likely to grow quickly.

## Porites deformis

Rugose species, but may grow slowly.

## Psammocora haimiana

Columnar species that resembles Pavona clavus in morphology. Common in Agat and Tumon Marine Preserve.

## Psammocora profundacella

Uncommon species, likely to grow quickly, very easy to find coralliths (i.e., circumrotatory mobile colonies) at Asan Beach (pending collection permit) and highly rugose.

## Stylophora pistillata

Uncommon species, but unusually abundant at Nathan's Dent. Likely to grow quickly. 

## Acropora tenuis

Hierarchical settlement behaviours of coral larvae to common coralline algae
